第四部分 · 专题深入 · 第 18 章

AI 对不同疾病领域的影响

AI 对不同疾病领域的影响并不均匀：肿瘤集中了最多的 AI 临床资产，其他领域的证据更少。本章提供一个比较框架，并逐一列出已查到的信号。

无对应原书章节（新增专题）
本章证据等级■■■■□□□□□□一手 41%（11/27）· 二手 59%中等一手 = 论文、监管文件与公司公告；公司公告是一手来源，但不是独立的效果证据。
趋势信号 · 截至 2026-11-18
  • AI 临床资产集中在肿瘤（约 57%–59%）与小分子（约 80%），管线偏窄。
  • 评估框架：数据可得性、生物复杂度、终点可测性，可用来判断下一个疾病领域。
  • 心血管与眼科以器械侧证据为主，疫苗与细胞和基因治疗的证据不足。
易过期的数字（日期、融资、估值）见附录 F；各版之间判断的变化见附录 E。以上是本书对本章内容的提炼与判断。
一句话结论。在已有数据里，AI 临床资产高度集中于肿瘤和小分子：ASCO 2026 的分析显示，117 个 AI 赋能资产中 59% 为肿瘤、82% 为小分子；同时，AI 来源的分子在整个临床管线中只占约 1%–2%。肿瘤领先既可能因为 AI 更适合，也可能因为肿瘤本身就是管线增长最快的领域，两者目前无法区分1,2,3。2026 年 11 月更新：首个有 AI 参与的获批药物出现在感染性疾病（中国，有条件批准），首批进入 III 期的 AI 药物在呼吸与过敏领域，而不在肿瘤4,5,6。
证据口径。“AI 资产”的统计口径不一（见附录 C），按疾病领域拆分的权威数据很少；除肿瘤的 59% 外，本章多为个案与公司公告。第 18 章的框架与解释都属本书判断，不是实证结论。

一、全貌：三个数字

59%
ASCO 2026 分析中 117 个 AI 赋能临床资产里属于肿瘤的比例1
82%
同一分析中小分子占比，其余为抗体等蛋白类药物1
1%–2%
AI 来源分子在整个临床管线中的占比（以 AI 原生生物技术公司作为代理）2

需要同时看两个背景。其一，肿瘤是过去二十年全球临床管线中增长最快的治疗领域3，所以 AI 资产集中于肿瘤，部分只是反映管线本身的构成。其二，Oliver Wyman 的分析称，早期临床试验中 AI 赋能药物的成功率显著高于历史水平，但证据仍有限2，与第 7 章“Phase I 好、Phase II 与历史持平”的判断一致。

另一项 2026 年 10 月在线发表的同行评审分析（129 个 AI 赋能资产、69 家公司，数据截至 2026 年 3 月 1 日）给出相近的分布：肿瘤 74 个（57.3%），小分子 103 个（79.8%），新靶点资产 48 个（37.2%）7,8；这与 ASCO 摘要的 59% 与 82% 口径接近，可互相印证，但两者样本不同。

二、比较框架（本书判断）

维度问题对 AI 的含义
数据可得性该领域有多少高质量的组学、影像与临床数据？数据越多越结构化，模型越容易训练与验证
生物学复杂度靶点是否明确？疾病机制是否清楚？AI 目前擅长产出候选分子，但在预测靶点是否在患者中成立上尚无优势（第 1、7 章）
终点可测性临床终点是否客观、可快速测量？终点越客观、越快，AI 辅助的试验设计与评估越容易被验证

三、逐个领域：已查到的信号

领域已查到的信号证据层级框架下的解释（判断）
肿瘤AI 资产占比最高（59%）1；BMS、AbbVie 的合作覆盖肿瘤9,10；AstraZeneca 收购 Modella 聚焦肿瘤临床开发与生物标志物11；Insilico 的 ISM6331 获快速通道资格，Schrödinger 的 SGR-1505 在淋巴瘤中报告 22% 的客观缓解率（二手）12中：数量最多，但多数仍在早期影像与病理数据丰富、终点（如缓解率）相对客观，管线本身也最大
免疫与炎症zasocitinib（银屑病）已在 FDA 优先审评，目标日期 2027 年第一季度13；Merck–Mayo 合作涵盖炎症性肠病与特应性皮炎14；AbbVie–Iambic 覆盖免疫10；Absci 的 ABS-101 为用于炎症性肠病的 AI 设计生物药（二手）12中：有接近上市的资产，但其设计方法偏物理模拟靶点机制相对清楚，且有可测的皮肤、肠道终点
呼吸与纤维化、过敏rentosertib（特发性肺纤维化）：靶点与分子均来自 AI 平台，III 期于 2026 年 7 月宣布启动、9 月首例给药15,5；Generate 的 GB-0895（重度哮喘）是首个进入 III 期的 AI 设计抗体，两项 III 期计划约 1,600 人6中：两个进入 III 期的个案，但都没有 III 期数据目标明确的疾病、读数客观（肺功能 FVC、哮喘急性发作率），但都需要 52 周的试验来验证
神经科学Merck–Mayo 涵盖多发性硬化14；BMS 与 AbbVie 的合作都包含神经科学9,10；Ono 与 Congruence 的合作扩展到神经学与免疫学，Congruence 自有管线含 GBA 相关帕金森病16低：以合作公告为主，未查到临床数据机制复杂、终点慢且主观性较强，数据获取难（判断）
代谢与肥胖Lilly 用减重与糖尿病产品的现金流扩大 AI 投入17；Lilly 与 NVIDIA 的联合实验室提出用机器人与物理 AI 扩大高需求药品的产能18。未查到 AI 来源的肥胖药物进入临床的证据；据报道 Superluminal（GPCR 靶点，6,000 万美元 B 轮）预计其 AI 发现的肥胖候选药物在 2026 年进入临床，尚未见试验登记19低：AI 更多出现在生产与商业端，而非分子发现核心产品多为已知靶点的多肽，瓶颈在产能与商业而非发现（判断）
罕见病Recursion 的 REC-4881（家族性腺瘤性息肉病）I b/II 期：12 周后 75% 的可评估患者息肉负担减小，中位减少 43%20；Anthropic 于 2026 年 10 月披露收购 Mithril Medicine（两种罕见肌营养不良）并称该项目仍为临床前21低到中：有早期临床信号，但样本小、多为公司披露患者少、数据稀缺，AI 的潜在价值大但验证难（判断）
感染性疾病Mprosevir（抗新冠）于 2026 年 7 月获中国 NMPA 有条件批准（西湖大学 9 月初公布），开发方称为首个“AI 辅助”的一类创新药；据中文报道，AI 用于确定 DEL 筛选条件与对照化合物并形成数据闭环4,22；另有分析称全球药企对感染性疾病的关注度较新冠高峰后回落3中：已有获批个案，但为有条件批准，且 AI 的作用是辅助而非提出靶点靶点已知（病毒蛋白酶）、筛选可高通量，AI 在“缩小候选”上最容易显效（判断）
心血管器械侧证据最多：FDA 授权的 AI 器械中，影像相关的心血管类有 130 款（占 9.6%），仅次于放射学23；一项随机试验中，AI 加心电图筛查使低射血分数的确诊率从 1.6% 升到 2.1%24。药物侧：未查到 AI 来源心血管药物进入临床的可靠证据器械侧：中；药物侧：证据不足器械数据多、结构化好；药物侧终点慢（心血管事件）、试验大且长（判断）
眼科糖尿病视网膜病变的自主 AI 筛查是已被广泛引用的案例（IDx-DR，现称 LumineticsCore，可由基层人员操作，2018 年清除）；2026 年另有青光眼筛查等的清除报道25中：器械侧成熟，但本稿的来源为二手博客，细节未核对图像数据丰富、诊断标准明确，适合自动化筛查（判断）；药物侧未查到证据
疫苗未查到可靠的临床阶段证据；一份行业综述称，mRNA 疫苗的风险投资在美国资助削减后大幅下降（2023 年约 5.1 亿美元，2025 年至当时约 9,000 万美元）26证据不足免疫原性预测难、周期受流行病驱动，资金环境先于 AI 的影响（判断）
细胞与基因治疗未查到 AI 来源疗法进入临床的可靠证据；Takeda 在 2025 年调整管线，退出细胞与基因治疗开发，Astellas 则仍把再生医学与罕见病细胞与基因治疗作为重点27证据不足模态复杂、病人少、数据稀缺（判断）

四、怎么用这个框架做优先级（判断）

  • 先选“数据足、终点客观”的领域做规模化验证：肿瘤与免疫炎症目前最具备条件。
  • 对“机制复杂、终点慢”的领域保持审慎：神经科学的公告多、临床证据少，应把合作里程碑与临床读数挂钩。
  • 区分“发现”与“其他环节”的价值：肥胖等领域，AI 的短期价值可能更多在生产、供应与商业。
  • 把“管线结构”当作混杂因素：判断 AI 的领域优势时，先剔除该领域本身的管线规模效应。

五、风险与开放问题

  • 统计口径：“AI 资产”的定义不统一，按领域拆分的数字可信度有限。
  • 幸存者偏差：公开的多是成功或进展中的案例，失败的项目与领域较少被报道。
  • 领域之间的外溢：在一个领域验证的平台，能否迁移到另一个领域，目前缺少证据。
  • 覆盖缺口：心血管、眼科、疫苗与细胞和基因治疗已在上表补充，但证据多为二手或不足；皮肤科、消化、肾脏、血液病等领域仍未覆盖。

第四部分 · 专题深入 · 第 19 章

客户侧的变化

患者、医生与支付方都已大规模使用 AI，这改变了药企触达客户、传递证据与处理安全信息的前提。

对应原书第 3 章 Pharmaceutical and Biotechnology Customers
本章证据等级■■□□□□□□□□一手 15%（2/13）· 二手 85%偏弱一手 = 论文、监管文件与公司公告；公司公告是一手来源，但不是独立的效果证据。
趋势信号 · 截至 2026-11-18
  • 医生使用 AI 的比例快速上升：AMA 调查显示 81%，2023 年为 38%。
  • 患者端大规模使用 AI 获取健康信息（KFF：约 32% 的成年人；OpenAI 的周问询量来自二手）。
  • 信息入口在变，药企要考虑自己的内容如何被 AI 引用。
易过期的数字（日期、融资、估值）见附录 F；各版之间判断的变化见附录 E。以上是本书对本章内容的提炼与判断。
一句话结论。药企的“客户”已经先于药企用上了 AI：据 AMA 2026 年调查，81% 的美国医生在执业中使用 AI；据 KFF 调查，约三分之一的美国成年人已就健康信息或建议求助于 AI；支付方一侧，CMS 的 WISeR 已在原始 Medicare 中试点 AI 辅助事先授权。药企面对的是一个信息获取方式、决策流程都在变化的客户群1,2,3。
证据口径。本章数据以美国为主；医生数据来自 AMA 的自填式调查（约 1,700 位医生），患者数据来自 KFF、Pew 与 Gallup 等调查，其中部分经二手汇总转述。其他国家的情况未覆盖。

一、三类客户的现状

81%
美国医生在执业中使用 AI 的比例，2023 年为 38%（AMA 2026）1
32%
美国成年人在过去一年就健康信息或建议求助 AI（KFF，2026 年 2–3 月调查）2,4
230M
OpenAI 在 2026 年 1 月称，全球每周有超过 2.3 亿人在 ChatGPT 上询问健康问题；2026 年 7 月称超过 3 亿5,6
8%
美国医生中表示“大多数患者会告知自己使用 AI”的比例；30% 认为多数患者可能在用6

二、患者与消费者

  • 工具丰富：2026 年 1 月 OpenAI 推出 ChatGPT Health，可连接病历与健康应用，并推出面向医疗机构的企业方案7,5；KFF 4 月的梳理还列出 Amazon Health AI（1 月）、Microsoft Copilot Health（3 月）、Perplexity Health（3 月）以及 Claude 面向付费用户的个人健康集成8。
  • 使用方式：KFF 调查中，32% 的成年人求助 AI 获取健康信息，其中 29% 为身体健康、16% 为心理健康；这些使用者中有 41% 上传过个人医疗信息4,8。据报道，58% 的身体健康类使用者之后会看医生，说明许多患者带着 AI 的答案走进诊室2。
  • 担忧与安全：77% 的成年人担心输入 AI 工具的医疗信息的隐私4；West Health–Gallup 对 5,500 多位美国成年人的调查中，约十分之一在过去 30 天内使用 AI 的人称收到过自己认为不安全的建议5。

三、医生与其他处方者

  • 用法：AMA 2026 年调查（约 1,700 位医生）显示，医生平均使用 AI 完成 2.3 项任务（2023 年为 1.1 项），最常见的是总结医学研究与临床文书1,9。
  • 态度：超过四分之三的医生认为 AI 提高了照护患者的能力（2023 年为 65%）；40% 持“既兴奋又担忧”的平衡态度，最担心患者隐私与医患关系的完整性1。
  • 对患者使用 AI 的看法：70% 的医生认为，患者适当地用通用 AI 获取日常健康信息是有益或无影响的；多数不愿看到患者用 AI 处理需要临床判断的事，近半数强烈反对患者用 AI 解读放射或病理结果10,9。
  • KOL（关键意见领袖）的行为：Veeva 对 75 位 KOL 的调查显示，他们用 AI 整合文献、核对诊疗标准、了解适应症与用例，并有人预计对 MSL 互动的需求会下降（见第 9 章）11。

四、支付方

支付方已在用 AI 做事先授权与利用管理：CMS 的 WISeR 自 2026 年 1 月起在六个州试点；UnitedHealth 据报道称其 AI 事先授权技术的首次通过批准率为 96%（公司自述）；同时有批评与诉讼提醒，缺少护栏的自动化可能以群体平均值替代个体判断。详见第 13 章3,12,13。

五、对药企意味着什么（本书判断）

客户变化对药企的含义
患者先问 AI，再看医生；会上传病历患者教育与说明书信息需要能被 AI 正确引用；患者支持项目要预期“带着 AI 答案来”的咨询
医生用 AI 总结研究与标准医学信息与 MSL 的价值要从“传递信息”转向“解释与语境”（第 9 章）；内容需要可被检索与引用（第 11 章）
支付方AI 辅助事先授权，答复时限缩短准入与患者支持团队要适配电子事先授权与更短时限；证据卷宗要面向“被 AI 初筛”的流程（第 13 章）
安全与合规AI 对话中可能出现不良事件信息需要明确 AI 渠道中安全信息的识别、捕获与上报流程（判断，需与药物警戒确认）

六、风险与开放问题

  • 准确性与责任：患者与医生都在依赖通用 AI，药企对其输出的影响有限，但仍需关注与自家产品相关的错误信息。
  • 隐私：患者向 AI 上传医疗信息的比例不低，而多数人担心隐私4。
  • 数字鸿沟：以美国数据为主的结论，未必适用于其他市场与人群。
  • 互动变化的度量：KOL 与医生需求下降只是调查中的预期，需要更多证据。

第五部分 · 展望 · 第 20 章

监管、数据、伦理与风险

监管往往决定 AI 在制药行业能走多远：本章汇总各监管文件的最新状态，并梳理数据、验证、生物安全、知识产权与责任归属。

无对应原书章节（新增）
本章证据等级■■□□□□□□□□一手 19%（6/32）· 二手 81%偏弱一手 = 论文、监管文件与公司公告；公司公告是一手来源，但不是独立的效果证据。
趋势信号 · 截至 2026-11-18
  • 监管仍以草案与原则为主：FDA 2025 年 1 月草案至今仍是草案，FDA 与 EMA 的十项原则不是正式指导。
  • 欧盟 AI 法案的高风险时间表被推迟，但它与 GMP、器械法规并行叠加。
  • 发明人署名：美国只认自然人，AI 是工具（USPTO，2025 年 11 月）。
易过期的数字（日期、融资、估值）见附录 F；各版之间判断的变化见附录 E。以上是本书对本章内容的提炼与判断。
一句话结论。截至 2026 年 11 月，监管仍以“原则加草案”为主：FDA 关于药品与生物制品中 AI 的 2025 年 1 月草案指导仍是草案，与 EMA 的 10 条联合原则不具约束力；欧盟的 GMP Annex 22 仍是草案，AI 法案的高风险义务已推迟到 2027–2028 年。这并不意味着监管缺位：FDA 自 2016 年以来已审阅 500 多份含 AI 成分的 CDER 申报，药企实际上已在按这些草案的思路准备证据1,2,3,4,5。
证据口径与本稿的一处更正。此前版本把“FDA 草案指导是否已定稿”列为未能确认。本章补查：FDA 自己的页面仍把 2025 年 1 月文件列为草案，且多份二手来源在 2026 年 9–10 月仍称其为草案，因此本稿判断其尚未定稿；最终状态仍应以 FDA 官网为准。其余内容多来自律所与行业机构的解读。

一、监管文件一览（截至 2026 年 11 月）

监管者文件状态范围与要点
FDA（CDER、CBER 等）《支持药品与生物制品监管决策的人工智能使用考量》草案（2025 年 1 月，卷宗 FDA-2024-D-4689）仍为草案；意见期 2025 年 4 月 7 日结束，截至 2026 年 9–10 月的来源仍称其为草案6,1,7七步、基于风险的可信度评估，按使用场景；不涵盖仅用于药物发现或不影响患者安全与数据可靠性的运营用途8,9
FDA 与 EMA 联合《药物开发中良好 AI 实践指导原则》（2026 年 1 月 14 日）已发布；不具约束力2,10共 10 条，覆盖全生命周期（含生产）；以人为中心、基于风险、遵循标准、明确使用场景等2,7
EMA关于 AI 在药品全生命周期使用的反思文件已定稿（2024 年 9 月）；明确涵盖药物发现9EMA 正在制定在其基础上的正式指南1
欧盟 / PIC/SEU GMP Annex 22（人工智能）及 Annex 11、Chapter 4 修订草案；意见期 2025 年 7 月 7 日至 10 月 7 日；截至 2026 年 8 月的报告仍无最终文本11,3关键用途只允许静态、确定性模型，把生成式 AI 与大语言模型排除在外；需合格人员与专人负责12
欧盟 AI 法案“数字综合方案”修订2026 年 7 月 27 日生效；附件 III 高风险义务推迟到 2027 年 12 月 2 日，嵌入受监管产品的推迟到 2028 年 8 月 2 日；通用目的 AI 义务自 2025 年 8 月 2 日起适用13,4,14,15禁止性实践、GPAI 与第 50 条透明度义务未被推迟（见第 16 章）16
FDA（CDRH）器械AI/ML 器械的 PCCP 指导（定稿，2024 年 12 月）；AI 器械软件功能生命周期草案（2025 年 1 月）PCCP 已定稿；生命周期草案截至 2026 年 7 月仍为草案17,18见第 5、17 章
USPTOAI 辅助发明的修订版发明人署名指导（2025 年 11 月 28 日）已生效；全面废止 2024 年 2 月的指导19,20只有自然人可为发明人；AI 被视为工具；Pannu 因素仅用于多名自然人之间的共同发明20
英国 PMCPA药品促销领域对 AI 的立场AI 可用于支持合规，但不免除公司依据 ABPI 守则的责任；指定签署人的最终认证不能仅靠 AI21见第 11 章

二、五个共同问题

问题现状药企要做什么（判断）
数据治理与归属JPM 2026 上数据归属成为中心议题：谁拥有临床与真实世界数据，如何训练专有模型，合作终止后哪些权利存续22；患者端 77% 担心隐私23把数据权利、模型与权重归属、终止后权利写进合同；按用途分级数据；见第 10 章的尽调清单
验证与可解释性FDA 要求按使用场景证明可信度而非“验证模型”本身；欧盟 Annex 22 草案对关键用途只接受静态、确定性模型24,12建立 AI 清单与使用场景登记；关键用途优先选择可解释、可冻结的模型；保留审计追踪
生物安全与两用风险OpenAI 的 GPT-Rosalind 以受信访问发放，权重不公开；报道称研究者警告，用生物数据训练的模型可能被滥用于设计危险病原体，OpenAI 通过访问模式应对25,26,27；Anthropic 于 2026 年 9 月 17 日以测试版推出 Life Sciences Verification Program，向经核实的生命科学团队提供对生物研究更宽松的安全措施版本28评估使用专用模型的访问资格与管控；对涉及病原体、毒素等的用途建立内部审批
知识产权美国 USPTO 把 AI 视为工具，发明人必须是自然人；业界评论指出，在药物发现中，谁“构思”了化合物，取决于所用模型类型与协作方式，现有指导对此考虑不足20,29在项目早期记录人的构思贡献；合同中约定 AI 平台方与药企之间的发明归属；预期发明人署名将被更多诉讼挑战
责任与公平英国 PMCPA 强调责任不转移21；支付方 AI 的拒付争议提示，缺少护栏的自动化可能以群体平均值替代个体判断30为每个 AI 用途指定对结果负责的人；在合同中划分企业与供应商的责任；对自动化决策保留申诉与人工复核

三、运营模型：一个最小可行的 AI 治理框架（判断）

要素做法对应的外部要求
AI 清单登记所有 AI 模型：用途、数据来源、关键性、责任人Annex 22 准备的前提31
使用场景与可信度计划为每个用途界定使用场景与风险，制定可信度评估计划FDA 草案的七步框架8
人工监督与问责关键决策保留人工复核；指定合格人员与专人Annex 22 草案；PMCPA 的立场12,21
供应商与合同数据权利、模型归属、变更通知、审计权、终止后处理JPM 2026 的数据议题；USPTO 的发明人规则22,20
董事会与风险把数据、数字与 AI 列为董事会固定议题，纳入企业风险体系Novartis 的做法32

四、风险与开放问题

  • 草案何时定稿：FDA 草案、Annex 22 的最终文本与对生成式 AI 的限制是否保留，仍不确定。
  • 多辖区冲突：FDA 的“按使用场景证明可信度”与欧盟的“关键用途限定模型类型”在思路上不同，跨国企业需要同时满足24。
  • “推迟”不是“免除”：AI 法案高风险义务只是时钟后移，义务本身没有变化14。
  • 前沿模型的治理：专用大模型的访问控制、评估与滥用防范，仍主要由模型公司自行设定。

一句话结论。未来三到五年，AI 对制药的影响更可能呈现为“局部很快、关键处很慢”：文档与流程自动化、基础设施绑定会先规模化；而决定价值的两件事——AI 来源药物能否在 II 期之后成功，以及监管把 AI 放在哪一个边界——要到 2027–2030 年才会逐步见分晓。本章的判断都是对前 20 章证据的推断，不是预测。
怎么用这一章。每条判断都标注了主要依据所在章节、本书的置信度，以及“什么会改变这一判断”。置信度是主观分级（高、中、低），不是统计概率。若某条判断所依赖的证据在附录 C 里被标为薄弱，请相应下调。

一、一张总览：价值链上的 AI 落地

环节（章）落地程度（本书判断）证据质量最大瓶颈
发现（第 7 章）临床阶段已有资产；无“靶点与分子均由 AI 提供”的药物获批，但 Mprosevir 获中国有条件批准（AI 辅助）；rentosertib 与 GB-0895 已进入 III 期，zasocitinib 在 FDA 审评中：有同行评审与监管文件，但样本小II 期能否成功：靶点生物学
药物开发（第 8 章）文档自动化已规模化；设计端在窄场景获监管认可中偏低：多为供应商案例入组与质量的前瞻性证据
医学事务（第 9 章）内部用途先行，合规越严部署越少低：小样本调查验证与合规
业务拓展（第 10 章）两端并存：用 AI 做 BD，与为 AI 资产做 BD低：效果证据少，交易样本多缺少 AI 对交易结果影响的证据
营销（第 11 章）内容产量激增，审核成为瓶颈低：行业评论与厂商MLR 容量与监管责任
销售（第 12 章）嵌入 CRM 的智能体已发布低：厂商公告与案例独立效果证据
管理式市场（第 13 章）支付方先行，药企侧工具为主低：律所解读与供应商支付方 AI 的透明度
生产运营（第 14 章）多数仍在试点，价值集中在偏差与维护中偏低：自述为主；监管文件为一手Annex 22 的最终规则与数据基础
贸易与分销（第 15 章）需求感知与短缺预警已部署低：公司高管说法缺少销售终端信号
企业支持职能（第 16 章）最易量化，仍处早期低：目标与二手估计治理与合规时钟

二、十个判断

#判断主要依据置信度什么会改变这一判断
1AI 最扎实的证据仍在发现端“更快产出候选分子”（搜索的规模与速度）；是否改变了“靶点是否成立”这一步，II 期数字目前给不出答案（19.5% 到约 55%，口径不同）第 1、7 章高rentosertib 与 GB-0895 的 III 期读数（需数年）；zasocitinib 的审评结果；Mprosevir 的确证性数据1,2,3,4
2文档与流程自动化是落地最快、最先规模化的用途第 8、9、11、16 章高独立评估显示质量问题，或监管对 AI 起草文件提出更严格要求
3差异化正从“模型”转向“数据、算力绑定与嵌入受监管流程的能力”第 3、4 章中开源模型追平封闭模型，使数据与算力绑定的优势缩小
4药企路线将继续分化为自建、外采、并购与企业级部署四种，且多数同时采用第 4 章中出现明确的“赢家路线”，或领导层更替导致战略收缩（如 Sanofi 的案例）5
5前沿模型公司与咨询公司将在药企预算上直接竞争，咨询公司越来越像平台与渠道第 6 章中偏低药企大规模改回依赖系统集成商；咨询数字被证伪
6监管是各环节落地的节奏器：发现端最快，开发与制造受制于可信度要求第 14、20 章高FDA 草案定稿的内容、Annex 22 的最终文本6,7
7器械端的 AI 将以“平台数据加逐项清除的自主功能”推进，而非一步到位的自主手术第 5、17 章高出现获批的、能做外科决策的系统，或第 4 级系统进入临床试验
8客户侧（医生、患者、支付方）已大规模使用 AI，药企的触达、证据传播与安全处理方式需要调整第 9、13、19 章中高更多国家与更大样本的数据；医生对 AI 的使用出现回落
9AI 在疾病领域的分布不均，部分反映管线结构而非 AI 的内在优势第 18 章中偏低按疾病领域拆分的、口径统一的权威数据
10数据权利与发明人署名将成为合作与交易的核心条款第 2、10、20 章中形成可复用的行业合同模板，或监管与司法给出明确规则

2028 年前后（据披露）Generate 的 SOLAIRIA-1/2（GB-0895）完成17首个 AI 设计抗体的 III 期；时间以公司披露为准，本稿未核实

四、三个值得跟到底的案例，和一个“停下来”的案例

前面的章节告诉读者“谁在做什么”，但读者要做预算决定时，需要的是“同样条件下究竟省了什么、谁做决定、什么结果会让人叫停”。下表把三个已有公开信息的案例，按负责人的视角拆开：左边是已经公开的，右边是还看不见的，最后一列是本书的判断：什么结果应当让负责人停掉项目。公开信息大多是公司或供应商的说法，所以“看不见的部分”比“看得见的部分”更长。

案例原来怎么做、谁做决定已公开的结果看不见的部分什么结果应当让负责人停掉（本书判断）
一个发现项目：Insilico 的 rentosertib传统路径从靶点到临床前候选常需数年；决定权在 Insilico 与出资方；公司称约 18 个月完成从靶点发现到临床前候选18II a 期 71 人、12 周，+98.4 mL 对 −20.3 mL，置信区间大幅重叠19；III 期已启动，首例给药 2026 年 9 月1同条件下省了多少：停掉的项目有没有算进分母；同一平台上还有多少项目没有走到临床III 期的主要终点（52 周 FVC 年下降率）不达预期，或安全性信号变得不可忽视；而不是“发现很快”这一事实
一个临床报告项目：Novo Nordisk 的 NovoScribe过去由 40–50 人的团队用数周撰写（各渠道说 12、15 周或 10 周以上）；决定权在 Novo 的内容数字化与监管团队20,21供应商案例称起草时间缩短 90% 以上；Novo 员工称撰写时间缩短 90%，并称有时纠正 AI 的错误比重写更费时21,22从首稿计时到签字定稿的总时长（含返工与监管问询）；质量的独立评估；省下的人力去了哪里定稿总时长没有缩短，或监管问询与返工增加，或质量审计发现错误率高于人工基线
一个分销项目：Cencora 的需求感知批发商拿到的是订单，而不是患者在柜台的真实需求；决定权在 Cencora 的供应链与数据团队23,24其高管称预测约改善 10%；2026 年第二季度美国仍有 227 项持续的药品短缺，连续第三个季度上升24,23预测改善是否传导到“患者拿到药”：缺货的根因（原料、产能、定价）是否在 AI 可及之内；10% 的口径与基线预测更准但缺货没有减少，说明瓶颈不在预测；此时应把资金转向数据共享或产能，而不是继续加预测模型

一个“停下来”的案例

我没有找到任何一家药企公开解释“看完漂亮的试点数据后决定不续约”的案例，这本身是一个信号：公开的多是续约与扩大，很少有人公开解释停下来的理由。能找到的最接近的两个，一个来自开发方，一个来自合作的结束：

  • Recursion 在 2025 年 5 月终止 REC-994、REC-2282，并把 REC-3964 转向对外授权。REC-994 在脑海绵状血管畸形（CCM）已有 62 人的 II 期数据，早期曾有积极信号；公司称累积的数据“不足以证明继续投入”，从整体结果看应当结束开发。这是开发方在看完早期信号之后，按全部数据叫停的例子25,18。
  • Bayer 与 Exscientia 的合作在达成一个药物发现里程碑后经双方同意结束。该合作 2020 年 1 月签署，含 2.4 亿欧元（含前期付款与未披露的里程碑）；Exscientia 保留两个靶点之一的开发选择权；这是公司披露的“里程碑后结束”，而不是药企解释“为什么不续约”。所查摘要没有显示结束的年份26。

对读者的含义（本书判断）：写下“什么结果会让我停掉”，应当在试点开始之前，而不是之后。一个可操作的做法是在合同或立项文件里预先写明三项：分母（所有启动的项目，含已停掉的）、定稿时点（从首稿计时到最终签字）、叫停阈值。没有这三项，漂亮的试点数据就很难变成“继续还是停止”的决定。

五、行动建议（本书判断）

读者建议
药企的战略与研发负责人
  • 把 AI 项目按“数据足、终点客观”与“机制复杂、终点慢”分层，前者先做规模化验证（第 18 章）。
  • 对发现端的合作，把里程碑与临床读数挂钩，而不是与平台访问挂钩（第 7、10 章）。
  • 先在文档与流程类用途上形成可复制的治理模板，再扩展到影响患者安全的用途（第 8、14、20 章）。
  • 建立 AI 清单与使用场景登记，作为面对 FDA 与欧盟要求的共同底座（第 20 章）。
业务拓展与投资者
  • 区分“平台优先”与“管线优先”的 AI 公司，并为前者设计以数据权利为核心的尽调清单（第 10 章）。
  • 对公司声称的性能，要求前瞻性验证而非只看基准（第 3、7 章）。
  • 把 AI 来源资产的定义写进条款，避免口径之争（第 1、18 章）。
器械公司
  • 把 AI 功能拆成可单独申报、可变更控制的应用，建立在统一的数据平台之上（第 5、17 章）。
  • 从一开始就为手术与设备数据的权利、隐私与二次使用设计合同（第 17 章）。
  • 关注欧盟 AI 法案对嵌入产品的 2028 年时点与 FDA 对 AI 器械软件的生命周期要求（第 16、20 章）。
咨询公司与服务商
  • 从“卖建议”转向可验证的结果：按里程碑与可衡量产出定价（第 6 章）。
  • 在合同中把数据权利、模型归属与终止后处理讲清楚（第 6、10、20 章）。
  • 对自己的数字保持克制：区分“AI 收入”的口径，避免与同行直接相加比较（第 6 章）。
监管与合规团队
  • 把“推迟”当作准备窗口而非免除：用这段时间缩小差距（第 16、20 章）。
  • 为 AI 用途指定对结果负责的人，并保留审计追踪与人工复核（第 11、14、20 章）。
  • 对跨辖区要求建立对照表：FDA 按使用场景证明可信度，欧盟对关键用途限定模型类型（第 14、20 章）。

六、本书的局限与下一版

  • 证据偏二手：许多数字来自二手汇总与公司自述，已全部列入附录 C；对外使用前应优先核对该清单。
  • 覆盖有缺口：Google 与 DeepMind、Microsoft 的医药模型，以及 Recursion、Xaira、Generate、Chai 等公司的 2026 年动作，已在第 3、6、7 章补充，但只是概述；日本与中国的药企和器械厂商（第 4、5 章）以及心血管、眼科、疫苗、细胞与基因治疗（第 18 章）也已补充概述；仍未覆盖 Evo 系列等基因组模型的细节、Meta、Amazon 与 IBM 的医药模型、Terumo 等器械厂商、CRO 与 CDMO，以及皮肤科、消化、肾脏、血液病等领域。
  • 以美国与欧盟为主：中国、日本、印度等市场的监管与实践未覆盖。
  • 时点：数据截至 2026 年 11 月 18 日，本领域变化很快；每章末尾的来源链接可用于更新。
  • 披露：本稿由 Claude（Anthropic 开发）协助起草；涉及 Anthropic 与 Claude 的内容仅依据公开资料，并与其他厂商按同样标准处理，请自行核实。

本稿中证据较弱、口径不一或尚未覆盖的内容。进入下一轮写作前，应优先处理这些条目。

事项状态与说明
AI 临床项目数量口径不一（117、173 以上、200 以上）均来自二手汇总，需回到原始数据库核对口径
Phase I、Phase II 成功率（21/24；4/10）引自二手对原论文的转述，需核对 Drug Discovery Today 原文
“尚无靶点与分子均由 AI 提供的药物获批”据二手行业分析，写作前需再核对一次
IsoDDE 的基准数字来自公司技术报告及其二手转述，未经同行评审
Isomorphic 与 Novartis、Lilly 的合作规模，以及进入临床的时间据 Nature 报道的转述（BAAI），未核对原文
FDA 2025 年 1 月草案指导是否已定稿已补查：FDA 自己的页面与多份 2026 年 9–10 月的二手来源仍称其为草案，故判断尚未定稿；最终状态以 FDA 官网为准
LillyPod 的 1,016 块 GPU；Pfizer 的 1,200 块以上 GPU来自二手报道与二手索引，未见公司给出的一手数字
Roche“药企已公布的最大 GPU 规模”公司自述；其他药企未必公布同口径数字
GPT-Rosalind 的基准成绩厂商自述
Pfizer 与 Boltz 合作的细节宣布日期来自新闻稿；“用 Pfizer 历史数据训练专属模型”部分来自二手报道
Merck 与 Google Cloud“最高 10 亿美元”合作来自二手报道，未见 Merck 一手公告
十大药企的 AI 治理信号（首席 AI 官、董事会议题等）主要来自 Lead With AI 等二手索引，需回到各公司年报核对
器械公司：Abbott、Boston Scientific 的 AI 信息多来自二手追踪与分析，缺一手来源；Boston Scientific 条目标为“待补”
FDA AI 器械清单中的厂商授权数（GE 120、Siemens 89、Philips 50 等）来自 Imaging Wire 经转述，需核对 FDA 清单原表
咨询公司的 AI 收入、员工与投资数字多为二手汇总，口径不一（甚至对同一公司的收入存在不同说法），需回到年报
IQVIA“前二十大药企中 19 家使用其智能体”二手报道，未见 IQVIA 一手确认
会议日期来自主办方或二手日历，使用前须以主办方官网为准
JPM 2025 的 AI 资料比 2026 年少，两年对照不对称
zasocitinib 是否计入“AI 药物”口径问题，已在第 7 章说明；核心设计方法是物理模拟
本稿仍未覆盖的模型、公司、地区与领域Evo 系列等基因组模型的细节；Meta、Amazon、IBM 的医药模型；Terumo 等器械厂商；CRO、CDMO；Medline；皮肤科、消化、肾脏、血液病等疾病领域。（Google/DeepMind、Microsoft、Chai、Xaira、Generate、Recursion、中国模型厂商、日本与中国药企和器械厂商、心血管、眼科、疫苗、细胞与基因治疗已补查并概述，多为二手来源）
“尚无靶点与分子均由 AI 提供的药物获批”的 2026 年 11 月更新结论仍成立，但 2026 年 7 月中国 NMPA 有条件批准了开发方称为“AI 辅助”的 Mprosevir（靶点已知；据中文报道，AI 用于确定筛选条件与对照化合物并形成数据闭环）。批准日期 7 月 29 日只见于一家试剂供应商的帖子，西湖大学于 9 月初公布，未核对 NMPA 原文；该说法来自开发方与官方媒体，AI 参与程度没有独立评估
rentosertib III 期的时间此前写“2026 年 7 月启动”；补查后更正为“7 月宣布启动，9 月 9–10 日首例受试者给药”（Insilico 新闻稿与行业媒体）；日期为 9 月 9 日或 10 日，不同来源略有出入
“三到四年获批”的估计来自主要研究者的说法，经行业媒体转述；非公司公布的时间表，也不是监管承诺
Isomorphic 的融资与估值2026 年 5 月 21 亿美元、11 月 17 日 50 亿美元 C 轮来自公司公告与媒体；约 500 亿美元的估值只见于彭博报道的转述，公司未披露
Generate 的 III 期与融资数字来自财经媒体与二手综述；两项试验的人数（各约 786 人，合计约 1,600）与完成时间需核对 ClinicalTrials.gov；有来源称与 Novartis 的合作条款为前期 1.52 亿美元，与本书第 10 章引用的 2024 年条款（前期 6,500 万美元，含 1,500 万美元股权）不一致，需核对
Chai 的命中率、融资与合作清单命中率来自公司预印本（总体约 16%，52 个靶点中约一半有可用抗体）；4 亿美元 C 轮与 38 亿美元估值、与 Lilly、Pfizer、Novartis、argenx、BMS 的合作来自二手综述（FTLOScience）；Roche 合作来自公司新闻稿
AlphaGenome 2 的性能数字“因果变异几乎翻倍（30% 对 16%）”来自 DeepMind 博客与 bioRxiv 预印本，未经同行评审
Microsoft Discovery 与 GSK“GSK 正在探索”出自 Build 2026 的媒体报道，规模与范围未披露；BioEmu 的训练数据规模出自媒体转述
中国模型厂商的参数量与上市信息BioMap xTrimo V4 的 2,680 亿参数、60 多个概念验证项目、港交所上市申请出自 36Kr 与 iTiger 的二手报道；Pinal 出自一份通讯稿，原 Nature 论文未核对
价值链总览的评级价值潜力、落地成熟度与十个标签都是本书的定性判断，依据各章证据，未做财务建模或外部校验；同一环节内部差异很大；成熟度主要依据公告者标为“采用度未知”
第 6 章“咨询公司新角色”的六种角色基于公开的合作公告与咨询公司自我叙述的推断，没有独立的效果评估；应作为假设；独立性张力的例子（Deloitte–Anthropic、PwC 转售）出自公开报道
前沿模型公司的收购与成果OpenAI 收购 Orchestra Bio（Axios 独家，条款未披露）；Anthropic 收购 Coefficient Bio（约 4 亿美元股票）出自媒体报道，Anthropic 未确认；10 月 27 日的合作成果由合作方自报，多为临床前，Enveda “人工审核时间减少 89%” 为合作方数字。本稿由 Claude 协助起草，此处利益相关，请独立核实
NovoScribe 的“12 周到 10 分钟”“15 周到分钟”与“90% 以上”来自 AWS 与 MongoDB 的供应商案例，两份案例的基线周数不一致；有二手转述称 Novo 自己的口径更保守（约 70%）
Unlearn 的 EMA 资格认定与 FDA 积极反馈来自公司页面自述，需核对 EMA 与 FDA 的原始文件
医学事务的调查数据（79 份、75 位 KOL、MSL 协会调查）小样本、由供应商或行业组织开展，只能说明倾向
营销章的产量 +29%、使用率 77%、执法函 200+出自 pharmaphorum 对行业基准的转述，需回到原始基准与 FDA 公开记录核对
销售章的 Aktana 与 Veeva 效果数据均为厂商案例或公告，无独立评估；Salesforce 等其他 CRM 厂商未覆盖
业务拓展章的中国来源资产数字（40%，450 亿到 1,050 亿美元）出自 IQVIA 分析，口径为“不含 AI 合作”的治疗性引进交易，需核对
管理式市场章的 WISeR、CMS-0057-F 与药品事先授权规则主要来自律所与行业机构解读，需回到 CMS 原文；药品事先授权规则的最终状态未核实
UnitedHealth 的 AI 投入与 96% 首次通过批准率公司自述，经二手转述
EU GMP Annex 22 的最终文本与对生成式 AI 的限制截至 2026 年 8 月的二手报告仍为草案；需核对最新状态
FDA 首封援引“过度依赖 AI”的 cGMP 警告信（2026 年 4 月）仅有单一二手来源，需核对 FDA 原文
制造章的“偏差调查缩短 50% 以上”出自厂商高管访谈与咨询公司研究，属自述
分销章的“约 10% 预测改善”与短缺数字前者为公司高管说法；后者出自 ASHP 与犹他大学的统计，经行业媒体转述
企业支持职能章的节省金额、PwC Strategy& 的 2,540 亿美元估计、药物警戒效率数字均为目标、估计或二手转引，非审计结果
EU AI 法案数字综合方案的时间表来自多家律所与行业机构的解读，需核对官方公报文本
手术机器人的自主性分级与“谁在哪一级”出自行业梳理文章，不同来源对分级标签叫法不一；SRT-H 的第 4 级为作者自评
SRT-H 的结果离体胆囊、一个阶段、少量样本；不同二手来源对“17 步”与“夹闭与切断阶段”的描述不一，本稿采用 Science Robotics 论文及其解读
疾病领域章的“59% 肿瘤、82% 小分子”和“1%–2% 管线占比”前者出自 ASCO 2026 分析的二手转述，后者出自 Oliver Wyman 以 AI 原生公司为代理的估算，口径不同
客户侧章的患者与医生数据美国为主；KFF、Pew、Gallup 数据部分经二手汇总；AMA 调查为自填式，约 1,700 位医生
USPTO 发明人署名指导的发布日期多个来源给出 2025 年 11 月 26 日或 28 日（联邦公报日期为 11 月 28 日）；采用 11 月 28 日
第 21 章的“置信度”与“2029 年以后”均为本书的主观判断与推断，不是统计概率或公司公布的时间表
D

附录 D：一手来源核对记录

2026 年 11 月，对 24 项承重论断回到一手来源做了核对。状态分三类：已核对原文、部分核对（数字或日期仍依赖二手）、未核对。这张表也说明本书的证据底子：能回到原文的是监管文件与论文，效果与规模数字多数仍停留在公司与供应商的说法。

rentosertib II a 期：+98.4 mL 对 −20.3 mLNature Medicine 摘要（PubMed 记录）数字核实；95% 置信区间分别为 10.9 至 185.9 与 −116.1 至 75.6，大幅重叠；据二手综述主要目的为安全性已核对原文

附录 E：版本变化日志

这本书是一份快照，而前沿变化很快。这里记录每一版新增了什么、哪些判断被更正或推翻，读者可以据此看出趋势在往哪个方向走。

版本日期新增更正或推翻
v1–v62026-11框架、21 章逐章写成、构建流程、中英文切换；v6 修复了切换语言时的阅读位置早期版本未逐版记录细节
v72026-11-18补查此前标为“尚未调研”的部分；新增 Mprosevir 的有条件批准、Generate GB-0895 的 III 期与上市、Isomorphic 的 50 亿美元融资、Chai、AlphaGenome 2、Microsoft Discovery、NVIDIA Clara、Anthropic 与 OpenAI 的收购等更正 rentosertib III 期时间（7 月宣布，9 月 9–10 日首例给药）；“尚无 AI 药物获批”加注 Mprosevir
v82026-11-18价值链总览（36 个价值点、两个尺度、九个标签、一张图）；第 6 章咨询公司新角色；KOL、MSL、HCP、MLR 等术语标注—
v92026-11-18附录 D 一手来源核对；每章证据等级；第 6 章反面情景；小样本标注；日本与中国药企和器械厂商；心血管、眼科、疫苗、细胞与基因治疗更正 Mprosevir 的批准日期与 AI 作用；rentosertib II a 期加置信区间；WISeR 对所选州提供者强制且只覆盖器械与操作类服务；FDA 草案把“起草申报材料”列为范围外
v102026-11-18II 期成功率三个数的并列与解释；与三十年前相比 AI 改变了哪一步；第 21 章三个案例与一个“停下来”的案例销售“通话前准备与记录”成熟度 4 调为 3，新增“采用度未知”标签；zasocitinib 不再称为“离计算设计药物获批最近”
v112026-11-18每章开头的趋势信号；本变化日志（附录 E）；可更新数据表（附录 F）；会议日期移出第 2 章—（本版没有改动判断）

Preface: How to Read This Draft

This is the eleventh version of Understanding AI in Pharma: all 21 chapters are written; on 18 November 2026 the parts previously marked as not yet researched were checked along with the latest developments, and a Value-Chain Overview, an analysis of consultancies’ new role in Chapter 6 and term annotations were added.

The book keeps the value-chain skeleton of Understanding Pharma (John J. Campbell) and rewrites each functional chapter around what AI changes. Appendix A lays out the remaining chapters, and Appendix B maps them to the source book.

The draft covers fast-moving material from 2025–2026. Data is current to 18 November 2026. Readers and uses span self-study, internal sharing and external use, so the draft stays objective and endorses no company.

Confirmed Writing Decisions

ItemDecision
The three additions (Ch. 16, 19, 20)Keep
Audience and purposeSelf-study, internal sharing and external use together; objective statement throughout
Length and languageAs detailed as practical for each chapter; Chinese and English written in parallel
Scope of Ch. 4–6Leading drug and device makers, at least the top five and ideally the top ten; this version covers ten drug makers and ten device makers (including Intuitive)

Four Writing Rules

  • Every key fact carries a source number. Sources are marked primary (company announcements, papers, regulatory documents) or secondary (media and industry analysis), with links at the end of each chapter.
  • Fact and judgment are kept apart. Content tagged “Judgment” is inference from fact, not fact itself.
  • An announcement is not validation. Partnership and product announcements are written separately from independent evidence of effect, and labeled.
  • Uncertainty stays visible. Anything that could not be verified goes into Appendix C rather than being filled by guesswork.
Disclosure. This draft was prepared with the assistance of Claude, developed by Anthropic. Content about Anthropic and Claude (for example in Chapters 3, 4 and 6) relies only on public material and is held to the same standard as other vendors; please verify independently.

Update Notes (v7 and v8, 18 November 2026)

  • New in v11 (to make the frontier and trends easier to grasp): each chapter opens with 2–3 trend signals stating the direction readers should watch; new Appendix E, a version change log recording what each version added and which judgments were corrected; new Appendix F, updatable data tables gathering conference dates, funding and valuations, regulatory dates, deal terms, and scale and counts with as-of dates, sources, staleness risk and the trigger for the next check; Chapter 2’s conference dates moved to Appendix F. No judgment was changed in this version.
  • New in v10 (answering the second reviewer): Chapter 7 gains two sections: it sets the three Phase II numbers side by side (BCG about 40%, Haque et al. 2026 at 19.5%, Oliver Wyman 45%–55%) and explains the differences, noting that stopping after Phase II does not mean the target was wrong (per secondary retellings, four of six discontinued cases in BCG’s sample cited business reasons); it uses zanamivir (a 1993 Nature paper, approved 1999) to show that computer-assisted design is not new, correcting the too-broad line calling zasocitinib the nearest approval of a computationally designed drug. The Value-Chain Overview lowers the maturity of pre-call preparation and notes from 4 to 3 and adds an “adoption unknown” tag (the 190+ CRM customers are platform customers, not the number who enabled agents). Chapter 21 gains three cases worth following to the end (discovery, clinical report, distribution), a case of stopping, and three items to fix before a pilot.
  • New in v9: 24 load-bearing claims were checked against primary sources, with results in Appendix D (12 verified against the original text, 8 partly checked, 4 still unchecked); every chapter now opens with an “evidence grade” note showing the primary and secondary source shares; Chapter 6 gains a counter-scenario and “what observations would change the judgment”; small-sample figure cards now show sample sizes; Japanese and Chinese drug makers (Chapter 4) and device makers (Chapter 5), and cardiovascular, ophthalmology, vaccines and cell and gene therapy (Chapter 18) were added. The check found and corrected several points: Mprosevir’s approval date and the account of AI’s role, the wide confidence intervals of rentosertib’s Phase IIa, that WISeR is mandatory for providers in the six states and covers only device and procedure services, and that the FDA draft explicitly lists drafting submissions as out of scope.
  • New in v8: the “Value-Chain Overview” at the start of Part III (listing, link by link, where AI can create value, labeled with value potential, maturity and nine tags, with a chart of value against maturity); a new Chapter 6 section on consultancies’ new role when model firms contract directly with drug makers; and inline annotations for terms such as KOL, MSL, HCP and MLR in Chapter 9 and elsewhere.
  • The parts previously marked “not yet researched” were checked: in Chapter 1’s value-chain map, medical affairs, business development, marketing, sales and access, and trade and distribution now point to the findings in Chapters 9–15; the models and companies listed under Chapter 3’s “gaps” (Google/DeepMind, Microsoft, Chai, Xaira, Generate, Recursion, NVIDIA, Chinese players, OpenAI and Anthropic) were checked one by one and tabulated.
  • New “latest developments”: Chapter 1 gains a “November 2026 update,” Chapter 7 gains “new clinical and capital signals,” and Chapters 6, 10, 18, 20 and 21 were updated in step.
  • Two important corrections: first, rentosertib’s Phase III is corrected to “start announced in July, first patient dosed on 9–10 September”; second, the conclusion that no AI-originated drug is approved still holds, but must note China’s conditional approval of Mprosevir in July 2026 (called “AI-assisted” by its developers).
  • What is still not covered is listed in Appendix C and Chapter 21; the new figures are mostly company or media disclosures, with the basis marked item by item in Appendix C.

Part I · Landscape and Trends · Chapter 1

How AI Is Reshaping Pharma

This chapter lays an AI map over the source book’s value chain: where evidence exists, where there are only announcements, and which stage matters most.

Source: Ch. 1–2 (Introduction to the Industry; What Is a Pharmaceutical?)
Evidence grade of this chapter■■■■□□□□□□Primary 36% (9/25) · secondary 64%MediumPrimary = papers, regulatory documents and company announcements; a company announcement is a primary source but not independent evidence of effect.
Trend signals · as of 2026-11-18
  • Evidence sits in the scale and speed of search, not in whether the target holds: Phase I is consistently high, Phase II ranges from 19.5% to about 55%, unresolved.
  • Milestones are moving from entering the clinic to approval and Phase III readouts: China conditionally approved Mprosevir, which developers call AI-assisted, and zasocitinib is under FDA review.
  • Capital keeps concentrating in platforms while clinical readouts stay scarce, and the gap is widening.
Numbers that go stale (dates, funding, valuations) are in Appendix F; changes of judgment between versions are in Appendix E. The above distills and judges this chapter’s content.
In one sentence. As of November 2026, the firmest evidence for AI sits in discovery, where it produces candidate molecules faster; announcements cluster around document-heavy workflows; and the costliest hurdle, Phase II success, has not yet shown an AI advantage. By the industry analyses retrieved, no drug whose target and molecule both came from AI has been approved; but in July 2026 China’s NMPA conditionally approved Mprosevir, an anti-COVID drug its developers call AI-assisted (see the November 2026 update below).2,311,12

The Value-Chain Map

The source book breaks a drug company into a chain of linked functions. The table marks the main AI touchpoints at each stage and the public evidence found so far. As of this version (18 November 2026) every stage has been researched; evidence quality varies widely, as the “State of public evidence” column and each chapter’s evidence note show.

Stage (source ch.)Main AI touchpointsState of public evidenceThis book
Discovery (Ch. 4)Target identification, molecule and protein design, structure and affinity prediction, wet/dry lab loopsClinical-stage assets exist; Phase I success above history, Phase II in line with it; no AI-originated drug approved2,3Ch. 7
Drug Development (Ch. 5)Trial design, patient recruitment, regulatory documents and data managementRegulatory frameworks exist (FDA 2025 draft, FDA-EMA 2026 principles)1,9; effectiveness evidence is mostly vendor announcements6Ch. 8
Medical Affairs (Ch. 6)Literature synthesis, medical information responses, evidence communicationResearched: literature monitoring and plain-language summaries are the most deployed uses, with less deployment where compliance is stricter; evidence is mostly small surveysCh. 9
Business Development (Ch. 7)Asset sourcing, diligenceResearched: two directions coexist, AI for sourcing and diligence (little effect evidence) and valuing and structuring deals for AI platforms and assets (many samples)Ch. 10
Marketing, Sales, Managed Markets (Ch. 8–10)Segmentation, content and compliance review, payer analyticsResearched: content output surging and MLR review the bottleneck; sales-side agents embedded in CRM (vendor evidence); payer AI prior authorization piloted by CMSCh. 11–13
Manufacturing (Ch. 11)Deviation root-cause analysis, digital twins, roboticsMostly pilots; effect figures mostly claims; the EU GMP Annex 22 draft limits the model types allowed in critical uses4,5Ch. 14
Trade and Distribution (Ch. 12)Demand forecasting, inventory optimizationResearched: demand sensing and shortage warning deployed; wholesalers lack point-of-sale signals; effects mostly executives’ accountsCh. 15
Corporate functions (source appendix)Finance, HR, IT, legal and compliance, pharmacovigilanceResearched: easiest to quantify and still early; savings are mostly targets, and the EU AI Act’s high-risk obligations are deferred, not cancelledCh. 16

For the specific places in each stage where AI can create value, labeled as highest value, most mature, early, technically hard or most compliance-sensitive, see the “Value-Chain Overview” at the start of Part III.

What the Numbers Say

80–90%
Phase I success of AI-discovered molecules; historic range about 40–65%. Small sample (the count “21/24” comes from secondary retellings, the abstract gives only percentages), all authors from BCG2,24
19.5%–55%
Phase II success differs widely across analyses: about 40% for BCG (a tiny sample), 19.5% for Haque et al. 2026 (129 assets), 45%–55% for Oliver Wyman; definitions differ, so unresolved (see Chapter 7, Section 8)2,24
117
AI-enabled clinical assets in an ASCO 2026 analysis; only 8 had completed Phase II; 59% oncology, 82% small molecules. Figures retold secondhand; the original abstract was not checked2
0
Approved drugs whose target and molecule both came from AI (per industry analyses retrieved)2,3

A note on definitions. Counts of AI clinical programs range from 117 to over 200 depending on the definition (whether “AI-assisted” counts, whether only interventional trials count). The figures are not directly comparable, so this book states source and definition each time.

The two assets closest to a “first approval” differ in nature. Rentosertib (Insilico, idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis) announced the start of Phase III in July 2026, and the first patient was dosed on 9–10 September7,13. Zasocitinib (Takeda, psoriasis) has an FDA application under Priority Review with a target action date in Q1 20278. The latter was designed mainly with physics-based simulation, so whether it counts as an “AI drug” depends on definition; see Chapter 7.

November 2026 Update: What Is New

These are developments found after the draft was first written, ordered by their bearing on the book’s judgments. Unless noted, all are company or media disclosures; see each chapter and Appendix C for definitions.

  • The first “AI-assisted” drug approval (conditional, China). In July 2026 China’s NMPA conditionally approved Mprosevir (isterovir hydrochloride tablets, brand name Aipusovir in Chinese reports) for adults with mild to moderate COVID-19, developed by Westlake University, Westlake Laboratory and Westlake Pharmaceuticals; Westlake announced it in early September (a chemical-reagent supplier gives 29 July as the approval date, which this draft could not check against NMPA text). The drug came from a DNA-encoded library of about 49 billion compounds, from which more than 100 candidate molecules were first picked; per a Westlake professor quoted in Chinese reports, AI models helped set screening conditions and control compounds and enabled data integration in a closed loop of computational prediction, experimental validation, data feedback and model re-optimization; English-language media add that AI narrowed the candidates to nine, a claim not found in the Chinese first-hand relays. It took about three and a half years from candidate discovery to completing clinical trials11,12,22,23. Note: this is a conditional approval; the target (a SARS-CoV-2 protease) was already known; and AI’s role was assisting screening and the data loop, not proposing the target. It therefore does not overturn the conclusion that no approved drug has both target and molecule from AI, but it shows that approvals of drugs with AI involvement have begun; commentary notes the COVID target is far simpler than those of oncology and other complex diseases and calls it a technical validation rather than proof that AI can solve clinical failure.
  • Rentosertib enters Phase III. Insilico says the first patient in GENESIS-IPF-3 was dosed on 9–10 September 2026, the first Phase III for a drug whose target and molecule both came from AI; the trial plans to enroll 320 people at 47 sites in China for 52 weeks13. A lead investigator puts the span from Phase III initiation to possible approval at three to four years under favorable conditions (trade-media retelling); the drug remains investigational13.
  • The first AI-designed antibody in Phase III. Generate:Biomedicines’ GB-0895 (long-acting anti-TSLP for severe asthma) announced two Phase III trials in December 2025 (SOLAIRIA-1 and SOLAIRIA-2, about 1,600 people, 52 weeks) and listed on Nasdaq in February 2026, raising $400 million14,21.
  • Capital keeps concentrating in AI drug design. Isomorphic Labs announced a $5 billion Series C on 17 November 2026 (co-led by Greenoaks and Sequoia), only about six months after its previous $2.1 billion and with no drug yet in the clinic; its disclosed partners include Novartis, Lilly and J&J15,20. Chai Discovery announced a collaboration with Roche on 6 November, using its latest model Chai-316.
  • Frontier-model firms move directly into drug development. OpenAI reportedly acquired Orchestra Bio on 20 October 2026 (folding drug-program agents into Rosalind; terms undisclosed); Anthropic bought Coefficient Bio in April 2026 for about $400 million in stock and on 27 October published early results from six partnerships, most still preclinical and partner-reported17,18,25. Disclosure: this draft was prepared with Claude, developed by Anthropic; the above relies only on public reports.
  • New models at the genome and cell level. Google DeepMind released AlphaGenome 2 on 11 November 2026, predicting the effects of DNA variants in specific cell types; the company says it identified nearly twice as many causal variants as its predecessor on one immune-cell dataset (30% versus 16%); research use only and not clinically validated19.

Four Threads Through the Book

Judgment
① Candidates first, target choice not yet
AI is good at producing molecules with drug-like properties, a reasonable explanation for the Phase I record. Phase II often tests whether the target biology holds in patients, and AI has not yet shown a clear edge there2,3. Note, though, that projects stopping after Phase II may also do so for commercial or operational reasons (per secondary retellings, four of six discontinued cases in BCG’s sample), so “Phase II tests the target” is an inference rather than these analyses’ conclusion (see Chapter 7, Section 8). It remains the yardstick for AI discovery claims.
Judgment
② From point models to platforms and loops
Lilly and NVIDIA describe a “continuous learning system” linking agentic wet labs with computational dry labs in a 24/7 loop4. The direction is data production and model iteration feeding each other.
Judgment
③ Open and closed expand together
Open structure models (the Boltz family) coexist with a closed drug-design engine (IsoDDE) and a trusted-access specialist LLM (GPT-Rosalind). Which one a company picks shapes who owns the data and the model. See Chapter 3.
Judgment
④ Regulation sets the pace stage by stage
The FDA’s January 2025 draft guidance excludes AI used solely for drug discovery or for operational efficiency that does not affect patient safety or data reliability1. Discovery therefore faces fewer constraints, while development and manufacturing face credibility assessments and similar requirements.

In January 2026 the FDA and EMA jointly published ten guiding principles of good AI practice in drug development9; they are non-binding10. Sources through October 2026 still call the FDA’s 2025 guidance a draft; see Chapter 20.

Part I · Landscape and Trends · Chapter 2

Signals: JPM and the Major Conferences

Read industry direction through conference signals: trace the AI storylines of J.P. Morgan Healthcare 2025 and 2026, and build a map of the major pharma conferences.

Source: none (new chapter)
Evidence grade of this chapter■■■□□□□□□□Primary 35% (8/23) · secondary 65%MediumPrimary = papers, regulatory documents and company announcements; a company announcement is a primary source but not independent evidence of effect.
Trend signals · as of 2026-11-18
  • The AI narrative at JPM has moved from partnerships and vision to proof and infrastructure: data ownership and compute alliances became topics.
  • Conference announcements are mostly partnerships and platforms, rarely clinical data; grade the credibility of conference signals.
  • Next checkpoints: JPM 2027, then AACR and ASCO (dates in Appendix F).
Numbers that go stale (dates, funding, valuations) are in Appendix F; changes of judgment between versions are in Appendix E. The above distills and judges this chapter’s content.
In one sentence. Over two years the AI narrative at JPM moved from partnerships and vision to proof and infrastructure. The 2025 headlines were NVIDIA’s alliances with data, genomics and clinical partners; the 2026 consensus is that AI must prove itself through return on investment, is treated as infrastructure rather than a differentiator, and that data ownership has become a negotiating focus1,2,3.

1. Two Years of JPM Side by Side

JPM is an investor conference and also the window where drug makers, technology firms and service providers cluster their partnership announcements, so announcement density is high. The table records only what was found with a source; the sources are post-event summaries with differing perspectives and are not a complete agenda.

DimensionJPM 2025 (13–16 January)JPM 2026 (12–15 January)
Overall toneSustained interest in AI and data, with NVIDIA and data companies grabbing headlines4,5From promise to proof: measurable impact and ROI emphasized, AI treated as infrastructure3,2
Notable announcementsNVIDIA alliances with IQVIA, Illumina, Mayo Clinic and Arc Institute, spanning agents, genomics and digital pathology1The NVIDIA–Lilly co-innovation lab (up to $1 billion)6; NVIDIA–Thermo Fisher autonomous lab collaboration7; AstraZeneca’s agreement to acquire Modella AI8
AI priorities in pharmaNVIDIA said it would start with clinical trials, the longest and costliest workflow in drug and device development5; “physical AI” for robot-assisted surgery also featured9AI-native discovery companies leaned on biological foundation models and proprietary data2; investors were skeptical of generic “AI drug discovery” claims and asked how proprietary data and closed-loop wet labs reduce clinical failure10
LLMs and consumer side—OpenAI launched ChatGPT Health and an enterprise offering for healthcare at the start of the year11; Anthropic announced Claude for Healthcare and expanded life-sciences tools the day before the conference12
DataThe Mayo–NVIDIA digital pathology work involved 20 million whole-slide images9Data ownership and control became a central theme: who owns clinical and real-world data, how it may train proprietary models, and which rights survive the end of a collaboration3
Parallel non-AI themesDealmaking revived, e.g. J&J’s purchase of Intra-Cellular ($14.6 billion)9More disciplined deals; “Obesity 2.0,” licensing from China, radiopharmaceuticals and ADCs drew attention10; more than 20 Chinese companies presented13

2. Three Shifts (This Book’s Judgment)

Judgment
From “what can it do” to “what has it done”
NVIDIA’s 2025 pitch was vision and partnerships; in 2026 the yardstick became measurable impact and return3,2. For drug makers, announcements need data, milestones or time saved to stay credible.
Judgment
From models to compute-and-data alliances
The signature 2026 moves were long, quantified alliances between drug makers and technology firms (Lilly and NVIDIA), not single model releases6. At the same time, data rights inside collaborations became a separate negotiation3.
Judgment
From point tools to agents and workflows
NVIDIA’s Kimberly Powell called 2025 a “breakout year” for agentic AI14; sessions on ambient AI and agent workflows drew standing-room crowds2. The drug-maker counterpart is enterprise-wide agent deployment (see Chapter 4).

4. Turning Conference Signals into Judgment

This section is judgment. Information quality at conferences varies widely; the grading below is this book’s own, not an industry standard.
Signal typeReliabilityExampleHow to read it
Regulatory acceptance or datesHighFDA acceptance of zasocitinib with Priority Review22Can be used directly as a timeline
Peer-reviewed or conference dataFairly highThe ASCO analysis of 117 AI assets18Mind sample size and definitions
Alliances with amounts and termsMediumLilly–NVIDIA up to $1 billion over five years6Shows strength of commitment, not output
Alliances without amountsLowerMost “strategic collaboration” announcementsSignals intent only; look for follow-through
Vision and roadmap statementsLowClaims such as “halving development time”Treat as marketing unless backed by data

5. JPM 2027 Watchlist

The next JPM is scheduled for 11–14 January 202723. These are the questions this book hopes to see answered then, all of them judgments:

  • Review outcomes for AI-originated drugs: the FDA target date for zasocitinib is in Q1 2027 (see Chapter 7).
  • Whether Isomorphic and peers reach the clinic: the publicly stated timing was around the end of 2026, and no drug was yet in the clinic when a $5 billion Series C was disclosed on 17 November 2026.
  • Whether FDA’s AI guidance is finalized: the 2025 draft was still a draft as of October 2026 (see Chapter 20).
  • Data terms: whether reusable templates emerge for data rights, model ownership and post-termination rights in collaboration agreements.
  • Evidence of agent output: whether enterprise agent deployments (such as BMS with Anthropic) can show quantified results.
Limits of this chapter. Most JPM summaries come from consultancies, law firms and trade media with differing vantage points; the full agenda was not reviewed. Coverage of 2025 is thinner than of 2026, so the two-year comparison is asymmetric.

Part I · Landscape and Trends · Chapter 3

The Stack: Compute, Models, Applications

Break AI’s effect on pharma into three layers, and take stock of the frontier models of the past two years.

Source: none (new chapter)
Evidence grade of this chapter■■■□□□□□□□Primary 33% (14/42) · secondary 67%MediumPrimary = papers, regulatory documents and company announcements; a company announcement is a primary source but not independent evidence of effect.
Trend signals · as of 2026-11-18
  • Competition is moving from general models to industry-specific models plus enterprise deployment: GPT-Rosalind, Claude for Life Sciences, Microsoft Discovery and NVIDIA Clara arrived together.
  • Compute is becoming a competitive factor for drug makers: Lilly built its own GPU cluster and Roche deployed more than 3,500 Blackwell GPUs (company claim).
  • Open models such as Boltz-2 lower the bar for structure prediction, while the closed leader’s figures are not yet peer reviewed.
Numbers that go stale (dates, funding, valuations) are in Appendix F; changes of judgment between versions are in Appendix E. The above distills and judges this chapter’s content.
In one sentence. Base-model capability is converging; differences increasingly come from proprietary data, compute alliances, and the ability to embed models in regulated workflows. Open and closed routes are both expanding, and drug makers must decide where to put their data and models.

The Three Layers

LayerRepresentative contentWhat it means for a drug maker
ComputeGPU clusters, cloud, lab-automation infrastructureRent or build; whom to bind to long term
ModelsStructure and affinity foundation models, specialist LLMs, general LLMsOpen, closed or trusted access; the value of fine-tuning on own data
ApplicationsPoint tools, workflows, agents and lab loopsWhether they can be embedded in regulated processes and validated

Compute: Drug Makers Bind Directly to Chip Vendors

  • Lilly’s “AI factory.” In October 2025 Lilly and NVIDIA announced a supercomputer to train biomedical foundation models1; the system, LillyPod, reportedly carries 1,016 NVIDIA Blackwell GPUs and went live in February 202619.
  • Co-innovation lab. On 12 January 2026 (during JPM), NVIDIA and Lilly announced up to $1 billion over five years for a joint lab built on NVIDIA BioNeMo and the Vera Rubin architecture, with a planned “continuous learning system” linking agentic wet labs and computational dry labs2.
  • Automated labs. Around the same time NVIDIA announced a collaboration with Thermo Fisher on autonomous lab infrastructure, aiming to turn labs into scalable “data factories”3.
  • Novo Nordisk. Novo Nordisk reportedly agreed in June 2025 to house an NVIDIA supercomputer at the Danish Centre for AI Innovation4.
  • Roche and Pfizer. On 16 March 2026 Roche announced 2,176 additional on-premises Blackwell GPUs, bringing combined capacity above 3,500, which it calls the largest announced by a drug maker; Pfizer is reportedly expanding to more than 1,200 GPUs20,21.

Judgment. Compute is shifting from a purchased resource to a strategic alliance. Lilly’s announcement is not only about GPUs but about using labs to generate large-scale ground-truth data for model training2. GPU counts have become a publicly compared yardstick between drug makers (about 1,000 at Lilly, over 3,500 at Roche), but compute can be rented; a loop that keeps producing high-quality experimental data is harder to copy.

Models: The Frontier of the Past Two Years

Models fall into two families: biomolecular models (predicting structure and binding, designing molecules) and vertical LLMs (reasoning, retrieval, documents and tool use). The table lists only models whose sources were checked in this draft; coverage is incomplete, with gaps listed at the end of the chapter.

ModelDeveloper / dateOpennessWhat it doesCaveats
AlphaFold 3Google DeepMind and Isomorphic Labs, 2024Not fully open5Predicts complex structures across proteins, DNA, RNA and ligands6Limits on novel chemistry and dynamic interactions7
Boltz-1 / Boltz-2MIT, later Boltz PBC; Boltz-1 Nov 2024, Boltz-2 Jun 2025Open (weights and training code)5Boltz-2 predicts structure and binding affinity jointly; the paper says it approaches FEP accuracy on public benchmarks8The authors caution that public-benchmark results do not capture all of real drug discovery8
BoltzGenBoltz, Oct 2025Open9Generates protein and peptide bindersThe Pfizer collaboration covers Boltz-2 and BoltzGen9
Chai-1Chai DiscoveryNon-commercial use5Multimodal structure predictionLilly’s Chai deal is worth up to $1.3 billion (biologics design)4
IsoDDEIsomorphic Labs, 10 Feb 2026Closed; technical report only, not peer reviewed10,11Combines protein-ligand structure prediction, binding-affinity estimation, cryptic-pocket detection and antibody-antigen interface prediction6The company reports more than double AlphaFold 3’s accuracy on hard benchmarks10; outside commentators note it may benefit from private partner data, whose effect cannot be judged11
GPT-RosalindOpenAI, launched 16 Apr 2026; left research preview 11 SepClosed; offered to eligible organizations through a trusted-access program12,13Reasoning across chemistry, protein engineering and genomics, with a Codex life-sciences plug-in linking more than 50 scientific tools and data sources12Benchmark results are the vendor’s own; launch partners included Amgen, Moderna and Thermo Fisher14
Claude for Life Sciences / HealthcareAnthropic, 20 Oct 2025; expanded 11 Jan 2026Closed; packaged connectors and skills15Connects to Benchling, Medidata, ClinicalTrials.gov and more; drafts trial protocols, prepares regulatory documents, monitors trial operations15,16This draft was assisted by Claude; this row relies only on public material, so please verify independently

Protein language models such as ESM-3 take a different route: they are trained on evolutionary sequences rather than predicting structure directly5.

Three Technical Routes

Judgment
Structure and binding foundation models
AlphaFold 3, Boltz, Chai and IsoDDE belong here. The contest has moved from predicting structure to predicting binding strength, finding cryptic pockets and designing molecules. Open (Boltz) and closed (IsoDDE) camps advance together.
Judgment
Vertical LLMs
GPT-Rosalind and Claude’s life-sciences products both stress model plus tools plus controlled access. They share a trusted-access or enterprise-security release pattern, partly because of dual-use risk12,14.
Judgment
Fine-tuning on private pharma data
On 8 January 2026 Pfizer and Boltz announced a collaboration to build Pfizer-specific models on top of open models using Pfizer’s historical data9. It is the template for “open base plus private data.”

Applications: From Point Tools to Loops

StageTraitExamples
Point toolsOne task, human judgment in betweenStructure prediction, affinity scoring, virtual screening
WorkflowsMulti-step tasks embedded in business systemsTrial protocol drafting, regulatory document preparation, Medidata-connected trial monitoring16
LoopsThe model proposes experiments, and results flow back into trainingThe agentic wet-lab and dry-lab cycle envisaged by Lilly and NVIDIA2

In May 2026 Bristol Myers Squibb and Anthropic signed a strategic agreement whose priorities include drafting clinical study reports, supporting regulatory submissions, and identifying root causes of manufacturing deviations in real time17. Announcements like this are plentiful while independent effect evidence remains scarce; later chapters separate announcements from verified results.

Value Capture: Who Gets the Value

This section is judgment, not fact. The inferences below are drawn from the public information above and are offered for discussion.
LayerMain moatImplication for drug makers
ComputeScale, supply, efficiencyLock in long-term partnerships; look beyond compute to data-generation capacity
ModelsProprietary data and partnerships; open models make base capability convergeOwn data is the core asset; decide which data may be used to train external models
ApplicationsDepth of workflow integration, compliance and validationWhether the use case can be justified under FDA/EMA credibility frameworks18

Operating Model: What a Drug Maker Needs

  • Data governance: define which data may train internal models, which may go to external models, and how traceability is kept.
  • Access and security: specialist LLMs are mostly released through trusted access, so companies need to prepare eligibility and usage controls.
  • Validation and credibility: under FDA and EMA principles, assess risk and validate models per context of use18.
  • Vendor strategy: open, closed and in-house routes can be combined, but need explicit criteria for choosing.

Frontier Update (November 2026)

Earlier versions listed models and companies here as not yet checked. They have now been checked one by one: the table lists new models and moves by organization, current to 18 November 2026. Most figures come from company blogs, preprints or press releases and are not independently verified, as the Basis column notes.

OrganizationModel or moveStatus and key pointsBasis
Google / DeepMindAlphaGenome and AlphaGenome 2AlphaGenome came out in 2025 with a non-commercial research API23; AlphaGenome 2 was released on 11 November 2026 and predicts the effects of DNA variants in specific cell types, trained on more than 100 million single-cell profiles across 68 tissues; on one immune-cell dataset it identified nearly twice as many causal variants as its predecessor (30% versus 16%); the non-commercial API is open and commercial use is a private preview on Google Cloud22Company blog and a bioRxiv preprint; research only, not clinically validated
Google / DeepMindCo-Scientist (multi-agent research assistant)A Gemini-based multi-agent system with initial validation on drug repurposing for acute myeloid leukemia and on liver-fibrosis targets; FTLOScience says the results were published in Nature in 2026 and that its proposal to repurpose vorinostat for liver fibrosis showed anti-scarring effects in organoids24,30Media and a secondary overview; the original paper was not checked here
MicrosoftMicrosoft Discovery, BioEmuDiscovery reached general availability at Build in June 2026 (an enterprise agentic R&D platform for chemistry, materials and life sciences), with GSK exploring it for drug-development workflows and Ginkgo and Causaly as partners25,26; BioEmu is a generative model of protein conformational ensembles trained on more than 200 milliseconds of molecular-dynamics simulation and about half a million protein-stability experiments (July 2025)27Media reports; the scale of GSK’s exploration is undisclosed
Chai DiscoveryChai-2, Chai-3Chai-2 (June 2025) designs antibodies de novo: with 20 or fewer designs tested per target, about half of 52 targets gave a working antibody in one experimental round, an overall hit rate of about 16%28,30; the latest Chai-3 is used in collaborations with Pfizer (June 2026) and Roche (6 November 2026), with Lilly, Novartis, argenx and BMS also named; a $400 million Series C in July 2026 at a $3.8 billion valuation29,30Company preprint and press releases; hit rates are company-reported and not independently reproduced
Protein and antibody design (academia and startups)RFdiffusion antibodies, ESM3, XairaBaker’s lab reported antibodies designed with a fine-tuned RFdiffusion in Nature in November 2025, with cryo-EM confirming the designed binding mode; EvolutionaryScale’s ESM3 generated a glowing protein only 58% similar in sequence to known fluorescent proteins; Xaira released the virtual-cell model X-Cell in March 2026 and in October the antibody platform X-Design with its first pipeline details (preclinical)30Secondary overview (FTLOScience); some results come from papers
Virtual cellsArc State, Xaira X-Cell, Chinese teamsArc Institute’s State was trained on 167 million cells; Xaira’s X-Cell reportedly has 4.9 billion parameters; per vcbeat, China’s virtual-cell track has had nearly ten financings since April 202630,33Secondary; virtual-cell predictions still need experimental checks
NVIDIAClara open models, BioNeMoClara open models (including CodonFM and La-Proteina) launched in October 202531; BioNeMo was expanded on 12 January 2026 (RNAPro, ReaSyn v2, BioNeMo Recipes, nvMolKit), with more than 20 firms said to be adopting it, including Basecamp Research, Boltz and Chai32Company press releases
Chinese playersBioMap xTrimo, ByteDance Protenix, Hengrui Lingzhu, XtalPi, Westlake PinalBioMap’s xTrimo V4 is said to have 268 billion parameters and to be involved in more than 60 proof-of-concept projects, and market sources say it filed for a Hong Kong listing; ByteDance developed the structure-prediction model Protenix; Hengrui built its own Lingzhu AIDD platform; XtalPi built a closed-loop autonomous discovery system; a Westlake team reported Pinal in Nature in November 2026, which turns text descriptions of function into protein sequences33,34,35,36,41Secondary (36Kr, SCMP and others) and company claims; scale and listing information not verified
OpenAIGPT-Rosalind; Orchestra Bio acquisitionGPT-Rosalind launched in April 2026 (trusted access); on 20 October 2026 OpenAI reportedly acquired Orchestra Bio, folding its drug-program agents into Rosalind, terms undisclosed37Media report (Axios exclusive)
AnthropicCoefficient Bio acquisition, Claude Science, Life Sciences Verification ProgramReportedly acquired Coefficient Bio (a startup of fewer than ten people) for about $400 million in stock in April 2026; Anthropic did not confirm the deal38; on 27 October it published early results from partnerships with BigHat, BMS, Divergence Labs, Enveda, Generate and The Column Group, saying most work is still preclinical; checked against Anthropic’s text: Divergence Labs generated about 35,000 antibody designs in about a month, with early tests finding 3 that bind IgE and 20 that dislodge it, 3 of which appeared to dislodge IgE more than the standard medication; at Enveda, over 1,061 pages of manufacturing documents Claude caught all 11 issues humans had found plus 7 more, with human review time down 89% (from more than 25 hours to under 3), and one regulatory-document draft took 4 hours against about three months (human review found one minor error); the BMS case is analysis for early clinical-development decisions39,42; on 17 September it launched the Life Sciences Verification Program in beta, giving verified teams a version of the safeguards that is more permissive for biology research40Partner-reported, mostly preclinical; disclosure: this draft was prepared with Claude

What These Developments Suggest (This Book’s Judgment)

  • De novo antibody and protein design is moving from papers to pharma contracts: Chai partnered with Lilly, Pfizer, Roche and others in 2026, and Generate’s AI-designed antibody has entered Phase III29,30. But hit rates and developability are different things, and clinical readouts are the test.
  • Genome and virtual-cell models bring the target question to the front: AlphaGenome 2, State and X-Cell all aim to judge earlier whether a target holds, which is exactly the link where Chapters 1 and 7 say AI has not yet shown an edge22,30.
  • Frontier-model firms have started buying drug-development capability: both OpenAI and Anthropic acquired life-sciences teams in 202637,38. This changes the “consultancies versus frontier-model firms” picture in Chapter 6.
  • China’s models and capital are a second pole: from BioMap to Insilico, models, listings and clinical progress are happening together34,35; the “China-origin assets” story in Chapters 4 and 10 should therefore be read together with the model layer.

Still Not Covered

The following are still unchecked, and their absence does not mean they are missing or unimportant: details of genomic language models such as the Evo series, healthcare models from Meta, Amazon and IBM, details of Recursion’s and Generate’s in-house models, Japanese players’ models, and the latest versions of open medical models such as MedGemma.

Part II · Industry Landscape · Chapter 4

How Pharma and Biotech Are Positioning on AI

A company-by-company comparison of the ten largest drug makers by 2025 revenue: compute, partners, build-versus-buy choices, and governance signals.

Source: Ch. 1 (structure of a full-service pharma company; evolution of the biotech company)
Evidence grade of this chapter■■■□□□□□□□Primary 31% (14/45) · secondary 69%MediumPrimary = papers, regulatory documents and company announcements; a company announcement is a primary source but not independent evidence of effect.
Trend signals · as of 2026-11-18
  • Drug makers are moving from pilots to enterprise agreements and in-house compute: BMS with Anthropic, Sanofi with OpenAI, Lilly with NVIDIA.
  • Multi-vendor strategies are becoming normal rather than betting on one model firm (as BMS and Novartis do).
  • Japanese drug makers lean toward shared compute plus open models and Chinese ones toward in-house platforms plus collaborations, unlike the Western enterprise-agreement route.
Numbers that go stale (dates, funding, valuations) are in Appendix F; changes of judgment between versions are in Appendix E. The above distills and judges this chapter’s content.
In one sentence. Within the search scope, each of the ten largest drug makers has announced at least one external AI alliance or acquisition, but the routes clearly diverge: Lilly and Roche pull compute and data in-house; Novartis, Pfizer and J&J bet on outside platforms for discovery; AstraZeneca chose to buy its partner; BMS, Merck and Sanofi focus on enterprise agents and document workflows1,2,3,4,5.

1. Scope and Method

“Leading” means the top ten by full-year 2025 revenue: J&J, Roche, Lilly, Merck, Pfizer, AbbVie, AstraZeneca, Novartis, Sanofi and BMS1. J&J’s revenue includes medical devices, which are covered in Chapter 5. Only sourced public information is recorded for each company; what could not be found is marked “not found,” never filled by guesswork.

Evidence note. Partnership and product announcements are mostly primary sources, but effect figures such as “time saved” or “shorter cycles” are nearly always company claims or secondary retellings. This chapter quotes only sourced figures and labels their type. It does not judge whether any company’s AI strategy is good or bad.

2. The Ten Companies One by One

Eli Lilly

FY2025 revenue $65.2B (+45%) · Route: own compute + joint lab + open its models
  • Compute: LillyPod runs on NVIDIA DGX SuperPOD B300 and reportedly carries 1,016 Blackwell GPUs; it went live in February 20266,7.
  • Joint lab: in January 2026 it announced a co-innovation lab with NVIDIA, up to $1 billion over five years, linking agentic wet labs and computational dry labs2.
  • Opening up: TuneLab, launched in September 2025, gives biotechs models trained on Lilly’s research data through federated learning, with neither side exposing its data8.
  • External deals: a biologics-design deal with Chai Discovery worth up to $1.3 billion9; the Isomorphic collaboration (nearly $3 billion with Novartis combined)10.
  • Organization: first Chief AI Officer, Thomas Fuchs, appointed in October 20247.
Judgment: Lilly holds compute, data and a joint lab most completely of the ten, and uses TuneLab to turn its data into an ecosystem entry point.

Johnson & Johnson

FY2025 revenue $94.2B (including devices) · Route: external platform for discovery
  • Isomorphic: in January 2026 it signed a cross-modality, multi-target research collaboration with Isomorphic Labs; Isomorphic handles in silico prediction and design, while J&J runs the experimental assays and advances selected programs11.
  • Scope: unlike Isomorphic’s earlier deals with Lilly and Novartis, which centered on small molecules, the J&J collaboration also covers large molecules and biologics10.
  • Not found: public detail on compute or enterprise agents in J&J’s drug business was not found in this draft; device-side AI is in Chapter 5.
Judgment: In what was found, J&J’s drug-side AI is mainly buying discovery capability from an outside platform while keeping experimental validation in-house.

Roche (incl. Genentech)

FY2025 revenue about $74.1B (including diagnostics) · Route: own compute + closed-loop experimentation
  • Compute: on 16 March 2026 it announced 2,176 additional on-premises NVIDIA Blackwell GPUs, bringing combined on-premises and cloud capacity above 3,500, which it calls the largest announced pharma GPU footprint; the NVIDIA collaboration began in 20233.
  • Lab-in-the-Loop: Genentech links experiments, data and AI models in an iterative loop and uses NVIDIA BioNeMo to train and fine-tune biological and molecular foundation models12.
  • Coverage: reportedly nearly 90% of Genentech’s eligible small-molecule programs integrate AI13.
  • Manufacturing and diagnostics: NVIDIA Omniverse digital twins of production facilities; in diagnostics, digital pathology and genomic data analysis3.
Judgment: Roche shares one compute base across its pharma and diagnostics businesses, a structure few peers have. “Largest GPU footprint” is the company’s own claim.

Merck & Co. (MSD)

FY2025 revenue $65.0B · Route: enterprise agents + data partnerships
  • Clinical documents: in June 2025 it co-developed an internal generative AI platform with McKinsey and its AI arm QuantumBlack to speed first drafts of critical-path clinical study documents, with qualified medical writers overseeing the work14.
  • Clinical data: in February 2026 it partnered with Mayo Clinic, using de-identified multimodal clinical data with Merck’s virtual-cell technology to improve target identification and early development decisions15,16.
  • Cloud and agents: in April 2026 it reportedly agreed a multi-year collaboration with Google Cloud of up to $1 billion to deploy agentic AI across R&D, manufacturing, commercial and corporate functions17.
Judgment: Merck’s public moves center on document workflows and data access, unlike the compute route of Lilly and Roche. The Google Cloud item comes from a secondary report.

Pfizer

FY2025 revenue $62.6B (−2%) · Route: partner-led, with AI supporting cost reduction
  • Boltz: on 8 January 2026 it announced a multi-year collaboration with Boltz to train Pfizer-specific models on its historical data atop open models, with Boltz scientists and Pfizer teams building custom workflows18.
  • Other partners: XtalPi (including a quantum-computing angle) and PostEra19; the XtalPi molecular-modeling collaboration was expanded in June 202520.
  • Compute and organization: reportedly expanding to more than 1,200 GPUs (February 2026); a new Chief AI, Data and Analytics Officer in 2026; in April 2025 it added $1.2 billion of savings by 2027, driven largely by digital enablement, AI and automation21.
  • Commercial side: the generative AI marketing platform Charlie and GENESIS for automating safety-monitoring administration21.
  • Ambition: CIO Dennis Hancock aims to use AI to reinvent the whole “DNA to patient” chain19.
Judgment: Pfizer is marked by many partners and clear commercial and cost uses. Compute and leadership details come from a secondary index, and AI leadership changed in 2026.

AbbVie

FY2025 revenue $61.2B (+8.6%) · Route: in-house data platform + a burst of recent partnerships
  • ARCH platform: the AbbVie R&D Convergence Hub pulls over 200 data sources into knowledge graphs for target discovery and predictive modeling22; AbbVie does not publicly disclose specific time savings from ARCH23.
  • Iambic: on 21 September 2026 it announced a multi-year collaboration with Iambic on small-molecule discovery across immunology, neuroscience and oncology24.
  • Valkai: on 29 September 2026 it announced a partnership with Valkai to apply AI in targeted areas of clinical development, aiming to reduce manual processing; no financial terms disclosed25.
  • Enterprise program: “AI@AbbVie” spans research, development and operations25.
Judgment: AbbVie announced two external collaborations in September 2026 spanning discovery and clinical work, while keeping its in-house data platform.

AstraZeneca

FY2025 revenue $58.7B (+8%) · Route: buy the partner
  • Modella AI: partnership from July 2025, then an agreement to acquire in January 2026 (around JPM); Modella’s pathology foundation models, data and staff move into oncology R&D, focused on clinical development and biomarker discovery; financial terms undisclosed4,26.
  • Motivation: the CFO called the earlier collaboration a “test drive” and said the aim was to bring the data, models and people in-house, reducing dependence on outside roadmaps4.
  • Organization: Jorge Reis-Filho, Chief of AI for Science Innovation, said the acquisition brings frontier pathology foundation models and AI agents27.
Judgment: Within the search, AstraZeneca is the only one of the ten to announce buying an AI company. Its value depends on delivering in clinical trials after integration.

Novartis

FY2025 revenue $54.5B · Route: multi-platform bets + board-level governance
  • Isomorphic: started in January 2024 with three targets, a $37.5 million upfront payment and up to $1.2 billion in milestones28; expanded in February 2025 by up to three programs29.
  • Other platforms: a 2024 deal with Generate:Biomedicines worth up to $1.1 billion ($65 million upfront)30; a November 2024 Schrödinger deal with $150 million upfront31.
  • Governance: the 2025 annual report names AI as a strategic risk, and from 2026 data, digital and AI are a standing board topic31.
Judgment: Novartis bets simultaneously on structure prediction (Isomorphic), generative biologics (Generate) and physics-based simulation (Schrödinger), and lifts governance to board level.

Sanofi

FY2025 revenue about $49.3B · Route: early “AI at scale,” now watching post-leadership change
  • OpenAI and Formation Bio: a three-way collaboration in May 2024; Sanofi provides proprietary data, OpenAI model fine-tuning and expertise, and Formation Bio engineering and a development platform, aiming at custom AI software and agents for drug development32.
  • Earlier AI deals: Owkin (expanded into immunology), Aqemia (December 2023, worth $140 million) and BioMap (October 2023, potentially worth over $1 billion)32.
  • Internal use: plai, a decision-support app built with Aily Labs and rolled out at scale in 202333.
  • Leadership: Paul Hudson, the CEO most associated with “AI at scale,” reportedly left on 17 February 2026 and Belén Garijo is the new CEO; whether AI stays a stated public priority awaits the new management’s word33.
Judgment: Sanofi was among the first drug makers to state “AI at scale” as a company goal; whether it continues after the leadership change is the point to watch.

Bristol Myers Squibb (BMS)

FY2025 revenue $48.2B · Route: enterprise-wide agent deployment + multi-vendor
  • Anthropic: on 21 May 2026 it announced a strategic agreement to deploy Claude Enterprise across research, clinical development, manufacturing, commercial and corporate functions as a shared intelligence platform for more than 30,000 employees5,34.
  • Three priorities: accelerating software engineering with Claude Code; embedding AI agents in discovery and development workflows; connecting fragmented institutional knowledge34.
  • Use cases: intelligent assistance for clinical study reports, patient safety narratives and regulatory submissions, aiming to shorten the time from clinical data completion to filing; support for quality investigations in manufacturing35.
  • Background: built on over three years of AI investment, with employees already given multiple frontier models through an internal platform in a multi-vendor strategy; in January 2026 it also partnered with the AI biotech Immunai35,34.
  • Disclosure: Anthropic develops Claude, the drafter of this book; this item relies only on public material.
Judgment: BMS is the most explicit of the ten in centering on enterprise agents rather than a discovery platform, while keeping multi-vendor options open.

BMSNot foundAnthropic, ImmunaiEnterprise agents, multi-vendorThree-plus years of internal investment

4. Five Routes (This Book’s Judgment)

RouteRepresentative companiesWhat it gainsCost and risk
Own compute and dataLilly, RocheControl and tight coupling with experimental loopsHeavy capital; needs sustained data production and talent
Buy discovery platformsNovartis, J&J, AbbVie, PfizerFrontier capability at lower fixed costOpaque methods; dependence on partner’s pace; complex data and IP terms
Acquire and internalizeAstraZenecaTalent, data and models in-houseIntegration risk; acquisition premium
Enterprise agents and document workflowsBMS, Merck, SanofiBroad coverage, relatively quick returnsEffect evidence mostly company claims; compliance and validation costs
Open data and models outwardLilly (TuneLab)Pulls in the biotech ecosystem and external dataData protection and competitive boundaries need careful design

The routes are not mutually exclusive: Lilly builds, buys and opens at once, and BMS works with Immunai beyond its agents. The difference is where the weight sits.

5. Other Companies Worth Noting

CompanySignalSource
Novo NordiskSigned a strategic alliance with OpenAI to apply its technology across operations; in June 2025 agreed to house an NVIDIA supercomputer at the Danish Centre for AI Innovation36,9Secondary
GSKOn 8 January 2026 it signed with Noetik: $50 million upfront plus near-term milestones to license AI virtual-cell models for cancer research20Secondary
AmgenOne of the customers working with OpenAI’s GPT-Rosalind at launch36Secondary
TakedaZasocitinib is a computation-designed asset with an FDA target date in Q1 2027 (see Chapter 7)37Primary

6. Big Pharma and Biotech: A Division of Labor

The source book treats the evolution of the biotech company as part of the pharma ecosystem. In an AI setting the current division of labor is roughly: AI-native biotechs own platforms and early assets, while big pharma supplies data, experimental validation, clinical development and commercialization, and gains platform access through partnership, licensing or acquisition. Evidence: Insilico’s rentosertib was discovered on its own platform and carried to Phase III38; Isomorphic provides design capability to Novartis, Lilly and J&J and had no drug in the clinic when it disclosed $5 billion of funding on 17 November 2026, saying it will move candidates toward the clinic, so this division of labor may change11,39; Boltz works with Pfizer through open models plus enterprise services18. This generalizes from known deals (judgment) and is not a law of the industry; whether it evolves into big pharma buying platform companies, as with AstraZeneca–Modella, depends on later deals.

7. Operating Model: Five Questions for Judging a Drug Maker’s AI Announcement

  • Data rights: who owns the training data and the resulting models, and which rights survive termination? (See the JPM 2026 data theme in Chapter 2.)
  • Compute commitment: rented, built, or bound long-term to a chip vendor? Are amounts and terms disclosed?
  • Measurable output: are milestones, time savings or clinical data given, or only the partner’s name?
  • Governance: is AI in the boardroom and the risk framework, and who answers for model validation? (Tied to the FDA and EMA credibility expectations.)
  • Multi-vendor strategy: is the ability to switch preserved? BMS and Novartis both use several platforms at once.

Supplement: Japanese and Chinese Drug Makers (November 2026)

Earlier versions covered only the ten drug makers ranked by 2025 revenue and omitted Japanese and Chinese ones. The table adds what public information shows; most comes from NVIDIA’s regional event posts and trade media, so it leans toward company accounts and cannot be compared line by line with the ten above.

CompanyAI moves found in public informationBasis
Takeda (Japan)Collaborating with Boltz to deploy BoltzMol-1 and BoltzProt-1 across its research organization for structure prediction, affinity estimation and generative design; zasocitinib is under FDA Priority Review (see Chapter 7); reprioritized its pipeline in 2025 and stepped back from cell and gene therapy development40,41Secondary

Effect on this chapter’s judgment (this book’s judgment): Japanese drug makers lean toward shared compute plus open models (the Tokyo-1 consortium, Boltz-2, BioNeMo), while Chinese ones lean toward in-house platforms plus collaborations with AI companies (Hengrui, Qilu, Luoxin); both differ from Western big pharma’s enterprise agreements. All of this is company accounts with little effect evidence.

Limits of this chapter. Public information is mostly company announcements and secondary indexes, and it changes quickly. Compute information for Merck, AbbVie, AstraZeneca, Novartis, Sanofi and J&J was not found; Japanese and Chinese drug makers are now outlined in the supplement above, but only for moves visible in public information, not a firm-by-firm deep dive.
