Part II · Industry Landscape · Chapter 5

How Medical Device Companies Are Positioning on AI

Treated separately from pharma: for device makers AI is both the product and the regulated object. A company-by-company look at the top device makers by revenue, plus Intuitive as the surgical-robotics leader.

Source: none (the source book focuses on drug makers)
Evidence grade of this chapter■■■□□□□□□□Primary 34% (13/38) · secondary 66%MediumPrimary = papers, regulatory documents and company announcements; a company announcement is a primary source but not independent evidence of effect.
Trend signals · as of 2026-11-18
  • Authorizations of device AI are rising while independent effect evidence lags: only 3 of 1,357 FDA-authorized AI devices have been tested on patient outcomes.
  • The competitive focus is shifting from imaging AI to surgical robots and data platforms.
  • Chinese and Japanese device makers are joining with in-house large models and NVIDIA platforms (United Imaging, Mindray, Fujifilm, Canon).
Numbers that go stale (dates, funding, valuations) are in Appendix F; changes of judgment between versions are in Appendix E. The above distills and judges this chapter’s content.
In one sentence. Device makers’ AI lands in three places: imaging and diagnostics (radiology makes up the great majority of the FDA’s AI device list), surgical robots and surgical-data platforms, and connected hospital operations. Unlike in pharma, AI in devices is itself the regulated, sold product, not only an R&D tool1,2,3,4.

1. Why Devices Deserve Their Own Chapter

DimensionDrug makersDevice makers
Role of AIMostly an R&D and operations tool; it enters regulatory view when used in submissions5AI is often embedded in the product as a regulated, sellable feature6
Regulatory pathThe FDA draft guidance proposes context-of-use credibility assessment5Authorization through 510(k), De Novo or PMA, then listing on the FDA’s AI-enabled device list6
Value carrierMolecules, assets and clinical outcomesInstalled base, consumables, software subscriptions and the data devices generate
Data sourceExperiments, omics and clinical trialsImaging, surgical video, device operation and connected-device data2,4

The FDA’s AI device list is dominated by radiology: in the fourth quarter of 2025, 72 new authorizations were added, of which 55 (76%) were radiology. By radiology AI authorizations, GE HealthCare has 120 (including acquisitions), Siemens Healthineers 89 (including Varian), Philips 50, Canon 45, United Imaging 38, Aidoc 31 and DeepHealth 281. These figures are retold from Imaging Wire and are secondary.

2. Scope

By the 2026 Medtech Big 100 revenue ranking the top ten are Medtronic ($36.4 billion), J&J MedTech ($33.8 billion), Medline ($28.4 billion), Siemens Healthineers ($26.4 billion), Stryker ($25.1 billion), Abbott’s device business ($21.4 billion), GE HealthCare ($20.6 billion), Philips ($20.2 billion), Boston Scientific ($20.1 billion) and BD ($18.5 billion)7,8. Medline is mainly a maker and distributor of medical supplies, and its AI moves were not researched here. Intuitive Surgical, the surgical-robotics leader, is listed separately although it is outside the revenue top ten, because it is the core subject of Chapter 17.

3. Company by Company

Medtronic

No. 1 on the 2026 list · revenue $36.4B · Route: surgical robot + surgical-data platform + endoscopy AI
  • Robot: the Hugo RAS received FDA clearance for urologic procedures in December 2025; 510(k) submissions for general and gynecologic use followed in June 20262.
  • Surgical AI platform: in July 2026 it showed Touch Surgery Aide, an AI-native surgical computing platform running on NVIDIA Holoscan, CUDA and TensorRT; the Touch Surgery ecosystem is in more than 1,500 operating rooms worldwide2.
  • First real-time AI application: Instrument Exit Point uses computer vision to warn when instruments leave the field of view; it is FDA-cleared and runs on Hugo2,9.
  • Endoscopy: management says AI in colonoscopy (GI Genius) finds about 50% more polyps than physicians alone (company claim)10.
  • Capital moves: a $700 million investment in surgical-robot partner Cornerstone Robotics, with distribution of its Sentire system11.
Judgment: Medtronic builds robot, surgical data and real-time AI apps into one platform bound to NVIDIA compute, a path similar to Lilly and Roche in pharma.

Johnson & Johnson MedTech

No. 2 on the 2026 list · revenue $33.8B · Route: new soft-tissue robot + digital ecosystem
  • Ottava: in July 2026 it received FDA De Novo authorization as the first table-integrated soft-tissue robotic system, covering ten general-surgery procedures of the upper abdomen3,12.
  • Digital ecosystem: Ottava connects to the Polyphonic digital ecosystem, bringing together learning, media and data-driven insights3.
  • Automation: software coordinates four table-integrated arms to automate predefined poses and simplify setup and breakdown3.
  • Competition: seen as a challenge to Intuitive’s da Vinci; J&J plans to split off its DePuy Synthes orthopedics business by mid-202713,8.
Judgment: J&J MedTech’s AI story centers on architecture, data and automation; what is public so far is mostly workflow automation such as automated positioning rather than intelligent decision-making.

Siemens Healthineers

No. 4 on the 2026 list · revenue $26.4B · Route: imaging AI + radiotherapy AI + therapy-segment consolidation
  • Strategy: at its November 2025 capital markets day it set out the “Elevating Health Globally” phase with AI as a key lever, and created a “Precision Therapy” segment combining Varian, Advanced Therapies and Ultrasound14.
  • Radiotherapy: it is working with NVIDIA on next-generation adaptive radiotherapy, with Varian involved, using NVIDIA Nemotron open foundation models for agentic co-planning15.
  • Authorizations: second on the radiology AI authorization ranking (89, including Varian)1.
  • Robotics: a neurovascular robotics partnership with Stryker announced in September 202516.
  • National program: in June 2026 it joined the Netherlands’ ten-year AI4Health programme (total investment €112.6 million) with an in-kind contribution of about €6 million, and takes part in radiotherapy planning and risk-assessment projects17.
Judgment: Siemens Healthineers places AI in an imaging-plus-radiotherapy combination; with Varian, AI extends from diagnosis to treatment planning.

Stryker

No. 5 on the 2026 list · revenue $25.1B · Route: robot installed base + smart hospital
  • Mako robots: more than 3,000 installed; at the end of 2025 over two-thirds of US knees and over a third of hips were done on Mako, with Mako 4 extending to shoulder (mid-2026) and spine18.
  • SmartCare: a new SmartCare business unit combines Vocera and care.ai for smart hospitals and healthcare IT19.
  • care.ai: AI-assisted virtual care workflows and ambient intelligence, acquisition announced in 202419.
  • Partnership: neurovascular robotics with Siemens Healthineers16.
Judgment: Stryker’s AI shows up mainly in orthopedic-robot software iteration and ambient hospital intelligence; its public material says little about specific models.

Abbott (device business)

No. 6 on the 2026 list · device revenue $21.4B · Route: consumer data + cardiac imaging AI
  • Libre Assist: a generative AI feature launched in the Libre app in January 2026 that uses food photos or text with continuous glucose data to predict how meals affect glucose20,21.
  • Cardiac: it reportedly acquired the cardiac-imaging AI company LARALAB in October 2025; it works with iCardio.ai on AI for the EnSite X mapping system; the Ultreon OCT platform uses AI21.
  • Evidence note: these items come largely from secondary trackers and analyses; Abbott’s own primary material was not retrieved in this draft.
Judgment: Abbott runs a consumer-data line (Libre) and a cardiac-intervention imaging line in parallel; the evidence level is lower and primary sources are needed.

GE HealthCare

No. 7 on the 2026 list · revenue $20.6B · Route: lead in authorizations + acquisitions + agentic research
  • Authorizations: in July 2025 it announced 100 FDA AI device authorizations, topping the list for a fourth year, with a goal of more than 200 by 20286. On the radiology ranking it has 120, including assets from acquisitions such as Bay Labs, Caption Health, MIM Software and icometrix1.
  • Products: AIR Recon DL uses deep learning to shorten MRI scans (by up to 50%) and is estimated to have scanned over 50 million patients since its 2020 launch6.
  • Frontier research: October 2025 AI Innovation Lab projects include an agentic AI diagnostic imaging assistant for radiology, agentic assessment of incidental CT findings, and fine-tuning an MRI foundation model with Mass General Brigham and UW–Madison22.
Judgment: GE HealthCare uses authorization count as its public yardstick and keeps adding AI assets through acquisitions. Authorization count does not equal clinical value.

Philips

No. 8 on the 2026 list · revenue $20.2B · Route: imaging foundation model + connected care
  • MRI foundation model: in May 2025 it announced a joint MR foundation model with NVIDIA built on VISTA-3D and MAISI; in 2026 it showed a predictive preview (“see before scanning”) pointing toward more autonomous, “self-driving” MRI23,24.
  • New products: in February 2026 it launched the helium-free 3.0T MRI BlueSeal Horizon and the AI-powered spectral CT Verida, stressing AI workflow automation25.
  • Authorizations: 50 on the radiology AI ranking (including DiA and TomTec)1.
Judgment: Philips competes on the same imaging track as Siemens and GE, differing in a narrative built on foundation models plus automated scanning.

Boston Scientific

No. 9 on the 2026 list · revenue $20.1B · Route: electrophysiology mapping and AI (limited public detail)
  • Electrophysiology: Farapulse pulsed-field ablation paired with the OPAL HDx mapping system; in the third quarter of 2025 about one in three Farapulse accounts used the integrated mapping system26.
  • Where AI sits: AI appears mainly in mapping and navigation software; no primary Boston Scientific material on AI itself was retrieved, so specific models and effects are not detailed.
  • Other moves: a planned acquisition of Penumbra that analysts expect to close by the end of 202627; this is portfolio, not an AI move.
Judgment: The evidence is insufficient to judge Boston Scientific’s AI strategy; this entry is marked as pending.

BD (Becton Dickinson)

No. 10 on the 2026 list · revenue $18.5B · Route: hospital medication management and connected-device data
  • Incada platform: in October 2025 it launched the Incada Connected Care Platform with the next-generation Pyxis Pro dispensing system, drawing on data from nearly 3 million smart connected devices28,4.
  • AI use cases: forecasting hospital drug demand and reducing medication waste; in April 2026 it brought Pyxis Pro and Incada to Europe, with the AI-powered Incada Analytics planned for Europe the following year4.
  • Guardrails: BD stresses boundaries and mitigations against model hallucination and judges projects on financial return and clinical outcomes28.
Judgment: BD is the most hospital-operations-oriented of the ten, with AI tightly coupled to medication-management workflows that intersect the drug supply chain.

Intuitive Surgical

Outside the revenue top ten · Q3 2026 revenue above $3B · Route: data loop + incremental autonomy
  • Scale: in Q3 2026 worldwide procedures grew about 15% year on year and it placed 546 da Vinci systems, 315 of them da Vinci 529; the installed base exceeds 13,200 systems per an earnings-call summary30.
  • da Vinci 5: about 1,200 systems installed and about 270,000 procedures by early 2026; US utilization is 11% higher than the prior-generation Xi31.
  • Autonomy features: FDA 510(k) clearances in the quarter for surgeon-initiated tool eject and multi-arm targeting, described as adding efficiency and surgeon autonomy32; about 8% of da Vinci 5 procedures use force-feedback instruments30.
  • Data route: capture structured surgical data first, then analyze it into insights (Case Insights), then deliver actionable guidance; AI is expected to inform workflow optimization, performance modeling and intraoperative guidance33.
Judgment: Intuitive’s AI route is data first and autonomy added feature by feature, not autonomous surgery in one step. See Chapter 17.

“—” means no corresponding public information was found within this draft’s search, not that none exists.

5. Five Observations (This Book’s Judgment)

Judgment
NVIDIA is the common compute partner on the device side
Medtronic, Philips and Siemens Healthineers all build public AI collaborations on NVIDIA platforms2,23,15, much as Lilly and Roche do on the pharma side.
Judgment
Imaging competes on authorization count
The radiology AI authorization ranking is concentrated in a few firms, partly through acquisitions1. Readers should separate count from real-world value.
Judgment
Robotics has become a multi-player race
Hugo (December 2025) and Ottava (July 2026) were cleared in succession and now coexist with Intuitive’s da Vinci13. AI appears as surgical-data platforms and incremental autonomy features.
Judgment
Data platforms are the new moat narrative
Touch Surgery, Polyphonic, Case Insights and Incada all aim to turn device-generated data into recurring software value.
Judgment
Devices and pharma meet at the hospital
BD’s medication management and GE HealthCare’s pharmaceutical diagnostics make device companies neighbors to pharma’s data and supply chain; Chapter 15 returns to this.

Supplement: Chinese and Japanese Device Makers (November 2026)

CompanyAI moves found in public informationBasis
United Imaging (China)By the end of 2025 it had launched more than 150 products, 58 of them FDA 510(k)-cleared, with more than 20 more cleared as AI-enabled devices; a multi-year research agreement with Washington University School of Medicine uses its AI-enhanced MRI for cardiovascular research34Press-release text retold secondhand; the FDA list was not checked
Mindray (China)The Qi Yuan large-model family (critical care, perioperative, clinical engineering and more); Q1 2026 R&D of 889 million yuan, 10.64% of revenue; a ten-year intelli-digitalization strategy presented at JPM 2026; full-stack AI in the Nuewa A20 obstetrics ultrasound35Trade-media retelling; company account
Fujifilm, Canon, Olympus (Japan)Fujifilm launched Japan’s first whole-body CT built on NVIDIA Blackwell using diffusion-based deep-learning reconstruction, runs the AI-augmented screening centers NURA, and co-developed a surgical AI application with NVIDIA; Canon launched Japan’s first NVIDIA-accelerated photon-counting CT; Olympus demonstrated cloud-connected endoscopes running AI applications in real time with NVIDIA and NTT36,37NVIDIA and trade media retelling; no independent effect evaluation seen
An independent piece of evidence (August 2026, PLOS Digital Health). The analysis covers all 1,357 AI/ML devices that the FDA cleared or approved through 5 December 2025 and finds that only 3 have been tested on patient outcomes; it also cites a separate independent analysis of 950 AI devices in which about 6% were associated with recalls, nearly half of them within the first year of clearance. This is consistent with this chapter’s judgment of many company claims and little independent effect evidence, and shows that FDA clearance is not proof of benefit to patients38.
Limits of this chapter. Information on Boston Scientific and Abbott is thin or mostly secondary; compute partners for J&J MedTech, Stryker and BD were not found; Medline was not researched; Chinese device makers (United Imaging, Mindray) and Japanese ones (Fujifilm, Canon, Olympus) are now outlined in the supplement above, while Terumo and others remain uncovered. Company-claimed effect figures have not been independently verified.

Part II · Industry Landscape · Chapter 6

Consultancies and Service Providers

Compare how the large consultancies approach AI, and map the service-provider ecosystem around pharma: life-sciences services firms, AI-native companies, and technology and frontier-model firms.

Source: Ch. 1 (industry vendors and suppliers)
Evidence grade of this chapter■■■□□□□□□□Primary 27% (17/63) · secondary 73%WeakerPrimary = papers, regulatory documents and company announcements; a company announcement is a primary source but not independent evidence of effect.
Trend signals · as of 2026-11-18
  • Model firms contract directly with drug makers, pushing consultancies toward integrator, assurer and transformer roles (a hypothesis lacking independent evidence).
  • The consulting industry is itself under AI pressure: several sources report falling headcount at McKinsey and job cuts in KPMG’s advisory practice.
  • Conflicts of interest are rising as consultancies act both as model firms’ channels and as drug makers’ “neutral” evaluators.
Numbers that go stale (dates, funding, valuations) are in Appendix F; changes of judgment between versions are in Appendix E. The above distills and judges this chapter’s content.
In one sentence. The providers around pharma are re-layering: consultancies are moving from advising to building and running platforms, frontier-model firms contract directly with drug makers (BMS with Anthropic, Sanofi with OpenAI), and life-sciences services firms such as IQVIA package industry data and agents into platforms. Drug makers gain three options and also face data-rights and vendor lock-in questions1,2,3,4.
Interest disclosure. This book was drafted with Claude, developed by Anthropic, and includes items about Deloitte’s collaboration with Anthropic and BMS’s agreement with Anthropic. These items rely only on public material and are held to the same standard as other consultancies and model firms; readers affiliated with any company named should keep possible bias in mind.

How to read the table (judgment). First, consultancies define “AI revenue” differently (generative AI, AI services, AI plus technology combined), so figures cannot be compared or added across firms. Second, consultancies are simultaneously users, sellers and possible casualties of AI, so their public narratives involve self-positioning. Third, the Deloitte and McKinsey figures are retold by secondary sources; go back to each firm’s annual report before relying on them.

5. How the Landscape Is Shifting (This Book’s Judgment)

Judgment
Consultancies: from advice to build and run
BCG says BCG X deploys platforms into client systems; McKinsey runs agents by the tens of thousands internally; Accenture takes on scaled delivery through platforms such as AI Refinery4,9. The offer is moving from reports to software and operations.
Judgment
Model firms: contracting directly with drug makers
The BMS–Anthropic and Sanofi–OpenAI agreements bypass the traditional integrator path1,2. Consultancies increasingly become channels, as with Deloitte’s certification and center-of-excellence arrangement with Anthropic11.
Judgment
Specialist services: holding position with data and agents
IQVIA packages industry data and agents into a platform, while Thermo Fisher and Illumina align with NVIDIA through lab and sequencing infrastructure3,18,20.
Judgment
AI-native firms: selling platform access, not drugs
Isomorphic, Boltz, Iambic and Noetik contract as research collaborations with milestones, while drug makers handle experimental validation and clinical development23,27,29,30.

The consulting industry is itself under AI pressure: several sources report a falling headcount at McKinsey and job cuts in KPMG’s advisory practice, while Accenture and BCG grow on AI work9,7,6. For drug makers it suggests part of the “buy consulting” budget is moving to “buy platforms.”

6. When Model Firms Contract Directly: The Consultancies’ New Role (This Book’s Judgment)

Direct contracting is no longer hypothetical: BMS signed an enterprise agreement with Anthropic, Sanofi works with OpenAI, Pfizer with Boltz and Chai, Lilly built a joint lab with NVIDIA, and Roche partnered with Chai in November 20261,2,27,32,34. Frontier-model firms also place their own scientists inside drug makers’ programs and buy biology teams35,36,37. Given that, consultancies’ position has not disappeared, but it must move from advising drug makers to joining several vendors, the drug maker’s own processes and regulatory requirements, and being accountable for the result.

First, What Has Been Taken or Squeezed

Former consulting workWho does it nowBasis and judgment
Technology selection and basic integration (connecting models, building platforms)Model and cloud firms deliver enterprise deployment directly; drug makers also build (for example Novo Nordisk built NovoScribe with AWS, MongoDB and Anthropic)1,38Squeezed. Enterprise agreements bypass the traditional integrator path
Scientific co-development and prototypesModel firms embed scientists in joint projects with drug makers and biotechs, and buy biology teams to fill gaps35,36,37Squeezed. Model firms pay dearly for domain capability, which shows such capability is scarce
Generic AI strategy reports and staff trainingDrug makers’ internal teams and model firms’ customer-success teamsSqueezed (this book’s judgment). Generic advice struggles to compete with those who hold the models and data directly
Joint builds for industry processes (such as a clinical study report platform)Consultancy and drug maker build together: the McKinsey–Merck platform5Still room. On condition that industry processes, compliance and models are joined up

Six Roles Left to Consultancies

Judgment
1. The last mile: process redesign and change management
IQVIA’s experience is that before deploying agents for MLR one must curate reference libraries, build claims libraries, adapt governance and roles and manage change; the biggest barrier is not the AI but organizational readiness39. Accenture’s research says digital pilots mostly stay in low-risk areas, with only 35% of surveyed executives calling their firms connected40; in the medical affairs survey validation and accuracy were the top barrier41. This is the most natural position for consultancies.
Judgment
2. Governance, validation and independent assurance
The FDA draft asks for credibility per context of use, and the draft EU Annex 22 asks for an AI inventory and qualified, named people42,43; the UK PMCPA stresses that responsibility does not shift39. Drug makers need help building an AI inventory, a context-of-use register and validation packages, and providing independent assurance to boards and inspectors.
Judgment
3. Multi-vendor integration and neutral evaluation
BMS describes a multi-vendor strategy and Novartis uses several discovery platforms at once44,45; Beroe’s analysis shows suppliers consolidating and clinical development as the next sourcing frontier46. Drug makers need someone to design benchmarks, compare options, and write portability and exit terms into contracts.
Judgment
4. Data-rights and deal-structure advisory
Data ownership was a central theme at JPM 202647; the Pfizer–Boltz collaboration centers on Pfizer-specific models trained on Pfizer data27; the US recognizes only natural persons as inventors48. These terms decide deal quality and are the focus of diligence and negotiation (see Chapter 10).
Judgment
5. Industry-specific products and managed operations
The McKinsey–Merck clinical study report platform5, Deloitte’s Zora agent platform13 and IQVIA.ai3 turn industry processes into reusable products that the provider runs and answers for.
Judgment
6. Channel and certification: delivery partners for model firms
Deloitte’s certification and center-of-excellence arrangement with Anthropic is an example of a consultancy acting as a scaled delivery channel for a model firm11. It brings business, but is in tension with the independence needed for roles 2 and 3 (see below).

Three Tensions and Risks

  • Independence: when the same consultancy is a channel or reseller for a model firm and also provides a “neutral” evaluation for a drug maker, conflicts of interest arise. Examples include Deloitte’s collaboration with Anthropic and PwC’s reported role as OpenAI’s first reseller11,6. Mitigations: disclose the relationship, contract for independence of evaluations, and decouple fees from the vendor chosen.
  • Being bypassed: the BMS and Sanofi agreements already bypass the traditional integrator path, and model firms embedding scientists and buying teams will squeeze room for outside domain experts further1,2,36. Consultancies’ moat is the drug maker’s processes and GxP and MLR experience, not general AI capability.
  • Economic model: several sources report a falling headcount at McKinsey and job cuts in KPMG’s advisory practice, while Accenture and BCG grow on AI work9,7,6. For drug makers this means contracts should move from hours to milestones and measurable output, with consultancies bearing part of the risk for results.

For Drug Makers: Which Work Package Goes to Whom (This Book’s Judgment)

Work packageBetter suited toReason and caution
Model access and infrastructureModel firms, cloud providersDirect contracting is faster, but keep multi-vendor options and portability
Scientific co-development (targets, molecules, antibody design)Model or AI-native firms plus the drug maker’s own scientistsNeeds the model and wet-lab loop; tie milestones to clinical readouts in the contract (see Chapters 7 and 10)
Process redesign and change management (MLR, medical information, clinical documents)Consultancies and specialist services, plus the drug maker’s business ownersThe bottleneck is organization and governance, not the model39
Validation, governance and compliance assuranceInternal quality and compliance first, with an external third party for independent assessmentResponsibility does not shift; the assurer should not also be a channel for the vendor being assessed39
Vendor evaluation and contracts (data rights, model ownership)The drug maker’s legal and BD teams plus a neutral advisorTerms on data and model ownership separate deal quality better than the upfront payment (see Chapter 10)
Value measurement and portfolio managementThe drug maker’s finance and strategy teams plus consultanciesMost savings figures are targets rather than results (see Chapter 16)

The Counter-Scenario: What If Consultancies Do Not Keep These Roles

First, the weakness of this section. Much of the evidence behind the six roles above comes from consultancies and services firms themselves: IQVIA’s blog, Accenture’s research, PwC’s reading of Annex 22, ZS’s articles39,40,43,49; and the evidence on the “consultancies are bypassed” side comes from model firms’ announcements1,50. Both sides are self-reported, and this draft found no independent data from the drug-maker side, for example where drug makers’ AI budgets actually go. This section should therefore be read as a hypothesis to be tested.
ScenarioWhat would happenHow far current evidence supports it (this book’s judgment)
Internal capability absorbs most rolesDrug makers appoint chief AI officers and build platforms and teams, leaving outside consultants peak demand and auditPartly supported: AstraZeneca bought Modella, Lilly built compute, Novo Nordisk built NovoScribe in-house51,52,38
Model firms take the last mile tooModel firms embed scientists, run joint projects and buy teams, taking on process redesign and validation directlyPartly supported: Anthropic’s deep work with six organizations and its Coefficient Bio acquisition, and OpenAI’s Orchestra Bio acquisition50,36,37; but mostly top customers and preclinical projects
Industry software platforms absorb process redesignIQVIA.ai and Veeva agents turn processes into products, so process redesign becomes software configurationPartly supported: IQVIA.ai and Veeva agents are released3,53; effect evidence comes from vendors
Consultancies do keep these rolesOn condition that organizational and regulatory bottlenecks persist and drug makers themselves lack people who know both drug-maker processes and AILimited evidence: IQVIA and Accenture support “the bottleneck is organizational”39,40, but they are interested parties

What Observations Would Change the Judgment

IndicatorSupports “consultancies keep a place”Supports “bypassed”Could this draft observe it
Where drug makers’ AI budgets go (consultancies, model firms, internal)Consultancy share steady or risingConsultancy share fallingNo public data found
Form of consulting contractsMore milestone-based, risk-sharing contractsStill hours-based, or total volume fallingOnly isolated cases seen
Size and seniority of drug makers’ internal AI teamsSmall teams relying on outside helpLarge teams building in-houseOnly isolated firms seen (such as Lilly and Novo Nordisk)
Coverage of model firms’ embedded staff and joint projectsLimited to top customersExtends to mid-sized drug makersAnthropic disclosed six; coverage unknown
Who gives AI assurance in inspections and auditsIndependent third-party assurance becomes standardInternal quality units sufficeNot yet observable
In one sentence (judgment). After AI firms contract directly with drug makers, consultancies’ new role can be summed up as integrator, assurer and transformer: joining several vendors with the drug maker’s processes, giving independent assurance on compliance and risk, and driving real change in organization and process; the value proposition moves from selling advice to being accountable for results. This rests mostly on public collaboration announcements and consultancies’ own narratives, with no independent effect evaluation, so treat it as a hypothesis rather than a conclusion.

7. Operating Model: How Drug Makers Choose Providers

DimensionQuestions to askWhy it matters
Data and IPWho owns training data? What happens to models, weights and derived data after termination?Data ownership was a central theme at JPM 202647
Validation and regulationCan the provider support context-of-use credibility assessment and audit trails?FDA and EMA principles call for risk-based management42
Measurable outputIs the contract priced on milestones rather than hours or licenses?Announcements abound while independent effect evidence is scarce (see Chapter 7)
Multi-vendor and portabilityCan several models and platforms be used at once? How costly is switching?BMS describes a multi-vendor strategy; Novartis uses several discovery platforms at once44,45
Internal capabilityWho inside the drug maker owns model validation, data governance and vendor management?AstraZeneca internalized through acquisition and Lilly built compute, showing that keeping capability in-house is also a choice51,52

Effect on this chapter’s judgment (this book’s judgment): frontier-model firms are no longer just supplying models but entering drug development directly through acquisitions and joint programs; drug makers choosing among consultancies, integrators and model vendors therefore face three kinds of players moving toward one another.

Limits of this chapter. Consultancy figures are mostly secondary compilations with differing definitions; life-sciences-specific moves by PwC, EY, KPMG, Bain, IBM and Capgemini were not found; CROs and CDMOs (such as ICON, Parexel and Lonza) are not covered; for AI-native firms such as Recursion, Xaira, Generate and Chai only the 2026 moves relevant to this chapter were added, not a firm-by-firm deep dive; Chinese AI discovery companies are only outlined in Chapter 3 and the table above.

VC

Value-Chain Overview: Where AI Can Create Value

The value chain is split link by link, listing the specific places where AI can create value, labeled with one scheme: two scales (value potential and maturity of adoption) plus tags such as easiest to quantify, technically hard and most compliance-sensitive. All ratings are this book’s judgment, with the evidence in each chapter.

How to read. Value potential answers “how much if it works” and maturity answers “how far has it got,” and they often run opposite: the highest-value places (target identification, patient enrollment) are still early with the thinnest evidence, while the most mature (document drafting, imaging AI, predictive maintenance) have medium value but are the easiest to quantify. Labels are not forecasts and not assessments of any company.

Scales and Tags

Value potential (V)Meaning
●●●●● HighestDecides R&D success, or moves the largest cost and time pools
●●●●○ HighLarge effect on cost, time or revenue
●●●○○ MediumClear gains in efficiency or quality
●●○○○ LowerLocal efficiency gains
●○○○○ LowMarginal improvement
Maturity of adoption (M)Meaning
●●●●● At scaleDeployed at scale by several firms, or regulator-recognized with public descriptions
●●●●○ DeployingReleased or commercial with several customers; effect evidence mostly from vendors or claims
●●●○○ Pilots and early deploymentDeployed at some firms, or recognized in a narrow setting
●●○○○ EarlyPlatforming, research prototypes or small pilots
●○○○○ ExploratoryRoadmaps, research exploration, or regulators still evaluating
TagRule
Highest valueValue potential rated 5: if it works, the largest effect on R&D success, time or the biggest cost pool
Most matureMaturity ≥ 4: deployed at several firms or recognized by regulators, with public descriptions
Easiest to quantifyOutput can be measured directly in time, cost or counts
EarlyMaturity ≤ 2: platforming, research prototype, small pilot or roadmap stage
Technically hardNeeds new scientific or engineering breakthroughs, or extreme data quality and validation
Most compliance-sensitiveTouches patient safety, data integrity or statutory duties in external communication
Thinnest evidencePublic evidence is mostly announcements or claims, with little independent evaluation
Externally drivenThe outcome depends on payers, regulators or partners
Data-limitedA key data signal is unavailable, limiting what models can do
Adoption unknownMaturity rests mainly on announcements or vendor accounts, and the denominator of firms and users actually using it is not public

One Chart: Value Potential Against Maturity

Each number maps to a row of the table below. The upper-left “bet zone” has high value but is still early, so control risk with milestones and clinical readouts; the upper-right “priority zone” has high value and is fairly mature, suited to scaling and governance first.

Part III · The Value Chain · Chapter 7

AI in Drug Discovery

A cornerstone chapter: what AI does across targets, molecules and the lab loop, how far the evidence goes, and what a drug maker must put in place.

Source: Ch. 4 (Discovery: importance / a closer look / operational model)
Evidence grade of this chapter■■■□□□□□□□Primary 30% (11/37) · secondary 70%MediumPrimary = papers, regulatory documents and company announcements; a company announcement is a primary source but not independent evidence of effect.
Trend signals · as of 2026-11-18
  • AI has changed the speed and scale of candidate output; there is no evidence it has changed whether the target holds.
  • Open and closed models run in parallel (Boltz-2, IsoDDE, Chai-2), and whose numbers can be trusted becomes the question.
  • China is becoming a major pole for platform companies and assets (Insilico listed in Hong Kong, Mprosevir), though asset-share figures are secondary.
Numbers that go stale (dates, funding, valuations) are in Appendix F; changes of judgment between versions are in Appendix E. The above distills and judges this chapter’s content.
In one sentence. AI drug discovery has moved from “can it design molecules” to “can it succeed in patients.” The two assets nearest the finish line differ in kind: rentosertib has entered Phase III (first patient dosed in September 2026)1,23, while zasocitinib was designed mainly with physics-based simulation and awaits an FDA decision in Q1 20274,5.
Primary-source check (November 2026). The Phase I “80–90%” and Phase II “about 40% (limited sample)” were verified in the original paper’s abstract and highlights; all authors are from Boston Consulting Group, and the analysis covers molecules of AI-native biotechs (including those partnered with drug makers, excluding COVID molecules); the specific counts “21/24” and “4/10” do not appear in the abstract and come from secondary retellings, so this book no longer treats them as verified numbers31. Rentosertib’s Phase IIa figures were verified in the Nature Medicine abstract, but the confidence intervals are wide30.

1. What AI Changes

The traditional path is to propose a target hypothesis, screen huge compound sets, and synthesize and test repeatedly; cost concentrates in trial and error and late-stage failure. Most development failures occur at Phase II9. AI changes the path in three places:

Judgment
Target hypotheses become data-driven
Multi-omics, network analysis, literature and patent data are combined to rank targets. Insilico used PandaOmics to propose TNIK as a fibrosis target1.
Judgment
Molecule design shifts from screening to generating
Generative chemistry proposes and optimizes molecules directly (Chemistry42 for rentosertib)1; protein and peptide binders can be model-generated (BoltzGen)13.
Judgment
Binding prediction no longer relies on physics alone
The Boltz-2 paper says it approaches FEP accuracy on public benchmarks18; Isomorphic says IsoDDE matches physics-based methods on affinity11. The evidence here rests mainly on preprints and company technical reports.

2. Representative Use Cases

StageWhat AI doesExampleEvidence level (this book’s judgment)
Target identification and validationCombines multi-omics, networks, literature and patents to rank targetsInsilico: PandaOmics proposes TNIK1One case in Phase III
Small-molecule designGenerative chemistry, property prediction, physics-based free-energy calculationRentosertib (generative chemistry)1; zasocitinib (FEP+ simulation)5Assets in the clinic
Structure and affinity predictionPredicts complex structure, binding strength and cryptic pocketsIsoDDE (Feb 2026) after AlphaFold 3, and open-source Boltz-211,18Benchmarks and technical reports; little peer review or prospective validation
Biologics and antibody designPredicts antibody-antigen interfaces, generates bindersBoltzGen; Lilly’s Chai deal worth up to $1.3 billion14Mostly preclinical work and partnership announcements
Lab loopsModels propose experiments, automated labs run them, data flows back to trainingThe Lilly–NVIDIA joint lab15Announcement and planning stage

3. Three Cases

Case 1: Rentosertib (Insilico, idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis)
  • Target and molecule both came from Insilico’s Pharma.AI platform: PandaOmics proposed the target TNIK, and Chemistry42 generated and optimized the molecule1
  • Phase IIa (GENESIS-IPF): 71 patients, 22 sites in China, 12 weeks; the 60 mg once-daily arm showed mean FVC improvement of 98.4 mL (95% CI 10.9 to 185.9) versus a 20.3 mL decline on placebo (95% CI −116.1 to 75.6), with widely overlapping intervals; per a secondary overview the trial’s main purpose was safety and tolerability, with lung function an exploratory endpoint2,30
  • Phase IIa results were published in Nature Medicine in 20251
  • Phase III (GENESIS-IPF-3): the start was announced in July 2026 and the first patient was dosed on 9–10 September; expected enrolment of 320 patients at 47 sites in China, once-daily dosing for 52 weeks, primary endpoint annual rate of FVC decline2,23,24
  • Still investigational and not approved by any regulator; a lead investigator estimates three to four years from Phase III initiation to possible approval under favorable conditions; China’s CDE granted Breakthrough Therapy Designation in May 2025 and the US FDA granted Orphan Drug Designation in February 20232,24,25
What it shows
AI can contribute at both the target and molecule ends and carry a program into Phase III.
What it does not show
Efficacy. Phase IIa was 12 weeks and 71 people; the Phase III design is itself a conventional randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study3, which shows AI results are still validated by conventional trials.
Case 2: Zasocitinib (Takeda, plaque psoriasis)
  • Discovered by Nimbus Therapeutics with Schrödinger, now developed by Takeda5,6
  • Design relied on structure-based methods built around physics-based FEP+ free-energy calculations5,6; some articles describe it as “AI-assisted” computationally guided design19
  • Phase III LATITUDE PsO 3001: sPGA 0/1 at week 16 in 71% on zasocitinib versus 11% on placebo and 32% on apremilast7
  • The FDA accepted the NDA under Priority Review (14 September 2026) with a PDUFA target action date in Q1 2027; the EMA has also accepted the marketing authorization application4
What it shows
Computation-driven design can yield an asset close to market.
What it does not show
Direct evidence for “generative AI drugs,” since the core method is physics-based simulation. Whether it counts as an “AI drug” depends on definition, one reason counts differ across trackers.
Case 3: IsoDDE and the Open Ecosystem
  • On 10 February 2026 Isomorphic Labs published the IsoDDE technical report, combining protein-ligand structure prediction, binding-affinity estimation, cryptic-pocket detection and antibody-antigen interface prediction11,17
  • The company says accuracy on hard, novel-structure tests is more than double AlphaFold 3’s; it is not peer reviewed, and code and methods are not public11,12
  • Isomorphic has reportedly signed collaborations with Novartis and Lilly worth nearly $3 billion in potential value, and Hassabis said the first AI-designed drugs should reach the clinic around the end of 202612; in January 2026 it added a cross-modality collaboration with J&J’s Janssen covering small molecules and biologics20
  • On the open side, Boltz-2 (June 2025) predicts structure and affinity together18; Pfizer and Boltz announced a collaboration on 8 January 2026 to train Pfizer-specific models on its historical data atop open models13
What it shows
The contest among structure models has moved to binding strength and design, and drug makers are betting on both closed and open routes.
What it does not show
That these predictions improve clinical outcomes. Validation mostly stops at benchmarks, with limited prospective evidence.

4. The Evidence: Phase I Versus Phase II

The most-cited analysis is a 2024 paper by BCG researchers in Drug Discovery Today: AI-discovered molecules showed Phase I success of 80–90% (21/24) against a historic 40–65%, while Phase II success was about 40% (4/10), in line with history8.

The usual reading is that Phase I mainly tests safety and pharmacokinetics, reflecting whether a molecule is drug-like, which is what models do well; Phase II tests whether the target biology holds in patients, where models have not yet shown an edge9,10. This is an interpretation, not a settled conclusion.

The samples are too small to conclude in either direction. With 24 and 10 molecules the confidence intervals are wide, and in the ASCO 2026 analysis only 8 AI-enabled assets had completed Phase II8. This chapter does not use the figures to judge success or failure.

5. Operating Model

Drug makers have five common ways to engage with AI discovery, each with different trade-offs:

ModelExampleTrade-off
Own compute and data, with a top technology partnerLilly: AI factory plus NVIDIA joint lab15Strongest control; heavy investment (up to $1 billion over five years for the lab) and a need for sustained experimental data production
Open base plus private fine-tuningPfizer × Boltz13Avoids single-vendor lock-in and keeps base capability inspectable; differentiation must come from own data and workflows
Partner with a closed platform companyIsomorphic × Novartis, Lilly and J&J (partly reported)12,20Access to frontier capability, but methods are opaque and depend on the partner’s iteration
Biologics design partnershipLilly × Chai Discovery, up to $1.3 billion14Milestone-based payment shifts part of the technical risk
AI-native in-house pipelineInsilico: rentosertib1End-to-end control, but bears the full clinical-development risk

Organization and Capabilities

  • Data assets: structure historical experimental data and keep it trainable; Lilly’s approach is to have labs continuously produce large-scale ground-truth data15.
  • Team shape: put biologists, chemists and model engineers together; the Lilly–NVIDIA joint lab co-locates the two groups15.
  • Evaluation: validate models with prospective experiments, not only public benchmarks; benchmark results do not capture all of real drug discovery18.
  • Governance boundary (judgment): the FDA’s 2025 draft guidance does not cover AI used solely for discovery16, but once the resulting data and conclusions support a submission they come into regulatory view; confirm the exact boundary with regulatory affairs.

6. Open Questions and a Watchlist

  • What is Phase II success really? This is the key test of the value of AI discovery, but three analyses now give figures from 19.5% to about 55%, see Section 8.
  • The gap between benchmarks and prospective validation: IsoDDE’s lead figures come from company reports and need independent verification.
  • Data moats: differences increasingly come from who owns high-quality private data rather than whose algorithm is newer.
  • Definition: there is no consensus on what an “AI drug” is, so counts are not directly comparable.
EventTimingNote
FDA decision on zasocitinibQ1 2027 (PDUFA target date)4If approved, the first approval of a drug designed on a physics-plus-machine-learning platform; it is not the first computationally designed drug (see Section 9 below), and whether it counts as an “AI drug” depends on definition
Isomorphic’s first clinical trialsNo drug yet in the clinic when the $5 billion Series C was disclosed on 17 November 2026; earlier guidance was by end-202612,29Company guidance; no registration of a first trial was found in this draft
Rentosertib Phase IIIFirst dosing September 2026; 52-week treatment period; a lead investigator expects possible approval within three to four years under favorable conditions23,25Results are years away; the estimate is a trade-media retelling
Generate’s SOLAIRIA-1/2 (GB-0895, severe asthma)52-week Phase III; about 786 people in each of two trials, primary endpoint annualized asthma exacerbation rate27The Phase III of the first AI-designed antibody; timing per company disclosure

7. November 2026 Supplement: New Clinical and Capital Signals

After this chapter was first drafted, several signals appeared that change “how far from a first approval.” They differ in nature, and the table notes the evidence and basis for each.

Asset or eventContentEvidence and basis
Mprosevir (Westlake, anti-COVID)Conditionally approved by China’s NMPA in July 2026 (Westlake announced it in early September; a reagent supplier gives 29 July, not checked against NMPA text), called by the developers China’s first “AI-assisted” Class 1 innovative drug and the first small molecule approved from DNA-encoded-library technology; from a library of about 49 billion compounds with more than 100 molecules picked first, AI models helped set screening conditions and control compounds and closed the data loop (English media add that AI narrowed candidates to nine, not found in the Chinese first-hand relays); about three and a half years from candidate discovery to completing clinical trials21,22Developers’ and state media’s account; conditional approval; the target was known and AI did not propose it; the degree of “AI assistance” is not independently assessed; commentary says the COVID target is far simpler than oncology targets and calls it a technical validation
GB-0895 (Generate, severe asthma)A long-acting anti-TSLP antibody designed for injection once every six months; two Phase III trials announced in December 2025; Nasdaq listing (GENB) on 27 February 2026 raising $400 million, with about $300 million earmarked to complete the two Phase III trials26,27Company disclosures and financial media; the first AI-designed antibody in Phase III, with no Phase III data yet
REC-4881 (Recursion, familial adenomatous polyposis)Phase 1b/2 TUPELO: after 12 weeks 75% of evaluable patients had a smaller polyp burden, a median reduction of 43%; 12 weeks after stopping, 9 of 11 evaluable patients were still below baseline28Company disclosure via a secondary overview; small sample; the same company dropped REC-994 in 2025 when early benefit did not last
Isomorphic Labs$2.1 billion raised in May 2026 and a further $5 billion Series C announced on 17 November; Bloomberg earlier reported a valuation of about $50 billion, which the company announcement did not disclose; still no drug in the clinic28,29Funding per company announcement; valuation per media; unconfirmed

Effect on this chapter’s judgments (this book’s judgment): first, “no AI-originated drug has been approved” still holds, but must add “a drug with AI involvement has received conditional approval”; second, an AI-designed antibody entering Phase III shows de novo design is no longer only a small-molecule story; third, capital concentration keeps rising while clinical readouts remain scarce, and the gap is widening.

8. Why One Question Gets Several Numbers: Phase II Success

This chapter has so far used BCG’s 2024 finding of about 40% for Phase II, from a limited sample and in line with history. Two analyses with different definitions appeared in 2026, with very different numbers, and must be read side by side:

AnalysisSample and definitionPhase IPhase IIHow to read it
BCG (Drug Discovery Today, 2024)Molecules of AI-native biotechs, including those partnered with drug makers and excluding COVID molecules; a mixed bag (AI-repurposed molecules, vaccines, antibodies, AI-discovered targets, AI-designed small molecules); per secondary retellings, of 24 molecules completing Phase I by December 2023, 21 succeeded, and of 10 completing Phase II about 4 succeeded31,3480–90%About 40%The authors note a limited sample; per secondary retellings, four of six discontinued cases cited business reasons
Haque et al. (Cancer Treatment Reviews, online 8 October 2026)129 AI-enabled assets across 69 companies that had entered at least one interventional human trial by 1 March 2026; 72 completed Phase I, 24 completed Phase II, only 5 entered or completed Phase III; oncology 57.3%, small molecules 79.8%3277.8%19.5%A cross-sectional descriptive analysis reporting “observed probability of success”; defined differently from BCG
Oliver Wyman (October 2026)AI-native biotechs as a proxy; 46 Phase II programs, about half still active; compared with BIO’s industry benchmark (29% for Phase II)3374% (progression) / 81% (completion)45% (progression) / 55% (completion)Limited sample; success counted two ways, progression to the next phase and phase completion

The differences between the three numbers come mainly from (this book’s judgment): which assets are included (only AI-native firms or anything with AI involvement; whether repurposed molecules, antibodies and vaccines count); how “success” is defined (progression, completion, or not terminated); whether projects terminated for business reasons count as failures; whether ongoing trials are excluded; the data cutoff; and very small samples. One therefore cannot say Phase II success has fallen or risen; they measure different things. The only safe conclusion is that Phase I success is high in every analysis while Phase II is unresolved, ranging from 19.5% to about 55%, all from small samples.

A deeper problem: stopping does not mean the target was wrong. Even with larger samples, “Phase II results” cannot answer whether AI picks the right targets: projects that stop after Phase II may do so for commercial priorities, clinical operations, funding or strategy rather than because the target fails biologically. Per secondary retellings, four of the six discontinued cases in BCG’s sample cited business reasons34. So this book’s earlier line that Phase II tests target biology is a reasonable inference but not the analyses’ own conclusion; the cleaner approach is to count stops for efficacy or safety separately from stops for other reasons, which public data cannot yet do.

9. Compared With Thirty Years Ago, Which Step Has AI Actually Changed? (This Book’s Judgment)

Computer-assisted design is not new. The 1993 Nature paper on zanamivir called it an example of rational, computer-assisted drug design, designing inhibitors from the crystal structure of influenza sialidase35; zanamivir and oseltamivir were both approved in 1999, with zanamivir’s FDA approval in July 199936,37. So if zasocitinib is approved it will not be the first computationally designed drug; at most it will be the first drug designed on a physics-plus-machine-learning platform. An earlier line in this book called it the nearest approval of a computationally designed drug, which was too broad and has been corrected.

StepThirty years agoWhat AI changes today (this book’s judgment)Evidence
Finding targetsLiterature, genetics and structural biologyMulti-omics and literature integration for ranking, probably faster; but whether the target holds in patients is still answered by clinical trialsWeak: cases and company claims
Designing moleculesStructure-based docking and iteration by chemistsGenerative chemistry, co-folding and affinity prediction; the change in search space and speed is clearest (see Chapter 3)Medium
Antibodies and proteinsImmunization and library screeningDe novo design; hit-rate figures come from company preprintsMedium to weak
Experimental iterationManual cyclesDry-wet loops and autonomous labsEarly

The safest statement today is that AI has mainly changed the scale and speed of search, and there is no evidence that it has changed whether the target holds, the hardest step. This is the yardstick for the rest of this chapter’s judgments.

Part III · The Value Chain · Chapter 8

Drug Development

How AI changes clinical development, from trial design and patient matching to clinical study reports and regulatory documents: what has reached scale and what is still gathering evidence.

Source: Ch. 5 (Drug Development: importance / a closer look / operational model)
Evidence grade of this chapter■■■■■■□□□□Primary 62% (13/21) · secondary 38%StrongerPrimary = papers, regulatory documents and company announcements; a company announcement is a primary source but not independent evidence of effect.
Trend signals · as of 2026-11-18
  • The most mature use is document automation, while the highest-value ones (patient enrollment, trial design) are still early.
  • The regulatory framework is taking shape but mostly as drafts and principles: the FDA’s January 2025 draft and the FDA–EMA ten principles of January 2026.
  • Vendors’ efficiency figures use inconsistent baselines, with no independent quality evaluation.
Numbers that go stale (dates, funding, valuations) are in Appendix F; changes of judgment between versions are in Appendix E. The above distills and judges this chapter’s content.
In one sentence. The firmest footing in clinical development is document automation: Novo Nordisk and Merck have both described sharply shortening the drafting of clinical study reports. On the design side, digital-twin covariate adjustment has been qualified by the EMA for specific settings, while patient matching and synthetic controls are still mostly at research or exploratory stage. Regulators ask that AI’s credibility be shown per context of use1,2,3,4,5.

1. What AI Changes

At JPM 2025 NVIDIA said clinical trials are the longest and costliest workflow in drug and device development, which is why it started there6. The traditional pain points cluster in two places: enrollment, where an NIH study from 2023 found that as many as 85% of trials fail to recruit enough patients for the required sample size7; and documents, since clinical study reports, protocols and submissions take heavy manual writing and several review rounds. AI enters at five points:

Judgment
Design and statistics
Patient-level disease-progression predictions (digital twins) are used for covariate adjustment to cut required sample size while holding statistical power3,8.
Judgment
Patient matching and enrollment
Large language models compare a patient record against trial criteria one by one, shortening screening time4.
Judgment
Documents and submissions
Structured authoring plus generative AI drafts clinical study reports, patient safety narratives and regulatory submission material9,2,10.
Judgment
Trial operations
Agents connect to trial data and operations systems (such as Medidata and ClinicalTrials.gov) for protocol drafting, operational monitoring and regulatory research11,12.
Judgment
Synthetic and external controls
Historical data are used to construct control comparators to reduce or replace some control enrollment; regulators are still evaluating fully synthetic controls13.

2. Representative Use Cases and Evidence Level

StageExampleEvidenceEvidence level (this book’s judgment)
Clinical study report draftingNovo Nordisk’s NovoScribe; the Merck–McKinsey platform9,2Company and vendor case studies, with drafting cut from weeks to minutes or days; human review retainedAt scale (company claims)
Covariate adjustment (digital twins)Unlearn’s method3The company says the EMA qualified its method for Phase 2 and 3 randomized trials with continuous outcomes, and the FDA gave positive feedback on covariate-adjusted analysesNarrow regulator-recognized setting (company claim)
Patient-to-trial matchingNIH’s TrialGPT4Peer reviewed: 87.3% criterion-level accuracy, close to experts’ 88.7%–90%; screening time cut 42.6% in a pilot user study4,7Research prototype with a published evaluation
Synthetic and external controlsSeveral companies and academic workRegulators are still evaluating fully synthetic controls; other work notes that augmenting trials with synthetic data gives no guarantee of valid statistical inference13,14Exploratory
Trial operations and protocol draftingClaude’s Medidata connector; IQVIA.ai clinical agents11,12Product and platform announcements; limited independent evidence of effectAnnouncements and early deployment

3. Three Cases

Case 1: Novo Nordisk’s NovoScribe (clinical study reports)
  • Background: per the AWS case, drafting a clinical study report used to take up to 15 weeks and coordinate 40–50 professionals1; the MongoDB case says about 12 weeks by a team of statisticians, scientists and technical authors9.
  • Method: collect the trial’s statistical outputs, combine them with expert-approved text snippets, and generate a draft from a template; every sentence is traceable to its source, and people review and approve9,1.
  • Result (vendor cases): time to produce the key documents cut by more than 90%, completed in minutes by a team of three, a headline in both the AWS and MongoDB stories1,9.
  • Scale: the MongoDB case says NovoScribe was then compiling about 30% of the company’s clinical study reports, expected to exceed 90% by year-end9.
  • Novo’s own staff: in Anthropic’s customer story Novo’s digitalization strategy director says Claude helped cut clinical study report writing time by 90%; internal writers previously averaged only 2.3 reports per year; the resources for device verification protocols fell by 95% (per Anthropic’s retelling)15. Danish press retelling Novo’s Skov: early technology is error-prone and correcting AI mistakes can take longer than rewriting; the company does not plan to cut writers for now but to reduce new hiring16.
What it shows
Structured authoring plus an LLM can be used at scale in a regulated document, with human review and traceability as preconditions.
What it does not show
It does not show that “12 weeks to 10 minutes” transfers directly: channels give different baselines (15 weeks, 12 weeks, “over 10 weeks”), every figure comes from a vendor or a vendor-relayed case, even the Novo staff’s “cut by 90%” is quoted inside a vendor’s story; “minutes” refers to drafting, not review and finalization, and there is no independent quality evaluation.
Case 2: Digital Twins and Covariate Adjustment (Unlearn)
  • Method: machine-learning models trained on historical data generate an individual disease-progression prediction for each participant (a digital twin), used for covariate adjustment in randomized trials3,8.
  • Regulation: the company says the method is qualified by the EMA for Phase 2 and 3 randomized trials with continuous outcomes, and the FDA gave positive feedback on its use for covariate-adjusted analyses3.
  • Extension: company authors also discuss using digital twins as synthetic controls in single-arm trials, with deployment points framed by the FDA’s AI draft guidance, and reanalyze ALS and Huntington’s data8.
What it shows
A narrow, well-defined statistical use can win regulatory recognition first: covariate adjustment inside a randomized trial, without replacing the control.
What it does not show
It does not show that synthetic controls are widely accepted. Regulators are still evaluating fully synthetic controls, and the regulatory statements above are the company’s own.
Case 3: TrialGPT (Patient-to-Trial Matching)
  • Method: an end-to-end framework that first retrieves candidate trials at scale, then judges criteria one by one, then gives trial-level scores and rankings4.
  • Accuracy: on 1,015 patient-criterion pairs annotated by three physicians, criterion-level accuracy was 87.3%, close to experts’ 88.7%–90%4,7.
  • Efficiency: in a user study modeled on NCI’s task, screening time fell from about 61.5 seconds to about 31.3 seconds, a 42.6% reduction4.
  • Context: a 2023 NIH study says as many as 85% of trials fail to recruit enough patients7.
What it shows
LLMs can save time at the screening step with close-to-expert accuracy.
What it does not show
It does not show faster enrollment or higher trial success. The study measures screening time per patient–trial pair, not the enrollment timeline of a whole trial.

4. Regulation and Governance

  • FDA’s January 2025 draft guidance: proposes a seven-step, risk-based credibility framework requiring a defined context of use, a credibility assessment plan and report, and lifecycle maintenance; it covers nonclinical, clinical, postmarketing and manufacturing; checked against the original text, it does not address drug discovery or operational efficiencies that do not affect patient safety, drug quality or the reliability of nonclinical or clinical study results, and the text names “drafting/writing a regulatory submission” as an example of such a use, so document drafting mostly falls outside the draft’s scope while still subject to GxP and the company’s quality system17,5.
  • FDA and EMA joint principles, January 2026: ten principles (human-centric design, risk-based approach, adherence to standards, clear context of use, multidisciplinary expertise, data governance and documentation, model design and development practices, risk-based performance assessment, life-cycle management, clear essential information), checked against the FDA and EMA texts; they are principles rather than formal guidance18,19.
  • Implication (judgment): document-drafting uses usually have human review as a backstop and are easier to justify; uses that directly affect enrollment, dosing or endpoint assessment need stricter validation. Confirm exact boundaries with regulatory affairs.

5. Operating Model

ElementPracticeBasis
Content and dataStructure expert-approved text, templates and statistical outputs so every sentence traces to its sourceNovoScribe’s approach9,1
Role of peopleQualified medical writers oversee; AI drafts and people review and approveMSD’s platform design2
Platform and vendorsThree shapes coexist: build (NovoScribe), co-build with a consultancy (MSD and McKinsey), enterprise platform (BMS and Anthropic)See Chapter 42,20
Validation and governanceDefine risk per context of use and keep audit trails; validate more strictly where patient safety or data reliability is affectedFDA draft and FDA-EMA principles5,21

6. Risks and Open Questions

  • Effect evidence is mostly vendor cases: independent, prospective evaluation is needed, especially on quality and regulator feedback, not only drafting speed.
  • Does enrollment really speed up: shorter screening time is not a shorter enrollment timeline; trial-level evidence is needed.
  • Regulatory boundaries for synthetic controls: which indications and evidence structures are acceptable is still evolving.
  • Accountability: who answers for errors in AI-drafted documents, and how contracts and quality systems allocate it.

Part III · The Value Chain · Chapter 9

Medical Affairs

How AI changes the synthesis, answering and communication of evidence: where medical affairs teams use it, how physicians and KOLs (key opinion leaders, i.e. influential specialist physicians) use it themselves, and how compliance limits adoption.

Source: Ch. 6 (Medical Affairs: importance / a closer look / operational model)
Evidence grade of this chapter■■□□□□□□□□Primary 17% (1/6) · secondary 83%WeakerPrimary = papers, regulatory documents and company announcements; a company announcement is a primary source but not independent evidence of effect.
Trend signals · as of 2026-11-18
  • Internal uses (literature monitoring, plain-language summaries) lead, while answers to physicians are the most cautious.
  • Physicians and KOLs use AI themselves, and MSL value may shift to insight beyond the label (KOLs’ own account).
  • This chapter’s data come mainly from small, vendor-run surveys.
Numbers that go stale (dates, funding, valuations) are in Appendix F; changes of judgment between versions are in Appendix E. The above distills and judges this chapter’s content.
In one sentence. Medical affairs is among the functions with the highest confidence in AI and the most cautious adoption: in surveys most practitioners believe AI will raise efficiency, yet the most deployed uses are still internal ones such as literature monitoring and plain-language summaries, and deployment is lowest where compliance scrutiny is highest. At the other end, physicians and KOLs (key opinion leaders) already use AI to synthesize literature, which changes how medical teams are needed1,2,3.
Evidence note. The data in this chapter come mainly from small surveys run by vendors or trade bodies (for example 79 responses, or 75 KOLs) and indicate leanings, not the whole industry. No company-level effect data for medical affairs was found in this draft.
Terms. KOL (Key Opinion Leader): a senior expert or physician in a disease area whose views shape peers’ treatment and prescribing habits, with whom drug makers’ medical teams exchange scientific views over the long term. MSL (Medical Science Liaison): a medical professional at a drug maker who holds scientific exchange with KOLs and does not sell. HCP (Healthcare Professional): physicians, pharmacists, nurses and others who deliver care to patients, of whom KOLs are the small, most influential part. MLR (Medical, Legal, Regulatory): the joint review of external materials by the medical, legal and regulatory functions.

1. What AI Changes

Medical affairs produces, organizes and communicates scientific evidence, including medical information responses, interactions between MSLs (medical science liaisons) and KOLs (key opinion leaders, influential experts in a disease area), literature and congress intelligence, and real-world evidence. The work depends on reading and summarizing large volumes of literature and feedback, which is what LLMs do well and where use began. Change happens on two sides:

Judgment
Inside the company: from reading papers to evidence intelligence
Literature monitoring and synthesis and plain-language summaries went into production first; consultancies such as ZS argue that medical insights must move from mining notes to an intelligence system that shapes decisions1,4.
Judgment
Outside: physicians and KOLs use AI themselves
Veeva’s survey of 75 KOLs shows they use AI to synthesize literature, check standards of care, learn about indications and use cases and put new evidence in context; some expect to need fewer MSL interactions and will engage only with MSLs who offer beyond-the-label insight3.

2. Representative Use Cases

Use caseWhat it doesEvidenceMaturity (this book’s judgment)
Literature monitoring and synthesisContinuously scans literature and produces structured reviews and key pointsOne of the most deployed use cases in a 2026 survey (44%) and the top area for further investment (38%)1,2In production at limited scale
Plain-language summariesRewrites scientific material into patient-readable versionsTied with literature monitoring, with 44% of respondents in production2In production
Medical information response draftsDrafts answers to physicians’ product inquiries for review before sendingListed as a future investment area in the survey1Early
MSL and KOL insight miningExtracts signals from MSL notes, advisory boards and medical information inquiriesConsultancies and vendors productize it (for example ZS’s ZAIDYN); leading firms invested in insight mining in 20254,5Being platformed; limited effect evidence
Support for MSL daily workLiterature search, data analysis, presentation prep, real-time retrieval during engagementsAn MSL society survey says 82% of medical affairs professionals report MSLs use or would use AI for these tasks6Widely tried

3. Survey Data

86%
of respondents (only 79 responses, a vendor-run survey) are confident or very confident that AI will improve medical affairs efficiency within two to three years1,2
44%
have literature monitoring in production; plain-language summaries also 44%2
82%
say MSLs use or would use AI (MSL society survey; sample size not verified here)6
#1
top barrier is validation and accuracy; the stricter the compliance scrutiny, the lower the AI deployment1

The numbers show one pattern: high confidence, little production. The surveys are small and run by vendors or trade bodies, so they indicate leanings rather than the industry as a whole.

4. Operating Model

ElementKey pointsBasis or judgment
Validation and accuracyMake validation a hard gate before launch; medical information answers and evidence communication must trace to sourcesThe top barrier in the survey1
Data inputsMSL notes, medical information inquiries, congresses and literature are the raw material; they need structure and access controlZS says insight mining must become an intelligence system4
Role of peopleAI drafts a baseline summary; medical affairs professionals contextualize, verify and finalizeA shared view in trade articles2
Outward-facing contentHCPs (healthcare professionals such as physicians, pharmacists and nurses) increasingly ask LLMs directly, so drug makers must think about how their scientific content is correctly cited by those tools (“generative engine optimization”)A secondary view; specific practices were not verified here
Adverse events and compliance (judgment)Define how adverse-event information arising in AI conversations is captured and reported; agree boundaries for off-label communication in advanceThis book’s judgment; confirm with pharmacovigilance and compliance

5. Risks and Open Questions

  • Where MSL value moves: if KOLs can synthesize literature with AI themselves, MSL differentiation may shift to beyond-the-label insight and real-world experience3. This is KOLs’ own account and needs larger samples.
  • Accuracy and hallucination: an error in an answer to a physician costs far more than an error in an internal summary.
  • Effects are hard to measure: time saved on literature monitoring is easy to count, while the quality and impact of evidence communication is not.
  • From pilot to process: high confidence but little production suggests the bottleneck is validation, compliance and workflow integration rather than the model.

Part III · The Value Chain · Chapter 10

Business Development

AI relates to business development in two ways: using AI to support asset sourcing and diligence, and valuing and structuring deals for AI assets and platforms.

Source: Ch. 7 (Business Development: importance / a closer look / operational model)
Evidence grade of this chapter■■□□□□□□□□Primary 25% (4/16) · secondary 75%WeakerPrimary = papers, regulatory documents and company announcements; a company announcement is a primary source but not independent evidence of effect.
Trend signals · as of 2026-11-18
  • The deal shape is a small upfront payment plus large milestones, with drug makers handling validation and clinical development.
  • China-origin assets are a rising share of big-pharma in-licensing (per a secondary analysis, definition unchecked).
  • AI asset valuation lacks an approved product as an anchor, and funding concentrates in the leaders.
Numbers that go stale (dates, funding, valuations) are in Appendix F; changes of judgment between versions are in Appendix E. The above distills and judges this chapter’s content.
In one sentence. Business development sits at both ends of AI. On one end, a surge of asset sources, especially licensed projects from China, strains manual sourcing and diligence and pushes teams toward AI. On the other, drug makers are buying access to AI platforms in volume and must value a platform rather than a molecule. Effect evidence for the first is scarce, while the second has plenty of public deal structures1,2,3.

1. What AI Changes

The source book describes business development as the function that acquires external assets and partnerships. AI affects it in two opposite directions:

Judgment
AI for BD: sourcing, diligence, intelligence
With ever more potential targets, teams need granular, comprehensive and current data; a January 2026 EY report details how AI tools can augment BD teams1. An RSM briefing also says drug makers use advanced analytics to find overlooked pipeline value, improve diligence and guide acquisition and partnership decisions4.
Judgment
BD for AI: buying platform access
Beroe’s analysis of 2026 pharma AI deals shows discovery still dominates, suppliers are consolidating, and clinical development is the next sourcing frontier3. What drug makers buy is often a platform and a capability rather than a single asset.

2. Why BD Needs AI: A Changing Source of Assets

40%
of assets in-licensed by big pharma in 2025 had a Chinese licensor, up by about a third from just under 30% in 20242
$45B → $105B
aggregate value of in-licensing deals involving Chinese-originated assets (excluding AI partnerships), 2024 to 20252
$15B
AstraZeneca’s January 2026 commitment to invest through 2030 in China across discovery, clinical development and more1

These numbers show both the count and the geography of targets widening beyond what in-person scouting can cover; reports say due diligence is especially critical for smaller biotechs and investors without a presence in hubs such as Shanghai, and BD teams are turning to AI to scout targets, though there remains large untapped potential for AI in asset sourcing1.

3. Representative Use Cases

StageWhat AI doesEvidenceMaturity (this book’s judgment)
Asset sourcing and screeningFinds and ranks candidates across patents, papers, trial registries and company announcementsAn EY report and trade coverage mention AI-augmented sourcing1Early; little effect evidence
Diligence and data-room analysisReads large volumes of technical, clinical and IP material and extracts key pointsAn RSM briefing says drug makers use advanced analytics to improve diligence4Early
Finding overlooked pipeline valueUses analytics to identify assets for repositioning or combinationAs above4Early
Valuing AI assets and platformsDistinguishes “platform-first” from “pipeline-first” AI biotechs to decide what to pay forA dealmaker guide contrasts the two models; internal AI groups at drug makers increasingly build or buy capability5Plenty of deal samples

5. Operating Model

ElementKey pointsBasis or judgment
Data foundationBD needs granular, comprehensive, current external data, plus data rooms and access control that support cross-border diligenceThe view in the EY report1
Diligence list for AI assets (judgment)Source and rights of training data; ownership of models and weights; rights after termination; dependence of the platform on a single program; ability to verify claimed performance independentlyThis book’s judgment, echoing the JPM 2026 data-ownership theme
Platform-first versus pipeline-firstPlatform deals buy capability and access, pipeline deals buy specific assets; valuation logic and risk differThe dealmaker guide’s contrast5
OrganizationBD and internal AI teams must cooperate: the AI team evaluates the technology, BD designs the termsInternal AI groups increasingly build or buy capability5

6. Risks and Open Questions

  • Little evidence of AI’s effect on deal outcomes: no data was found within the search showing AI sourcing or diligence improves deal success or returns.
  • Platform valuation is hard: with no approved product as an anchor for AI platforms, milestone payments mostly presume future success (see Chapter 7).
  • Data and IP disputes: the rights to data used in model training are the likeliest source of dispute in AI deals.
  • Geopolitics (judgment): geopolitical tension may affect cross-border IP and data flows and thus deal pace. Specific policies were not verified here.
