Part III · The Value Chain · Chapter 11

Marketing and Brand Management

AI makes content production fast, so the bottleneck shifts to medical, legal and regulatory (MLR) review, while regulators’ expectations of AI-generated content have not loosened.

Source: Ch. 8 (Marketing and Brand Management: importance / a closer look / operational model)
Evidence grade of this chapter■■■■■□□□□□Primary 50% (3/6) · secondary 50%StrongerPrimary = papers, regulatory documents and company announcements; a company announcement is a primary source but not independent evidence of effect.
Trend signals · as of 2026-11-18
  • Content output rises while MLR review becomes the bottleneck: the faster the drafting, the longer the queue for review may grow.
  • Regulators are also using AI to check content: the FDA issued over 200 drug advertising and promotion enforcement letters in 2025 and says it uses AI tools to monitor proactively (per pharmaphorum); the UK PMCPA gives no absolution for using AI.
  • Physicians ask LLMs for information, so drug makers must consider how their scientific content is cited correctly by AI.
Numbers that go stale (dates, funding, valuations) are in Appendix F; changes of judgment between versions are in Appendix E. The above distills and judges this chapter’s content.
In one sentence. In marketing, AI first changes the quantity of content, not its quality: generative AI speeds up drafting while pushing pressure onto MLR review. US promotional material output reportedly rose 29% year on year while 77% of approved content is rarely or never used by the field, and regulators have given AI-generated content no exemption1,2,3.
Evidence note. The figures on output, usage and enforcement come from a pharmaphorum trade commentary retelling industry benchmarks; “time saved” figures are company or vendor claims. No independent, peer-reviewed marketing-effect study was found in this draft.

1. What AI Changes

The source book describes marketing and brand management as the function around positioning, customer insight and communication. In an omnichannel setting, segmentation and personalization require many variants and formats from one core asset; AI makes that almost free on the production side, so MLR review becomes the new gate. IQVIA’s MLR business head writes that the issue is no longer content production but the ability to review and approve content in time2.

+29%
year-on-year growth in US promotional material production (2025 industry benchmark, per pharmaphorum)1
77%
of approved content is rarely or never used by field teams1
200+
enforcement letters the FDA issued on drug advertising and promotion in 2025, reportedly the highest in nearly 25 years1

2. Representative Use Cases

Use caseWhat it doesEvidenceMaturity (this book’s judgment)
Segmentation and insightFiner HCP (healthcare professional) segmentation to decide content and channelIQVIA says data and analytics are enabling increasingly sophisticated HCP segmentation2Fairly mature
Content generationProduces many variants, formats and derivatives from one core assetPfizer’s Charlie builds generative AI into marketing content workflows with review steps4Deployed
MLR-assisted reviewChecks against approved claims, verifies references and sources, flags compliance issues and inconsistencies, standardizes quality checksIQVIA describes agents supporting these steps; Veeva’s PromoMats already has AI agents2,5Early deployment
Omnichannel personalizationDynamically decides the next content and touchpoint by customer and channelVeeva says the industry is moving from a system of record to a system of insights6In progress

3. Regulation: No Exemption for AI

  • Stronger enforcement: per pharmaphorum, the FDA issued over 200 enforcement letters on drug advertising and promotion in 2025 and said explicitly that it uses AI and technology tools to surveil drug advertisements proactively1. The same technology is being used by drug makers to produce content and by regulators to inspect it.
  • Responsibility does not shift: the UK PMCPA’s position is that AI may support compliance work but does not absolve companies of their responsibilities under the ABPI Code, and the requirement of final certification by a nominated signatory cannot be met by AI alone2.
  • United States: reportedly the FDA requires manufacturers to ensure accuracy, fair balance and evidentiary support whatever drafting tool is used, while the FTC requires substantiation for endorsements and comparative claims; state AI-disclosure laws and the EU AI Act add documentation and human-oversight duties3. This is a secondary summary; check the originals before relying on it.

4. Operating Model

IQVIA’s experience is that before deploying agents for MLR the foundations must be laid: curating reference libraries, making materials AI-readable, building and maintaining claims libraries, adapting governance and roles, and managing change2. AI does well on structured, rule-based tasks, but many MLR judgments depend on context, regional differences and professional judgment, so full automation is unlikely.

ElementKey pointsBasis
Claims and reference librariesClaims linked to evidence and materials made AI-readable; references de-duplicated, metadata consistent and traceableIQVIA’s MLR practice2
Governance and accountabilityDefine which steps may be automated and which require human review; define ownership and accountability for AI-generated contentIQVIA and trade commentary2,3
Embed rather than run in parallelPut AI inside the existing MLR workflow rather than a parallel track; use pharma-specific tools to leave an audit trailA shared recommendation in trade commentary3
Phased rolloutLay data and content-governance foundations, then pilot, then scaleIQVIA’s advice2

5. Risks and Open Questions

  • A mismatch of speeds: the faster the drafting, the more congested review becomes; without redesigning MLR, AI only moves the backlog from front to back.
  • New compliance risks in generated content: traditional review was not designed to catch content that sounds plausible but lacks support1.
  • No effect evidence: time saved is easy to count, but there is no public evidence on effects on prescribing behavior or patient outcomes.
  • Field usage: with 77% of approved content rarely or never used, “more content” does not mean “more useful content”1.

  3. The Compliance Tightrope (PM360 via Pulse, 30 Mar 2026)secondary

Part III · The Value Chain · Chapter 12

Sales

How AI changes field-force work: CRM turns from a system of record into a system of insights, with agents entering pre-call preparation, call documentation and coaching.

Source: Ch. 9 (Sales: importance / a closer look / operational model)
Evidence grade of this chapter■■■■□□□□□□Primary 43% (3/7) · secondary 57%MediumPrimary = papers, regulatory documents and company announcements; a company announcement is a primary source but not independent evidence of effect.
Trend signals · as of 2026-11-18
  • CRM is moving from a system of record to embedded agents, but real adoption is not public.
  • Physicians use AI themselves, so the value proposition of field engagement is being reassessed.
  • Compliance boundaries for call records and off-label communication must be defined before agents go live.
Numbers that go stale (dates, funding, valuations) are in Appendix F; changes of judgment between versions are in Appendix E. The above distills and judges this chapter’s content.
In one sentence. AI on the sales side lands mainly as agents embedded in CRM: Veeva released the first AI agents for Vault CRM and PromoMats in December 2025, Vault CRM has more than 190 live customers, and 14 of the top 20 drug makers have committed to it. But the effect data that can be found publicly mostly comes from vendors, with little independent evidence1,2,3.
Evidence note. This chapter relies on product announcements from Veeva and Aktana and secondary summaries of them; the “productivity” and “sales-target attainment” figures are vendor cases. No independent effect study of field teams at drug makers was found, and other CRM vendors such as Salesforce are not covered.

1. What AI Changes

The source book describes sales as communication and reach between the field team and physicians. The change can be read along Veeva’s own timeline: Andi in 2019 was an assistant inside CRM offering data-based “next best action” suggestions; Vault CRM Bot and voice control were announced in November 2024; the first AI agents were released in December 20254,1. The industry narrative is that CRM is moving from a system of record to a system of insights that drives HCP (healthcare professional, mainly physician) engagement through embedded agents2,5.

3. Effect Evidence: Mostly From Vendors

CaseClaimed resultType

The cases share one trait: the metrics are mostly engagement, productivity and target attainment, for a specific product or team, with no public randomized comparison or third-party evaluation. Readers should treat them as evidence of possibility, not of average effect.

4. Operating Model

ElementKey pointsBasis or judgment
Data and systemsAI’s value depends on complete, consistent data, content and activity records in CRM; system migration (for example to Vault CRM) is groundworkVeeva says 14 of the top 20 have committed to migrate2
Compliant content sourcesContent used by agents must come from approved libraries, linked to Chapter 11’s MLR and claims librariesConsistent with Chapter 11; Veeva has put agents in PromoMats1
Role of the field (judgment)AI takes preparation and documentation so reps spend time where judgment and relationships matter; performance metrics must adjustThis book’s judgment
External changePhysicians use AI to get information themselves, and some KOLs (key opinion leaders, influential specialist physicians) expect fewer MSL (medical science liaison) interactions, which sets new demands on the value of field engagementVeeva’s KOL survey7

5. Risks and Open Questions

  • Effect evidence: controlled trials or third-party evaluation are needed to show whether AI suggestions improve prescribing or patient outcomes.
  • Vendor concentration: core CRM and AI agents from the same vendor create pricing and migration risk.
  • Compliance: the boundaries for call records, HCP interactions and off-label communication must be defined before agents go live.
  • Field acceptance: whether reps trust and follow recommendations decides whether “released” becomes “used.”

Part III · The Value Chain · Chapter 13

Managed Markets

AI changes managed markets from two sides: payers and regulators use it to automate prior authorization, while drug makers’ access teams use it to organize evidence, write dossiers and analyze payers.

Source: Ch. 10 (Managed Markets: importance / a closer look / operational model)
Evidence grade of this chapter■■□□□□□□□□Primary 23% (3/13) · secondary 77%WeakerPrimary = papers, regulatory documents and company announcements; a company announcement is a primary source but not independent evidence of effect.
Trend signals · as of 2026-11-18
  • AI on the payer side is moving faster: WISeR runs in six states through 2031, and providers in the selected states must take part.
  • WISeR covers device and procedure services, not drugs, so the effect on drug makers is indirect.
  • Value dossiers and evidence integration on the drug-maker side are being productized, with effect evidence from vendors.
Numbers that go stale (dates, funding, valuations) are in Appendix F; changes of judgment between versions are in Appendix E. The above distills and judges this chapter’s content.
In one sentence. Managed markets is the link where outside change is fastest and the drug maker’s own evidence is thinnest: on the payer side, CMS’s WISeR model has piloted AI and machine learning-assisted prior authorization review in six states since January 2026; on the drug-maker side, AI is mainly used for evidence synthesis and dossier writing, yet nearly all public effect evidence comes from vendors. Drug makers must handle both being reviewed by AI and preparing with it1,2,3,4.
Evidence note. Drug-maker tools and effect figures (such as cutting pricing and HTA analysis to minutes) mostly come from vendor claims; payer-side policy information comes mainly from law-firm and trade-body readings, to be checked against CMS texts. No independent effect study of drug makers’ access teams was found.

1. What AI Changes

The source book describes managed markets as the function that builds access, pricing and contracting strategy toward payers, health plans and purchasers. AI runs on two lines here:

Judgment
Payer side: automated prior authorization
CMS’s WISeR model runs from 1 January 2026 to 31 December 2031, working with companies specialized in AI prior authorization to test AI-assisted review in Original Medicare; clinicians must take part in review, with AI meant to support rather than replace clinical judgment2,5.
Judgment
Drug-maker side: evidence, dossiers and payer intelligence
AI integrates evidence, drafts AMCP-format dossiers, turns dossiers into payer presentations, and analyzes payer policy and pricing or HTA outcomes3,6,4.

2. The Payer Side: What Is Happening

ItemContentSource type
WISeR modelA CMS Innovation Center model running from 1 January 2026 in six states (NJ, OH, OK, TX, AZ, WA), in two three-year agreement periods through 31 December 2031 across four MAC jurisdictions, for services in Original Medicare considered vulnerable to fraud, waste and abuse. Checked against the CMS request for applications and the CRS report: providers in the six states must take part; contractors are companies that use AI and similar technology for review; a clinician must review every denial; the first prior-authorization requests were accepted from 5 January, for services from 15 January; the services covered are device and procedure categories such as nerve stimulators, epidural steroid injections and skin substitutes, not drugs7,8,9Primary (CMS, CRS) plus law-firm readings
What is distinctive about participantsThe first CMS Innovation Center model whose participants are only technology innovators; it also tests a “gold card” exemption in which providers with a 90% approval rate may be exempted from future prior authorization10,1Secondary readings
CMS-0057-FRequires affected payers to respond within 7 days for standard and 72 hours for urgent requests and to give specific denial reasons; the FHIR-based API requirements mainly take effect on 1 January 2027; the rule does not cover drugs11Secondary reading
Prior authorization for drugsA rule extending electronic prior authorization requirements to drugs has reportedly been proposed, with comments through 15 June 202611Secondary; final status not verified here
Payers’ AI claimsUnitedHealth reportedly says it is investing about $1.5 billion in AI in 2026 and reports a 96% first-pass approval rate for its AI prior-authorization technology5Company claim retold by a secondary source
ControversyOne reading says CMS’s wider use of AI for medical-necessity decisions may conflict with state laws limiting AI in utilization review, and that providers should anticipate more denials1; others cite litigation over nH Predict as showing that unguarded automation can substitute population averages for individual clinical judgment11Secondary views

Implication for drug makers (judgment): payers’ prior authorization is getting faster and may also get stricter; patient-support and provider-facing teams must adapt early to electronic prior authorization and shorter response windows and track payers’ published prior-authorization metrics. Since 2026, Medicare Advantage, Medicaid, CHIP and marketplace plans have public prior-authorization reporting requirements5.

4. Operating Model

ElementKey pointsBasis or judgment
Joint evidence planningHEOR, medical and access teams plan evidence needs together rather than handing off in sequenceHow a 2026 HEOR trends article describes integration12
Human in the loopPayer-facing content must be human-reviewed; the ISPOR study also uses a human-in-the-loop approachThe ISPOR poster study6
Data and compliance (judgment)Content for payers must be traceable to sources and linked to medical and MLR workflows, reusing the validation and review expectations of Chapters 9 and 11This book’s judgment
External monitoringTrack payers’ published prior-authorization metrics and developments on WISeR and CMS-0057-FCMS public reporting requirements5,11

5. Risks and Open Questions

  • Transparency of payer AI: drug makers and providers can hardly tell how much AI shaped an access decision, which affects dispute handling and evidence strategy.
  • Denials and fairness: without guardrails, automation may substitute group averages for individual judgment11.
  • Drug coverage: CMS-0057-F does not currently cover drugs, and rules for drug prior authorization are still evolving11.
  • No effect evidence on the drug-maker side: whether faster dossiers produce better access outcomes has no public evidence.

Part III · The Value Chain · Chapter 14

Manufacturing Operations

AI in manufacturing centers on quality and operations rather than R&D-style innovation, and is bounded by GMP; the EU’s draft Annex 22 draws the clearest line.

Source: Ch. 11 (Manufacturing Operations: importance / a closer look / operational model)
Evidence grade of this chapter■■□□□□□□□□Primary 25% (4/16) · secondary 75%WeakerPrimary = papers, regulatory documents and company announcements; a company announcement is a primary source but not independent evidence of effect.
Trend signals · as of 2026-11-18
  • Uses such as predictive maintenance are mature, while critical quality decisions are constrained: draft Annex 22 says generative AI should not be used in critical GMP applications.
  • Annex 22 remains a draft, with the working group targeting final text in Q4 2026 (a target, not a confirmed date).
  • Digital twins are mainly used for planning and what-if analysis of production lines.
Numbers that go stale (dates, funding, valuations) are in Appendix F; changes of judgment between versions are in Appendix E. The above distills and judges this chapter’s content.
In one sentence. Manufacturing is where AI faces the clearest regulatory limits: the EU GMP draft Annex 22 would allow only static, deterministic models in critical uses, keep generative AI and LLMs out of critical decisions, and require qualified personnel and a named owner; most companies remain at pilot stage. The value visible so far sits in deviation investigations, predictive maintenance and digital twins, with effect figures mostly from vendors or consultancies’ own claims1,2,3,4.
Evidence note. Figures such as deviation investigations cut by more than 50% come from vendor executive interviews and consultancy research and are claims; Annex 22 is still a draft and its final text could not be confirmed; the FDA warning-letter example has a single secondary source.

1. What AI Changes

The source book describes manufacturing operations as the function that turns a candidate into a qualified product, with quality and supply assurance at its core. AI’s value in manufacturing is less about discovering something new than about cutting deviations, downtime and batch failures: a GlobalData analysis says digital twins, predictive maintenance and real-time quality monitoring are being applied to cut downtime, reduce waste and improve batch consistency, but most companies remain at pilot stage, held back by legacy systems and uneven data quality3. Accenture’s research says only 35% of surveyed executives describe their companies as “connected organizations” in manufacturing and technical operations, and digital pilots are often confined to low-risk, low-impact areas4.

2. Representative Use Cases

Use caseWhat it doesEvidenceMaturity (this book’s judgment)
Predictive maintenanceTrains models on vibration, temperature and process records to spot degradation and recommend intervention windows65% of European drug makers in a survey plan to invest; already widely deployed5Fairly mature
Deviation investigationsDetects out-of-spec results, pulls batch records and drafts root-cause analysesA vendor in a PharmTech interview says investigation time falls by more than 50% in validated GxP environments; Accenture lists “over 50%” as an achievable benefit6,4Deployed at some firms; effect is a claim
Quality investigation and batch-release supportSupports quality investigations and batch-release decisionsThe BMS–Anthropic agreement mentions manufacturing support for quality investigations and batch-release decisions7Announcement and early deployment
Digital twinsBuild virtual copies of facilities and lines to simulate process changes before going liveRoche uses NVIDIA Omniverse for digital twins of production facilities8; Merck, Novartis and BMS reportedly run real-time what-if simulations in some sales-and-operations processes6Being deployed
Visual inspection and yield predictionAI visual checks, production yield and quality-deviation predictionNovartis uses AI visual inspection and predicts quality deviations in manufacturing and quality9Deployed (company description)
Robotics and physical AIUses robots and physical AI to expand capacity for high-demand medicines and strengthen supply resilienceProposed by the Lilly–NVIDIA joint lab10Planning stage

3. Regulation: The EU Draft Annex 22 and the FDA

ItemContentNote
Draft and timingOn 7 July 2025 the European Commission and PIC/S published a joint consultation on the revision of Chapter 4 (documentation) and Annex 11 (computerised systems) and a new Annex 22 (artificial intelligence), open until 7 October 202511Primary
ScopeApplies to computerised systems that use AI models in critical applications (those directly affecting patient safety, product quality or data integrity) in the manufacture of active substances and medicinal products11,1Primary and secondary
Model limitsCritical uses allow only static, deterministic models and exclude generative AI and LLMs; non-critical contexts may use them with a human in the loop1,5Secondary reading
ControlsQualified personnel, a named process subject-matter expert, monitored operator training and segregation of duties; ongoing oversight covers change control, model performance monitoring and human review when needed1,11Primary and secondary
StatusPer reports of August 2026, Annex 22 remains a draft with no final text replacing it; the draft text (as quoted by PharmTech) says it “does not apply to generative AI and large language models, and such models should not be used in critical GMP applications”; the consultation drew about 1,300 comments and the EMA inspectors’ working group targets delivering final text to the Commission in Q4 2026 (a target, not a confirmed date); a workshop on 30 June–1 July 2026 did not amend the July 2025 draft; industry groups oppose a blanket exclusion of generative AI12,2,13Secondary quotation plus secondary reports; the Commission’s original draft was not obtained, so verify
FDA’s approachThe FDA’s January 2025 draft guidance does not propose validating models but a seven-step credibility assessment for a defined context of use; its scope covers manufacturing (the original text uses AI visual inspection of fill volume in vials as an example, showing model risk can fall to medium when AI is not the sole basis for release, and stresses that the quality control unit holds ultimate responsibility for final product quality)14,2Primary (FDA text) plus secondary
Enforcement signalPer one analysis, in April 2026 the FDA issued what appears to be its first cGMP warning letter citing “overreliance on AI,” to a firm that used AI agents to draft specifications and records without adequate human review12Single secondary source, to be verified
EU AI ActPwC says the Act’s risk classification places many pharma manufacturing applications in the “high-risk” class; high-risk obligations for AI embedded in regulated products are deferred to August 2028 (see Chapter 16)15,16Secondary

4. Operating Model

ElementKey pointsBasis or judgment
AI inventory and tieringFirst inventory every AI model used in manufacturing and quality, noting use, data source and criticalityPwC says many firms lack such an inventory, a prerequisite for Annex 22 readiness15
Model choicePrefer static, deterministic models for critical uses; confine generative AI to non-critical, human-in-the-loop settings (drafting, retrieval)The direction of draft Annex 221
People and responsibilityQualified personnel, a named process expert and monitored trainingThe draft’s main controls1
Lifecycle managementChange control, performance monitoring and input-drift monitoring; acceptance criteria defined and documented in advanceThe draft and trade readings11,5
Treatment of existing deploymentsTrade commentary says that once Annex 22 applies, grandfathering in-scope AI systems is unlikely and reassessment is neededThe view in European Pharmaceutical Review5

5. Risks and Open Questions

  • Final rules: the final Annex 22 text and whether the limit on generative AI survives are still to be confirmed.
  • Pilot to scale: most firms are still piloting, and the bottleneck is data infrastructure and legacy systems rather than the models3,4.
  • Cross-market execution: a small process change can trigger different variation filings in different markets, and existing digital twins rarely automate that layer6. This view comes from one vendor founder and needs more evidence.
  • Overreliance: one warning letter suggests that releasing AI-drafted specifications and records without review creates compliance risk12.

Part III · The Value Chain · Chapter 15

Trade and Distribution

Where AI sits in pharmaceutical distribution: demand sensing, inventory and shortage warning, cold-chain risk. Wholesalers’ lack of point-of-sale signals is the structural puzzle of this link.

Source: Ch. 12 (Trade and Distribution: importance / a closer look / operational model)
Evidence grade of this chapter■□□□□□□□□□Primary 14% (1/7) · secondary 86%WeakerPrimary = papers, regulatory documents and company announcements; a company announcement is a primary source but not independent evidence of effect.
Trend signals · as of 2026-11-18
  • Better forecasts do not mean patients get the drug: the US still had 227 active drug shortages in Q2 2026, a third consecutive rise.
  • Wholesalers see pharmacy orders but not necessarily the patients who left the counter without the drug, which is data-limited.
  • Control towers and cold-chain AI are emerging, with limited public detail.
Numbers that go stale (dates, funding, valuations) are in Appendix F; changes of judgment between versions are in Appendix E. The above distills and judges this chapter’s content.
In one sentence. AI’s value in distribution centers on seeing demand shifts and shortage risk earlier. In the US system wholesalers usually do not get customers’ point-of-sale data and rely on orders and historical demand; AI compares forecasts with ordering patterns to spot deviations earlier. Shortages are still rising: the US had 227 active national drug shortages in Q2 2026, the third consecutive quarterly increase1,2,3.
Evidence note. The effect figures here (such as an improvement of about 10% in forecasting) come from executives speaking in trade media and are not independently verified; survey figures on adoption are secondary retellings. European, Chinese and emerging-market distribution systems are not covered, nor are the specific provisions of the DSCSA.

1. What AI Changes

The source book describes trade and distribution as the logistics and commercial arrangements that get medicines to pharmacies, hospitals and patients. The link is marked by thin margins and high complexity: one analysis says US pharmaceutical wholesalers typically earn net margins below 1% against about 23% on average for branded manufacturers4. In such a structure, small gains in forecasting and inventory are worth a lot.

227
active national drug shortages in the US in Q2 2026, a third consecutive quarterly increase (ASHP and the University of Utah)1,2
87
new drug shortages through Q3 20265
~10%
forecasting improvement Cencora attributes to AI demand sensing (executive’s account)1
<1%
typical net margin of US drug wholesalers (per an ISG analysis)4

2. Representative Use Cases

Use caseWhat it doesEvidenceMaturity (this book’s judgment)
Demand sensingCompares forecasts with ordering patterns to flag demand deviations earlyCencora’s approach; its executive cites about a 10% forecasting improvement1Deployed (company description)
Shortage predictionUses machine learning to predict shortages and supply disruptions so distributors can act earlierMcKesson describes using different models for different forecasting horizons6Deployed (company description)
Demand signals with knowledge graphsCombines demand signals with knowledge graphs to anticipate shortagesAn emerging approach mentioned at LogiPharma 20262Emerging
Shipment exception handlingResolves transport exceptions and flags demand shiftsExecutives at LogiPharma 2026 described AI already handling such work2Early deployment
Control towers and agentsSpecialized AI agents, within stability and contract guardrails, reroute shipments and release alternate supplyTrade articles describe maturing capability, with human-led decisions still the premise7Emerging
Cold-chain riskAssesses risk of cold-chain interruption for biologics, mRNA vaccines and cell and gene therapiesThe cold chain is called the defining operational test of distribution; Cencora cites a $1 billion investment in cold-chain resilience7,3Early; limited detail

3. The Structural Puzzle: No Point-of-Sale Signal

Unlike consumer supply chains, pharmaceutical wholesalers mostly do not get their customers’ demand data: consumer industries can see from point-of-sale data whether sales run ahead of or behind forecast, while pharma wholesalers cannot2. Cencora’s Brent Wilhelm says a drug can be in the supply chain and still not be available; the chain still runs on historical demand data, and AI could close the real-time visibility gap3. AI’s role here is therefore closer to inferring demand from the indirect signal of orders than to having true demand.

4. Operating Model

ElementKey pointsBasis or judgment
Clean data firstInventory and order data must be centralized and consistent before AI forecasting is meaningfulA shared view in trade articles and among health-system leaders7
Multi-party data sharing (judgment)Sharing demand and inventory signals among manufacturers, wholesalers and providers is key to visibility; sharing terms raise competition and compliance issuesThis book’s judgment, consistent with the JPM 2026 data-ownership theme
Role of peopleAI helps planners make near-real-time adjustments, with people keeping decision authorityTrade article7
Traceability and complianceDSCSA enforcement is tightening, and traceability requirements mature alongside AI control towers and digital twinsHow a trade article describes the 2025–2026 operating environment7

5. Risks and Open Questions

  • Whether gains translate into fewer shortages: shortages are still rising, showing forecasting gains do not equal supply gains, since root causes such as raw materials, capacity and pricing lie outside AI’s reach.
  • Adoption and governance: a Gartner survey of 509 supply-chain leaders lists AI- and agent-driven changes in ways of working as a major factor5; whether the industry is “moving too fast” is itself a live debate.
  • Signal quality: inferring demand from orders risks amplifying the bullwhip effect (judgment).
  • Out of scope: distribution systems and regulation differ in Europe, China and emerging markets, so conclusions here cannot be extrapolated directly.

Part III · The Value Chain · Chapter 16

Corporate Functions

Finance, HR, IT, legal and compliance, plus bridging functions such as pharmacovigilance: back-office functions are often the first to reach scale and the first to meet governance questions.

Source: Appendix (financial management and control, human resources, IT, legal and compliance, other corporate functions)
Evidence grade of this chapter■□□□□□□□□□Primary 7% (1/15) · secondary 93%WeakerPrimary = papers, regulatory documents and company announcements; a company announcement is a primary source but not independent evidence of effect.
Trend signals · as of 2026-11-18
  • Finance and HR adoption is low, and pharmacovigilance case processing is among the most compliance-sensitive deployments.
  • Savings figures from drug makers are mostly targets rather than results (such as Pfizer’s additional $1.2 billion target).
  • The EU AI Act’s high-risk duties are deferred to December 2027 and August 2028, and recruitment AI is a high-risk use.
Numbers that go stale (dates, funding, valuations) are in Appendix F; changes of judgment between versions are in Appendix E. The above distills and judges this chapter’s content.
In one sentence. Corporate functions are where AI is easiest to put a number on at a drug maker: Pfizer pins its additional $1.2 billion of savings by 2027 largely on digital enablement, AI and automation, and Novartis already uses AI in finance, HR and marketing. Overall it is still early: a survey of finance teams across industries says only 17% use AI in core workflows. On governance, the EU AI Act’s high-risk obligations have been deferred, not cancelled1,2,3,4.
Evidence note. The figures here mostly come from secondary consulting articles, industry surveys and company interviews; savings and efficiency figures are mostly company targets or claims, not audited results. Functions were not researched one by one, and tax and procurement are not covered.

1. What AI Changes

The source book’s appendix treats finance, human resources, IT, legal and compliance as the corporate functions that keep a drug maker running. They have clear processes, relatively structured data and easily counted costs, so they are the easiest to quantify. PwC Strategy& estimates that if drug makers industrialize AI use cases across the enterprise they could add $254 billion in annual operating profit by 2030, with operations holding 39% of the value, R&D 26%, commercial 24% and the rest from support functions such as finance, IT, HR and legal (figure cited secondhand)3.

2. Representative Use Cases

FunctionWhat AI doesEvidenceMaturity (this book’s judgment)
FinanceAutomating monthly and quarterly close, reconciliation, contract review and revenue recognitionA guide to biotech finance says close is the most labor-intensive recurring process and where automation gains are clearest; a survey says only 17% of finance teams use AI in core workflows3Early
Human resourcesDrafting policy documents, announcements and job descriptionsNovartis uses AI tools in finance, HR and marketing and gives employees access to the latest generative AI assistants2Deployed (company description)
IT and software engineeringAgent-run IT operations; AI to speed software engineeringNovartis reportedly signed a five-year contract with Cognizant in July 2026 to run its global IT with agentic AI; BMS lists speeding software engineering with Claude Code as one of three priorities5,6Emerging, partly outsourced
Legal and complianceSummarizing regulations, reviewing contracts, checking contentA trade article says Moderna’s legal teams use generative assistants to summarize regulations (secondary)7; Pfizer’s Charlie supports content review and compliance flags1Early
Pharmacovigilance and adverse-event processingTriage, extracting safety information and drafting case narrativesPer secondary articles, case processing consumes up to two-thirds of drug-safety resources; IQVIA projects digital workers can compress case-processing from about a week to about 24 hours; Pfizer’s GENESIS automates safety-monitoring administration8,9Deployed; effect is a claim
Global business services (GBS)Shared-service centers evolving toward “generative business services”HFS Research says 40% of pharma GBS remains in the legacy back-office archetype and 60% in a slightly upgraded enterprise-standardization archetype10In progress

3. Quantified Targets: Pfizer and Novartis

CompanySignalNote
PfizerAnnounced in April 2025 an additional $1.2 billion of savings by 2027, driven largely by digital enablement, AI and automation1; its 10-K says it expanded cost-reduction programs in selling, informational and administrative expenses, driven largely by digital enablement (including automation and AI) and process simplification11A company target, not an audited result
NovartisIn Q3 2026 selling, general and administrative costs were 7% lower than a year earlier, and the company has started tying AI to costs without giving a number; its 19 November 2026 investor day includes an AI panel5Secondary index

4. Governance and Regulation: The EU AI Act Timeline

DateWhat appliesStatus
2 Feb 2025Prohibited AI practices and definitionsApplies; not deferred12
2 Aug 2025General-purpose AI model obligations and governance frameworkApplies; not deferred12,13
27 Jul 2026The Digital Omnibus amending the AI Act enters into forceIn force4,12
2 Aug 2026Article 50 transparency obligations and othersApplies; not deferred13
2 Dec 2027Obligations for stand-alone high-risk AI systems under Annex III (covering recruitment, credit scoring and similar uses)Deferred 16 months from August 2026 (checked against the Council timeline)14,4,15
2 Aug 2028Obligations for high-risk AI embedded in regulated products such as medical devicesDeferred from the earlier timetable (checked against the Council timeline)14,15,13

Implication for drug makers (judgment): recruitment AI in HR falls under Annex III’s high-risk uses, AI for medical devices is affected by the 2028 deferral (relevant to the device makers in Chapter 5), and drug manufacturing must also watch EU GMP Annex 22 (Chapter 14). “Deferred” does not mean “exempt”: commentators repeatedly stress that the obligations did not change, only the clock4,15.

5. Operating Model

ElementKey pointsBasis or judgment
Start with quantifiable processesBegin with processes that are clear and countable, such as close, reconciliation, contract review and case processingAdvice from a biotech finance guide3
AI governance councilA cross-functional council reviews AI use cases for safety and compliance; AI sits in the boardroom and risk frameworkTrade-article advice; Novartis makes data, digital and AI a standing board topic8,5
Outsource or buildBack-office functions often advance by outsourcing plus agents (such as Novartis’s IT operations contract), and compliance and data responsibilities must be written into contractsThe Novartis–Cognizant contract (reported)5
Functional boundaries (judgment)Pharmacovigilance, legal and compliance carry statutory duties, so AI use needs a named human owner and audit trailThis book’s judgment; confirm with the functions concerned

6. Risks and Open Questions

  • Savings as a target or a result: for most companies the savings are targets, and actual effects remain to be seen in results; Novartis’s cost decline has not been tied quantitatively to AI5.
  • People and roles: AI in back-office functions will change job structures, and no reliable industry data was found for this draft.
  • A compliance time gap: deferral of the AI Act’s high-risk obligations may relax preparation; commentators advise using the time to close the gap4.
  • Accountability: how responsibility is divided between company and vendor when AI-drafted or AI-processed cases and contracts go wrong.

Part IV · Focus Topics · Chapter 17

Medical Devices and Surgical Robotics

A focus on surgical robotics: how the field moved from one dominant platform to several, why AI’s role is a data platform plus incremental autonomy, and how far the academic frontier is from the clinic.

Source: none (new focus topic)
Evidence grade of this chapter■■■□□□□□□□Primary 28% (7/25) · secondary 72%WeakerPrimary = papers, regulatory documents and company announcements; a company announcement is a primary source but not independent evidence of effect.
Trend signals · as of 2026-11-18
  • New entrants in surgical robots are being authorized: Hugo at the FDA (December 2025), Ottava via De Novo (July 2026) and CMR Versius entering the US (March 2026).
  • Autonomy is moving from assistance toward incremental autonomy, while full autonomy remains in ex vivo and animal studies.
  • Independent patient-outcome evidence is still lacking.
Numbers that go stale (dates, funding, valuations) are in Appendix F; changes of judgment between versions are in Appendix E. The above distills and judges this chapter’s content.
In one sentence. In 2025–2026 surgical robotics became a multi-player field: Medtronic’s Hugo was FDA-cleared in December 2025, J&J’s Ottava received De Novo authorization in July 2026, and CMR Surgical made its US debut in March 2026, all alongside Intuitive’s da Vinci. AI shows up as surgical-data platforms and autonomy features added one by one; academically, a Johns Hopkins system has autonomously completed a phase of cholecystectomy on ex vivo organs, but by one survey no commercially available system makes a surgical decision on its own1,2,3,4,5.
Evidence note. Regulatory and product information on surgical robots comes mostly from company press releases and trade media; the autonomy levels and “who sits where” come from trade surveys, and sources label the levels differently. This draft does not assess the clinical efficacy of any system.

1. The Landscape: From One Platform to Several

Company / platformLatest milestoneRole of AI and dataSource
Intuitive (da Vinci, Ion)546 systems placed in Q3 2026, 315 of them da Vinci 5; about 1,200 da Vinci 5 installed6,7Case Insights, force feedback, surgeon-initiated tool eject and multi-arm targeting; capture data first, then insight, then guidance8,9Primary and secondary
Medtronic (Hugo)FDA clearance for urology in December 2025; submissions for general and gynecologic use in June 20261Touch Surgery Aide and the first real-time AI application, IEP, running on NVIDIA1Primary
J&J MedTech (Ottava)De Novo authorization in July 2026, the first table-integrated soft-tissue robotic system2Polyphonic digital ecosystem; software-coordinated automated arm positioning2Primary
Stryker (Mako orthopedics; neurovascular with Siemens)More than 3,000 Mako installed; over two-thirds of US knees at the end of 2025 used Mako10The SmartCare digital business unit11Secondary
CMR Surgical (Versius)Introduced to US surgeons at the SAGES meeting in late March 20263—Secondary
Distalmotion (Dexter)FDA authorization in October 202412—Secondary
Moon Surgical (Maestro)An FDA-cleared robotic assistant13Added edge-to-cloud data capture across its installed base, aiming to sell procedure-data insight rather than only the robot13Secondary
Vicarious SurgicalReportedly shut down to liquidate assets14—Secondary

The table shows two things: platform-level competition has intensified and hospital purchasing is no longer a single-platform decision3; and every player is moving the competitive point from the machine to data and software, consistent with Chapter 5’s judgment that data platforms are the new moat narrative.

2. The Autonomy Ladder: Where Things Stand

Level (Science Robotics framework)MeaningRepresentativeStatus
Robotic assistance (low levels)The robot performs specific actions under the surgeon’s controlCore functions of da Vinci, Hugo, Ottava and MakoCommercial5
Task automationAutomatically completes well-defined tasks under continuous supervisionAutomated positioning, instrument-exit warnings, multi-arm targetingPartly commercial2,1,8
Level 4: high autonomy under supervisionThe robot makes and executes decisions under qualified supervision without step-by-step approvalJohns Hopkins’ SRT-H (rated level 4 by its authors)Research, not a product4,15
Level 5: full autonomyThe robot performs the procedure with no human involvementNoneNo real surgical system reaches it15

Note: sources label levels 3 and 4 differently (some call level 4 “conditional autonomy”); this chapter uses the Science Robotics framework cited by the SRT-H authors. That framework’s authors also warn that at higher levels (level 5 and possibly level 4) the robot is not only a medical device but is also practicing medicine, raising regulatory, ethical and legal questions4.

3. Three Cases

Case 1: SRT-H (Johns Hopkins, ex vivo cholecystectomy)
  • Method: a hierarchical framework trained by imitation learning, with a large language model planning tasks and a separate low-level controller executing them13,4.
  • Result: reported in Science Robotics in July 2025, the system autonomously completed the clipping-and-cutting phase of cholecystectomy on eight ex vivo gallbladders with a 100% success rate and no human intervention5,15.
  • Level: the authors rate it level 4 (high autonomy under supervision), because the system generated its own corrective actions and there was no user input during trials4.
  • Earlier work: the same lab’s STAR robot autonomously completed more than 83% of a suturing task in live-pig intestinal anastomosis, with a human correcting the rest5.
What it shows
Step-level autonomy is feasible in a controlled ex vivo setting, and an LLM-plus-low-level-controller structure can work for surgical tasks.
What it does not show
It does not show clinical readiness: this is ex vivo organs, one phase and a small sample; surveys stress it is not a purchasable product and does not show that unsupervised human surgery is solved5,12.
Case 2: Intuitive’s Incremental Autonomy
  • In Q3 2026 FDA 510(k) clearances came for da Vinci 5’s surgeon-initiated tool eject and multi-arm targeting, described as adding efficiency and surgeon autonomy8.
  • About 8% of da Vinci 5 procedures use force-feedback instruments16.
  • Route: capture structured surgical data first, then analyze it into insight, then deliver actionable guidance; AI is expected to inform workflow optimization, performance modeling and intraoperative guidance9.
  • US utilization is reportedly 11% higher than the prior-generation Xi, which the company ties to designing more surgeon autonomy and efficiency into da Vinci 57.
What it shows
The commercial route is autonomy features added and cleared one by one, each with its own regulatory recognition, while data accumulates.
What it does not show
It does not show these features improve clinical outcomes: public material stresses efficiency and utilization, with no outcome data.
Case 3: Medtronic’s Touch Surgery Aide
  • A July 2026 AI-native surgical computing platform that uses NVIDIA Holoscan, CUDA and TensorRT so several AI applications can run at once in live surgery1.
  • The first application, Instrument Exit Point, uses computer vision to give a visual warning when instruments leave the field of view; it is FDA-cleared and runs on Hugo, and the platform is designed to expand into laparoscopic surgery over time1,17.
  • The Touch Surgery ecosystem is in more than 1,500 operating rooms worldwide, linking preoperative planning and training, intraoperative tele-mentoring and AI-powered postoperative insight1.
What it shows
It illustrates a platform-plus-apps approach: compute and a data platform first, then AI applications added and cleared one by one.
What it does not show
It does not show real-time AI has materially changed surgical outcomes: the first application is an out-of-view instrument warning, a safety aid rather than a decision.

4. Regulatory and Commercialization Paths

PathContentSource type
New category: De NovoOttava went De Novo as the first table-integrated soft-tissue robot, covering ten upper-abdominal general-surgery procedures2,18Primary and secondary
Existing category: 510(k)Hugo’s urology indication was FDA-cleared and its expanded indications were submitted through 510(k); Intuitive’s new features were cleared through 510(k); Medtronic’s IEP received FDA clearance (the exact pathway was not stated in the material found)1,8Primary and secondary
AI device lifecycleThe FDA finalized predetermined change control plan (PCCP) guidance for AI/ML devices in December 2024; a January 2025 draft on the lifecycle of AI-enabled device software functions was still a draft as of July 202619,20Secondary
FDA AI-enabled device listRadiology dominates; 55 of 72 new authorizations in Q4 2025 were radiology21Secondary
Independent effect evidenceThrough 5 December 2025 the FDA had cleared or approved 1,357 AI/ML devices, of which only 3 have been tested on patient outcomes (PLOS Digital Health, August 2026); Stanford’s AI Index 2026 also notes that authorizations of AI devices are rising while clinical evidence lags22,23Primary (paper and annual report)
FDA call for comment on generative-AI devicesThe FDA issued a discussion paper on generative-AI-enabled medical devices (docket FDA-2026-N-7874) covering premarket confirmation, risk-proportionate postmarket monitoring, and considerations for foundation models and agentic AI; comments were due 19 October 202624Primary (FDA press release)
EUAI Act obligations for high-risk AI embedded in regulated products such as medical devices are deferred to 2 August 202825Secondary (see Chapter 16)

5. Operating Model: What Hospitals and Vendors Must Do (Judgment)

PartyKey pointsBasis
VendorsLayer data capture, platform and apps; file each AI feature separately and keep change control; design clear contracts for data ownership and secondary usePlatform-plus-apps practice; this book’s judgment1,13
HospitalsMove from single-platform buying to multi-platform portfolios; assess data interfaces and portability; train surgical teams and set usage norms for AI featuresThe changed procurement environment3
Drug makers (judgment)Surgical and perioperative data may become a source of real-world evidence, involving drug–device combinations and data partnerships; data rights must be clarified with device makersThis book’s judgment, linked to the data-rights themes of Chapters 9 and 10

6. Risks and Open Questions

  • From efficiency to outcomes: public evidence is mostly efficiency and utilization, with little outcome data.
  • Accountability: when an AI prompt or automated function errs, whether the vendor, hospital or surgeon answers; higher autonomy levels also raise the boundary of “practicing medicine”4.
  • Data and privacy: capture, use and cross-border transfer of surgical video and data need clear rights and protection.
  • Stability of the field: some players have exited (Vicarious Surgical’s liquidation), so capital and scale are thresholds14.

Part IV · Focus Topics · Chapter 18

AI Across Disease Areas

AI’s effect is uneven across disease areas: oncology holds the most AI clinical assets and other areas have less evidence. This chapter offers a comparison framework and lists the signals found for each area.

Source: none (new focus topic)
Evidence grade of this chapter■■■■□□□□□□Primary 41% (11/27) · secondary 59%MediumPrimary = papers, regulatory documents and company announcements; a company announcement is a primary source but not independent evidence of effect.
Trend signals · as of 2026-11-18
  • AI clinical assets concentrate in oncology (about 57%–59%) and small molecules (about 80%), a narrow pipeline.
  • The framework of data availability, biological complexity and endpoint measurability helps judge the next disease area.
  • Cardiovascular and ophthalmology rest mainly on device-side evidence, while vaccines and cell and gene therapy lack evidence.
Numbers that go stale (dates, funding, valuations) are in Appendix F; changes of judgment between versions are in Appendix E. The above distills and judges this chapter’s content.
In one sentence. In the data available, AI clinical assets concentrate heavily in oncology and small molecules: an ASCO 2026 analysis finds 59% of 117 AI-enabled assets are oncology and 82% small molecules; meanwhile AI-originated molecules make up only about 1%–2% of the whole clinical pipeline. Oncology’s lead may reflect AI’s fit, or simply that oncology is the fastest-growing area of the whole pipeline; the two cannot yet be told apart1,2,3. November 2026 update: the first approval of a drug with AI involvement came in infectious disease (China, conditional), and the first AI drugs in Phase III are in respiratory and allergy rather than oncology4,5,6.
Evidence note. The definition of an “AI asset” differs across counts (see Appendix C), and authoritative data split by disease area are scarce; apart from oncology’s 59%, this chapter rests mostly on cases and company announcements. The framework and explanations are this book’s judgment, not empirical conclusions.

1. The Overall Picture: Three Numbers

59%
of the 117 AI-enabled clinical assets in an ASCO 2026 analysis that are oncology1
82%
share of small molecules in the same analysis, the rest being antibodies and other protein drugs1
1%–2%
share of AI-originated molecules in the whole clinical pipeline (using AI-native biotechs as a proxy)2

Two backgrounds matter. First, oncology was the fastest-growing therapeutic area of the global clinical pipeline over two decades3, so AI assets’ concentration in oncology partly reflects the pipeline’s own composition. Second, Oliver Wyman’s analysis says AI-enabled drugs show significantly higher success rates than historical levels in early clinical trials, though evidence remains limited2, consistent with Chapter 7’s view of good Phase I and Phase II in line with history.

Another peer-reviewed analysis published online in October 2026 (129 AI-enabled assets across 69 companies, data through 1 March 2026) gives a similar distribution: oncology 74 assets (57.3%), small molecules 103 (79.8%), assets against a novel target 48 (37.2%)7,8; this is close to the ASCO abstract’s 59% and 82% and the two corroborate each other, though the samples differ.

2. A Comparison Framework (This Book’s Judgment)

DimensionQuestionWhat it means for AI
Data availabilityHow much high-quality omics, imaging and clinical data does the area have?The more and the more structured the data, the easier models are to train and validate
Biological complexityIs the target clear? Is the disease mechanism understood?AI is good at producing candidates but has shown no edge in predicting whether a target holds in patients (Chapters 1 and 7)
Endpoint measurabilityAre clinical endpoints objective and quickly measurable?The more objective and faster the endpoint, the easier AI-assisted design and assessment are to validate

3. Area by Area: Signals Found

AreaSignals foundEvidence levelExplanation under the framework (judgment)
OncologyHighest share of AI assets (59%)1; BMS and AbbVie collaborations cover oncology9,10; AstraZeneca bought Modella to focus on oncology clinical development and biomarkers11; Insilico’s ISM6331 won Fast Track, and Schrödinger’s SGR-1505 reported a 22% objective response rate in lymphoma (secondary)12Medium: most assets, but mostly early stageRich imaging and pathology data, relatively objective endpoints such as response rate, and the largest pipeline
Immunology and inflammationZasocitinib (psoriasis) is under FDA Priority Review with a target date in Q1 202713; the Merck–Mayo collaboration covers inflammatory bowel disease and atopic dermatitis14; AbbVie–Iambic covers immunology10; Absci’s ABS-101 is an AI-designed biologic for inflammatory bowel disease (secondary)12Medium: an asset near market, though designed mainly with physics-based simulationRelatively clear target mechanisms and measurable skin and gut endpoints
Respiratory, fibrosis and allergyRentosertib (idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis): target and molecule both from an AI platform, Phase III announced July 2026 with first dosing in September15,5; Generate’s GB-0895 (severe asthma) is the first AI-designed antibody in Phase III, with two Phase III trials planning about 1,600 people6Medium: two cases in Phase III, neither with Phase III dataWell-defined diseases with objective readouts (lung-function FVC, asthma exacerbation rate), but both need 52-week trials to validate
NeuroscienceMerck–Mayo covers multiple sclerosis14; the BMS and AbbVie collaborations both include neuroscience9,10; Ono and Congruence widened their deal to neurology and immunology, with Congruence’s own pipeline including GBA-associated Parkinson’s disease16Low: mostly collaboration announcements, no clinical data foundComplex mechanisms, slow and more subjective endpoints, and hard data access (judgment)
Metabolic and obesityLilly uses cash from its weight-loss and diabetes franchise to expand AI spending17; the Lilly–NVIDIA joint lab proposes robots and physical AI to expand capacity for high-demand medicines18. No evidence was found of an AI-originated obesity drug in the clinic; Superluminal (GPCR targets, $60 million Series B) reportedly expects its AI-discovered obesity candidate to enter the clinic in 2026, with no trial registration seen19Low: AI appears more in manufacturing and commercial than in molecule discoveryCore products are mostly peptides against known targets, so the bottleneck is capacity and commercialization rather than discovery (judgment)
Rare diseaseRecursion’s REC-4881 (familial adenomatous polyposis), Phase 1b/2: after 12 weeks 75% of evaluable patients had a smaller polyp burden, a median reduction of 43%20; in October 2026 Anthropic disclosed its acquisition of Mithril Medicine (two rare muscular dystrophies) and said the program is still preclinical21Low to medium: early clinical signals, but small samples and mostly company disclosuresFew patients and scarce data: large potential value but hard validation (judgment)
Infectious diseaseMprosevir (anti-COVID) was conditionally approved by China’s NMPA in July 2026 (announced by Westlake in early September), called by its developers the first “AI-assisted” Class 1 innovative drug; per Chinese reports AI set DEL screening conditions and control compounds and closed the data loop4,22; another analysis says industry attention to infectious disease has fallen back since the COVID-era peak3Medium: an approved case exists, but it is conditional and AI’s role was assistance rather than proposing the targetThe target is known (a viral protease) and screening is high-throughput, so AI shows an effect most easily in narrowing candidates (judgment)
CardiovascularMost evidence is on the device side: among FDA-authorized AI devices, imaging-related cardiovascular ones number 130 (9.6%), second to radiology23; in a randomized trial AI-plus-ECG screening raised the diagnosis of low ejection fraction from 1.6% to 2.1%24. On the drug side no reliable evidence of an AI-originated cardiovascular drug in the clinic was foundDevices: medium; drugs: insufficient evidenceDevices have plentiful, well-structured data; on the drug side endpoints (cardiovascular events) are slow and trials large and long (judgment)
OphthalmologyAutonomous AI screening for diabetic retinopathy is a widely cited case (IDx-DR, now LumineticsCore, operable by primary-care staff, cleared in 2018); 2026 also brought reported clearances for glaucoma screening and more25Medium: mature on the device side, but this draft’s source is a secondary blog and details were not checkedRich image data and clear diagnostic standards suit automated screening (judgment); no drug-side evidence found
VaccinesNo reliable clinical-stage evidence found; an industry overview says venture financing for mRNA vaccines fell sharply after US funding cuts (about $510 million in 2023 to about $90 million in 2025 to that point)26Insufficient evidenceImmunogenicity is hard to predict and cycles are driven by epidemics, so the funding climate precedes any AI effect (judgment)
Cell and gene therapyNo reliable evidence of an AI-originated therapy in the clinic was found; Takeda reprioritized its pipeline in 2025 and stepped back from cell and gene therapy, while Astellas remains committed to regenerative medicine and CGT for rare diseases27Insufficient evidenceComplex modalities, few patients and scarce data (judgment)

4. Using the Framework to Prioritize (Judgment)

  • Choose data-rich, objective-endpoint areas for scaled validation first: oncology and immunology-inflammation best meet the conditions today.
  • Stay cautious where mechanisms are complex and endpoints slow: neuroscience has many announcements and little clinical evidence, so tie collaboration milestones to clinical readouts.
  • Separate discovery value from other links: in areas such as obesity, AI’s near-term value may lie more in manufacturing, supply and commercial work.
  • Treat pipeline structure as a confounder: when judging AI’s area advantage, first strip out that area’s own pipeline-size effect.

5. Risks and Open Questions

  • Counting definitions: “AI asset” is not uniformly defined, so area-level figures have limited reliability.
  • Survivorship bias: public cases are mostly successes or programs in progress, while failed projects and areas are less reported.
  • Spillover across areas: whether a platform validated in one area transfers to another has little evidence so far.
  • Coverage gaps: cardiovascular, ophthalmology, vaccines and cell and gene therapy are now in the table above, though the evidence is mostly secondary or insufficient; dermatology, gastroenterology, nephrology, hematology and more remain uncovered.

  26. Biotech sector overview: AI drug discovery, CRISPR, mRNA vaccines (FounderNest)secondary

Part IV · Focus Topics · Chapter 19

The Customer Side

Patients, physicians and payers already use AI at scale, which changes the premises on which drug makers reach customers, deliver evidence and handle safety information.

Source: Ch. 3 (Pharmaceutical and Biotechnology Customers)
Evidence grade of this chapter■■□□□□□□□□Primary 15% (2/13) · secondary 85%WeakerPrimary = papers, regulatory documents and company announcements; a company announcement is a primary source but not independent evidence of effect.
Trend signals · as of 2026-11-18
  • Physician use of AI is rising fast: the AMA survey shows 81% against 38% in 2023.
  • Patients use AI for health information at scale (KFF: about 32% of adults; OpenAI’s weekly query figures are secondary).
  • The information entry point is changing, and drug makers must consider how their content is cited by AI.
Numbers that go stale (dates, funding, valuations) are in Appendix F; changes of judgment between versions are in Appendix E. The above distills and judges this chapter’s content.
In one sentence. Drug makers’ customers have adopted AI ahead of them: in the AMA’s 2026 survey 81% of US physicians use AI in practice; per KFF about a third of US adults have turned to AI for health information or advice; on the payer side CMS’s WISeR is piloting AI-assisted prior authorization in Original Medicare. Drug makers face customers whose ways of getting information and making decisions are changing1,2,3.
Evidence note. The data here are mostly US: physician data come from the AMA’s self-reported survey (about 1,700 physicians), and patient data from KFF, Pew and Gallup surveys, some retold by secondary compilations. Other countries are not covered.

1. Where the Three Customer Groups Stand

81%
of US physicians use AI in practice, up from 38% in 2023 (AMA 2026)1
32%
of US adults turned to AI for health information or advice in the past year (KFF, February–March 2026 survey)2,4
230M
weekly users worldwide asking ChatGPT health questions per OpenAI in January 2026; over 300 million by July 20265,6
8%
of US physicians say most of their patients disclose using AI; 30% believe most patients are probably using it6

2. Patients and Consumers

  • Rich tooling: in January 2026 OpenAI launched ChatGPT Health, which can connect to medical records and wellness apps, alongside an enterprise offering for healthcare organizations7,5; KFF’s April review also lists Amazon Health AI (January), Microsoft Copilot Health (March), Perplexity Health (March) and Claude personal-health integrations for paid users8.
  • How it is used: in KFF’s survey 32% of adults turned to AI for health information, 29% for physical health and 16% for mental health; 41% of these users have uploaded personal medical information4,8. 58% of physical-health users reportedly saw a provider afterward, so many patients bring AI answers into the exam room2.
  • Concerns and safety: 77% of adults worry about the privacy of medical information they give AI tools4; in a West Health–Gallup survey of more than 5,500 US adults, about one in ten who used AI in the past 30 days reported advice they believed was unsafe5.

3. Physicians and Other Prescribers

  • Use: the AMA’s 2026 survey (about 1,700 physicians) shows physicians use AI for 2.3 tasks on average (1.1 in 2023), most often summarizing medical research and clinical documentation1,9.
  • Attitude: more than three-quarters of physicians say AI improves their ability to care for patients (65% in 2023); 40% hold a balanced “equally excited and concerned” view, worried most about patient privacy and the integrity of the patient–physician relationship1.
  • Views on patient use: 70% of physicians say patients’ appropriate use of general-purpose AI for routine health information is beneficial or has no impact; most do not want patients using AI for tasks needing clinical judgment, and nearly half strongly oppose patients using AI to interpret radiology or pathology results10,9.
  • KOL (key opinion leader) behavior: Veeva’s survey of 75 KOLs shows they use AI to synthesize literature, check standards of care and learn about indications and use cases, and some expect to need fewer MSL interactions (see Chapter 9)11.

4. Payers

Payers already use AI for prior authorization and utilization management: CMS’s WISeR has piloted in six states since January 2026; UnitedHealth reportedly reports a 96% first-pass approval rate for its AI prior-authorization technology (company claim); critics and litigation warn that unguarded automation may substitute group averages for individual judgment. See Chapter 133,12,13.

5. What It Means for Drug Makers (This Book’s Judgment)

CustomerChangeImplication for drug makers
PatientsAsk AI first, then see the physician; upload recordsPatient education and labeling must be correctly citable by AI; patient-support programs should expect consultations from patients who arrive with AI answers
PhysiciansUse AI to summarize research and standardsMedical information and MSL value must shift from delivering information to explanation and context (Chapter 9); content must be retrievable and citable (Chapter 11)
PayersAI-assisted prior authorization and shorter response timesAccess and patient-support teams must adapt to electronic prior authorization and shorter windows; evidence dossiers must suit AI-screened workflows (Chapter 13)
Safety and complianceAdverse-event information may appear in AI conversationsDefine how safety information in AI channels is identified, captured and reported (judgment; confirm with pharmacovigilance)

6. Risks and Open Questions

  • Accuracy and accountability: patients and physicians both lean on general-purpose AI, over whose output drug makers have limited influence, but errors about their own products still deserve attention.
  • Privacy: a sizable share of patients upload medical information to AI while most worry about privacy4.
  • Digital divide: conclusions based mostly on US data may not hold in other markets and populations.
  • Measuring the shift in engagement: reduced demand from KOLs and physicians is only an expectation in surveys and needs more evidence.

Part V · Outlook · Chapter 20

Regulation, Data, Ethics and Risk

In pharma, regulation often sets the ceiling for AI: this chapter consolidates the latest status of regulatory documents and sorts out data, validation, biosecurity, intellectual property and accountability.

Source: none (new chapter)
Evidence grade of this chapter■■□□□□□□□□Primary 19% (6/32) · secondary 81%WeakerPrimary = papers, regulatory documents and company announcements; a company announcement is a primary source but not independent evidence of effect.
Trend signals · as of 2026-11-18
  • Regulation is still mostly drafts and principles: the FDA’s January 2025 draft remains a draft, and the FDA–EMA ten principles are not formal guidance.
  • The EU AI Act’s high-risk timetable has been pushed back, but it stacks alongside GMP and device regulation.
  • Inventorship: the US recognizes only natural persons, and AI is a tool (USPTO, November 2025).
Numbers that go stale (dates, funding, valuations) are in Appendix F; changes of judgment between versions are in Appendix E. The above distills and judges this chapter’s content.
In one sentence. As of November 2026 regulation is still mostly principles plus drafts: the FDA’s January 2025 draft guidance on AI for drugs and biologics is still a draft and the ten joint principles with the EMA are non-binding; the EU GMP Annex 22 is still a draft and the AI Act’s high-risk obligations are deferred to 2027–2028. That does not mean regulation is absent: the FDA has reviewed more than 500 CDER submissions with AI components since 2016, and drug makers in practice already prepare evidence along the lines of these drafts1,2,3,4,5.
Evidence note and a correction. Earlier versions listed whether the FDA draft guidance is final as unconfirmed. This chapter checked further: the FDA’s own page still lists the January 2025 document as a draft and several secondary sources in September–October 2026 still call it a draft, so this draft treats it as not final; the FDA website remains the authority. Most other content comes from law-firm and trade-body readings.

1. Regulatory Documents at a Glance (as of November 2026)

RegulatorDocumentStatusScope and key points
FDA (CDER, CBER and others)Draft guidance “Considerations for the Use of AI to Support Regulatory Decision-Making for Drug and Biological Products” (January 2025, docket FDA-2024-D-4689)Still a draft; the comment period closed 7 April 2025, and sources through September–October 2026 still call it a draft6,1,7Seven-step, risk-based credibility assessment per context of use; excludes AI used solely for drug discovery or for operational uses that do not affect patient safety or data reliability8,9
FDA and EMA jointlyGuiding Principles of Good AI Practice in Drug Development (14 January 2026)Published; non-binding2,10Ten principles covering the full lifecycle (including manufacturing); human-centric design, risk-based approach, adherence to standards, clear context of use and more2,7
EMAReflection paper on the use of AI across the medicinal product lifecycleFinalized (September 2024); explicitly includes drug discovery9The EMA is working toward formal guidelines building on it1
EU / PIC/SEU GMP Annex 22 (artificial intelligence) with revisions to Annex 11 and Chapter 4Draft; consultation 7 July to 7 October 2025; a report of August 2026 still shows no final text11,3Critical uses allow only static, deterministic models and exclude generative AI and LLMs; qualified personnel and a named owner are required12
EU AI ActThe “Digital Omnibus” amendmentsIn force 27 July 2026; Annex III high-risk obligations deferred to 2 December 2027 and those for AI embedded in regulated products to 2 August 2028; general-purpose AI obligations have applied since 2 August 202513,4,14,15Prohibited practices, GPAI and Article 50 transparency obligations were not deferred (see Chapter 16)16
FDA (CDRH) devicesPCCP guidance for AI/ML devices (final, December 2024); draft on the lifecycle of AI-enabled device software functions (January 2025)PCCP final; the lifecycle draft was still a draft as of July 202617,18See Chapters 5 and 17
USPTORevised inventorship guidance for AI-assisted inventions (28 November 2025)In effect; fully rescinds the February 2024 guidance19,20Only natural persons can be inventors; AI is treated as a tool; the Pannu factors apply only among multiple natural persons20
UK PMCPAPosition on AI in pharmaceutical promotionAI may support compliance but does not absolve companies under the ABPI Code; final certification by a nominated signatory cannot rest on AI alone21See Chapter 11

2. Five Cross-Cutting Issues

IssueWhere things standWhat drug makers should do (judgment)
Data governance and ownershipData ownership was a central theme at JPM 2026: who owns clinical and real-world data, how it may train proprietary models, and which rights survive termination22; on the patient side 77% worry about privacy23Write data rights, model and weight ownership and post-termination rights into contracts; tier data by use; see Chapter 10’s diligence list
Validation and explainabilityThe FDA asks for credibility per context of use rather than validating the model itself; draft Annex 22 accepts only static, deterministic models for critical uses24,12Keep an AI inventory and context-of-use register; prefer explainable, freezable models for critical uses; keep audit trails
Biosecurity and dual useOpenAI’s GPT-Rosalind is released through trusted access with undisclosed weights; reports say researchers warn that models trained on biological data could be misused to design dangerous pathogens, which OpenAI addresses through its access model25,26,27; on 17 September 2026 Anthropic launched the Life Sciences Verification Program in beta, giving verified life-science teams a version of the safeguards that is more permissive for biology research28Assess eligibility and controls for specialist models; set internal approval for uses involving pathogens, toxins and the like
Intellectual propertyThe USPTO treats AI as a tool and inventors must be natural persons; commentators note that in drug discovery who “conceived” the compound depends on the model type and collaboration, which current guidance does not fully consider20,29Record human conception early in projects; contract for invention ownership between AI platform and drug maker; expect inventorship to be challenged more in litigation
Accountability and fairnessThe UK PMCPA stresses that responsibility does not shift21; disputes over payer AI denials suggest unguarded automation can substitute group averages for individual judgment30Name an accountable person for each AI use; allocate responsibility between company and vendor in contracts; keep appeal and human review for automated decisions

3. Operating Model: A Minimum Viable AI Governance Framework (Judgment)

ElementPracticeMatching external expectation
AI inventoryRegister every AI model: use, data source, criticality, ownerA prerequisite for Annex 22 readiness31
Context of use and credibility planDefine context of use and risk for each use and draft a credibility assessment planThe FDA draft’s seven-step framework8
Human oversight and accountabilityKeep human review of critical decisions; name qualified people and an ownerDraft Annex 22; the PMCPA position12,21
Vendors and contractsData rights, model ownership, change notification, audit rights, post-termination handlingThe JPM 2026 data theme; USPTO inventorship rules22,20
Board and riskMake data, digital and AI a standing board topic and part of the enterprise risk systemNovartis’s practice32

4. Risks and Open Questions

  • When drafts finalize: the final FDA draft, Annex 22 text and whether the limit on generative AI survives remain uncertain.
  • Conflicts across jurisdictions: the FDA’s credibility per context of use and the EU’s model-type limits for critical uses differ in approach, and multinational firms must satisfy both24.
  • “Deferred” is not “exempt”: the AI Act’s high-risk obligations only moved on the clock; the obligations themselves did not change14.
  • Governance of frontier models: access control, evaluation and misuse prevention for specialist LLMs are still set largely by the model companies themselves.
