Part V · Outlook · Chapter 21

The Next 3–5 Years: Judgments and Actions

Closing the book: ten judgments, a watchlist, actions by type of reader, and the book’s own limits.

Source: none (new chapter)
Evidence grade of this chapter■■□□□□□□□□Primary 19% (5/26) · secondary 81%WeakerPrimary = papers, regulatory documents and company announcements; a company announcement is a primary source but not independent evidence of effect.
Trend signals · as of 2026-11-18
  • The confidence grades on this book’s ten judgments are the author’s subjective ratings, and none has been independently validated; Appendices C and D mark what is still unverified.
  • Observation windows for the next 12 months: JPM 2027, the FDA decision on zasocitinib (Q1 2027 target), the final text of Annex 22 and Phase III readouts.
  • Stop decisions should be written before a pilot starts: the denominator, the finish line and the stop threshold.
Numbers that go stale (dates, funding, valuations) are in Appendix F; changes of judgment between versions are in Appendix E. The above distills and judges this chapter’s content.
In one sentence. Over the next three to five years AI’s effect on pharma is likelier to be fast in places and slow where it matters: document and workflow automation and infrastructure alliances will scale first, while the two things that decide value, whether AI-originated drugs succeed beyond Phase II and where regulators draw the boundary for AI, will become clear only gradually between 2027 and 2030. The judgments here are inferences from the evidence of the previous twenty chapters, not forecasts.
How to use this chapter. Each judgment names the chapters holding its main evidence, this book’s confidence, and what would change it. Confidence is a subjective grade (high, medium, low), not a statistical probability. If the evidence a judgment rests on is marked weak in Appendix C, discount it accordingly.

1. An Overview: AI Adoption Along the Value Chain

Link (chapter)Adoption level (this book’s judgment)Evidence qualityBiggest bottleneck
Discovery (Ch. 7)Clinical-stage assets exist; no drug with both target and molecule from AI is approved, but Mprosevir has conditional approval in China (AI-assisted); rentosertib and GB-0895 are in Phase III and zasocitinib is under FDA reviewMedium: peer review and regulatory documents, but small samplesPhase II success: target biology
Drug development (Ch. 8)Document automation at scale; design recognized by regulators in narrow settingsMedium to low: mostly vendor casesProspective evidence on enrollment and quality
Medical affairs (Ch. 9)Internal uses first; the stricter the compliance, the less the deploymentLow: small surveysValidation and compliance
Business development (Ch. 10)Both directions: AI for BD, and BD for AI assetsLow: little effect evidence, many deal samplesNo evidence of AI’s effect on deal outcomes
Marketing (Ch. 11)Content output surging, review becoming the bottleneckLow: trade commentary and vendorsMLR capacity and regulatory accountability
Sales (Ch. 12)Agents embedded in CRM releasedLow: vendor announcements and casesIndependent effect evidence
Managed markets (Ch. 13)Payers lead, drug-maker tools mostly vendor-ledLow: law-firm readings and vendorsTransparency of payer AI
Manufacturing (Ch. 14)Mostly pilots, value concentrated in deviations and maintenanceMedium to low: claims mostly; regulatory documents primaryThe final Annex 22 rules and data foundations
Trade and distribution (Ch. 15)Demand sensing and shortage warning deployedLow: executive accountsNo point-of-sale signal
Corporate functions (Ch. 16)Easiest to quantify, still earlyLow: targets and secondary estimatesGovernance and compliance clock

2. Ten Judgments

#JudgmentMain evidenceConfidenceWhat would change it
1The firmest AI evidence is still faster candidate generation (scale and speed of search); whether AI changed whether the target holds is not answered by the Phase II figures yet (19.5% to about 55%, defined differently)Ch. 1, 7HighPhase III readouts for rentosertib and GB-0895 (years away); the review outcome for zasocitinib; confirmatory data for Mprosevir1,2,3,4
2Document and workflow automation is the fastest to land and first to scaleCh. 8, 9, 11, 16HighIndependent evaluation showing quality problems, or stricter regulatory demands on AI-drafted documents
3Differentiation is shifting from models to data, compute alliances and the ability to embed in regulated workflowsCh. 3, 4MediumOpen models catching closed ones, narrowing the advantage of data and compute alliances
4Drug makers’ routes will keep diverging into build, buy, acquire and enterprise deployment, with most using several at onceCh. 4MediumA clear winning route emerges, or leadership change leads to strategic retreat (as in the Sanofi case)5

4. Three Cases Worth Following to the End, and One Case of Stopping

Earlier chapters tell readers who is doing what, but a reader making a budget decision needs to know what was really saved under the same conditions, who decides and what result would make someone call a halt. The table splits three cases with public information from a decision-maker’s view: the left is what is public, the right is what is not yet visible, and the last column is this book’s judgment on what result should make the owner stop the project. Public information is mostly company or vendor accounts, so the invisible part is longer than the visible part.

CaseHow it was done before, and who decidesWhat is publicWhat is not visibleWhat result should make the owner stop (this book’s judgment)
A discovery project: Insilico’s rentosertibThe traditional path from target to preclinical candidate often takes years; decisions rest with Insilico and its funders; the company says it took about 18 months from target discovery to preclinical candidate18Phase IIa: 71 people, 12 weeks, +98.4 mL versus −20.3 mL with widely overlapping confidence intervals19; Phase III under way, first dosing in September 20261How much was saved under the same conditions: whether stopped projects are in the denominator; how many other projects on the same platform never reached the clinicThe Phase III primary endpoint (annual FVC decline over 52 weeks) falls short, or safety signals become impossible to ignore; not the fact that discovery was fast
A clinical-report project: Novo Nordisk’s NovoScribeDone before by teams of 40–50 people over weeks (channels say 12, 15 or over 10 weeks); decisions rest with Novo’s content-digitalization and regulatory teams20,21Vendor cases say drafting time fell by over 90%; Novo staff say writing time fell by 90% and that correcting AI mistakes can sometimes take longer than rewriting21,22Total time from first draft to signed final including rework and regulator queries; independent quality evaluation; where the saved labor wentTotal time to final sign-off does not fall, or regulator queries and rework rise, or a quality audit finds an error rate above the human baseline
A distribution project: Cencora’s demand sensingWholesalers see orders, not patients’ real demand at the counter; decisions rest with Cencora’s supply-chain and data teams23,24Its executive cites about a 10% forecasting improvement; the US still had 227 active drug shortages in Q2 2026, a third consecutive quarterly rise24,23Whether the forecasting gain translates into patients getting the drug: whether root causes of shortages (raw materials, capacity, pricing) are within AI’s reach; the definition and baseline of the 10%Forecasts get more accurate but shortages do not fall, showing the bottleneck is not forecasting; funds should then move to data sharing or capacity rather than more forecasting models

A Case of Stopping

I found no case in which a drug maker publicly explained deciding not to renew after seeing attractive pilot data, which is itself a signal: what gets published is mostly renewal and expansion, and few explain why they stopped. The two closest cases I could find, one from a developer and one from a partnership ending:

  • Recursion ended REC-994 and REC-2282 and moved REC-3964 toward out-licensing in May 2025. REC-994 had Phase II data from 62 people in cerebral cavernous malformation and had shown early positive signals; the company said the accumulated data did not justify continued investment and that the totality of results supported ending development. This is a developer calling a halt on all the data after early signals25,18.
  • The Bayer–Exscientia collaboration ended by mutual agreement after a drug-discovery milestone. Signed in January 2020 and worth €240 million including upfront and undisclosed milestones; Exscientia kept the option to develop one of the two targets; this is a company-disclosed end after a milestone, not a drug maker explaining why it did not renew. The abstract found does not show the year of the ending26.

Implication for readers (this book’s judgment): writing down what result would make me stop should happen before the pilot, not after. A practical way is to fix three things in the contract or the project charter in advance: the denominator (every project started, including those stopped), the finish line (from first draft to final sign-off) and the stop threshold. Without these, attractive pilot data rarely turn into a decision to continue or stop.

5. Recommended Actions (This Book’s Judgment)

ReaderRecommendations
Drug makers’ strategy and R&D leaders
  • Tier AI projects into data-rich, objective-endpoint areas and complex-mechanism, slow-endpoint areas, and scale validation first in the former (Chapter 18).
  • For discovery collaborations, tie milestones to clinical readouts rather than platform access (Chapters 7 and 10).
  • Build a replicable governance template on document and workflow uses before extending to uses that affect patient safety (Chapters 8, 14 and 20).
  • Keep an AI inventory and context-of-use register as a common base for FDA and EU expectations (Chapter 20).
Business development and investors
  • Separate platform-first from pipeline-first AI companies, and build a diligence list centered on data rights for the former (Chapter 10).
  • For claimed performance, ask for prospective validation rather than benchmarks alone (Chapters 3 and 7).
  • Write the definition of an AI-originated asset into terms to avoid disputes over counting (Chapters 1 and 18).
Device makers
  • Break AI features into applications that can be filed and change-controlled separately, on a unified data platform (Chapters 5 and 17).
  • From the start, design contracts for the rights, privacy and secondary use of surgical and device data (Chapter 17).
  • Track the EU AI Act’s 2028 date for embedded products and the FDA’s lifecycle expectations for AI device software (Chapters 16 and 20).
Consultancies and service providers
  • Move from selling advice to verifiable results: price on milestones and measurable output (Chapter 6).
  • Make data rights, model ownership and post-termination handling explicit in contracts (Chapters 6, 10 and 20).
  • Be restrained with your own figures: separate the definitions of “AI revenue” and avoid direct addition against peers (Chapter 6).
Regulatory and compliance teams
  • Treat deferral as a preparation window rather than exemption, and use the time to close the gap (Chapters 16 and 20).
  • Name an accountable person for each AI use and keep audit trails and human review (Chapters 11, 14 and 20).
  • Build a comparison table of cross-jurisdiction expectations: the FDA’s credibility per context of use versus the EU’s model-type limits for critical uses (Chapters 14 and 20).

6. The Book’s Limits and the Next Version

  • Evidence leans secondary: many figures come from secondary compilations and company claims, all listed in Appendix C; check that list first before external use.
  • Coverage gaps: healthcare models from Google and DeepMind and Microsoft, and the 2026 moves of companies such as Recursion, Xaira, Generate and Chai, were added in Chapters 3, 6 and 7 but only in outline; Japanese and Chinese drug and device makers (Chapters 4 and 5) and cardiovascular, ophthalmology, vaccines and cell and gene therapy (Chapter 18) were also added in outline; still not covered are details of genomic models such as the Evo series, healthcare models from Meta, Amazon and IBM, device makers such as Terumo, CROs and CDMOs, and areas such as dermatology, gastroenterology, nephrology and hematology.
  • Mostly the US and EU: regulation and practice in China, Japan, India and other markets are not covered.
  • Timing: data are current to 18 November 2026 and the field changes fast; the source links at the end of each chapter can be used for updates.
  • Disclosure: this book was drafted with Claude, developed by Anthropic; content about Anthropic and Claude relies only on public material and is held to the same standard as other vendors, so please verify independently.
